The card's 155 at 250 was nine high for the same reason.** The twelve lines currently on the hardboard went on on the Thursday of week seventy-nine and are twenty-five days old at 241, twenty-nine at 242, thirty-two at 243, thirty-four at 244, thirty-five at 245, thirty-six at 246, thirty-nine at 247 and forty-nine at 248, and are sixty-two days old at 250; the thirteenth line was written on the Thursday of week eighty-six and is eight days old at 249 and thirteen at 250. **The heating went off at seven in the morning on the Monday of week eighty-three and was off for a fortnight, was not restored when the water went down on the Friday of week eighty-four, and is off at the close of the volume: ten days at 245, eleven at 246, fourteen at 247, twenty-four at 248, thirty-two at 249 and thirty-seven at 250.** The Exchange's last open sitting before this movement was exactly eight weeks before Chapter 244 and exactly four weeks before Chapter 250. The pair of shoes were carried out of that room on the Friday of week eighty-four. The question on the back of the docket is about sixteen weeks old at Chapter 249. **The condition is quoted in NONE of the ten chapters and paraphrased in NONE of them. The standing problem is NOT SAID in any of the ten and is referred to in ten load books. The relay is not staged, the woman who carried it is not asked again and is not named, and the word *carrier* does not appear in any of the ten files. There is no panel and no `* * *` marker anywhere in this batch, the volume's budget being one and Chapter 209 having spent it. The name Talia Venn appears in Chapters 249 and 250 and in no other of the ten, and the woman of twenty-four of Chapters 245, 246, 249 and 250 is the same woman and the prose does not say so until the Friday of week eighty-seven, when she gives her name at the door.**

## What Chapters 241–250 added to the record, in the order it is established

1. **Ch 241, the Monday of week 83 — the heat goes off, and the eight words over the door are untrue from the first hour of the day.** Nine on the docket and nine in date and a stair fire door in Nine Bridges whose closer he reset for the fourth time in about a fortnight and did not attend, having attended three times and confirmed each time that the frame is as he has described it. **The heating was off at seven in the morning and nobody had paid for it and nobody was going to.** A cook of twenty-seven set her kettle going at its fixed point in the day, said once that a kettle is a kettle and the room is not warm, and gave two numbers off a scrap she keeps for a milk order — eleven cups on the Friday, seven on the Monday — with the reading that the movement in the number is a movement in the number of people who are cold. Three of the four people who came for the temperature did not attend on the Monday and none has attended since, and no person in that room stated that aloud and there is no instrument there by which it could be established. **A man of thirty-four who works nights asked for about four seconds: that somebody telephone him on the morning the heat goes off so that he need not be the person who walks in and finds out. A cook of twenty-seven, at a sink, offered a standing Tuesday at about ten, refused thanks in advance, and said she had known for about three weeks that he could not stop her.** The man of twenty-two declined, in public, to rub off the upper line on the board or stand near it, and asked for that to be recorded and said there is nowhere to record it, and moved about four feet. A man of about sixty stood in the doorway, said he had come for the temperature, observed there was none, sat for about an hour, read both lines on the board and offered no comment on either.
2. **Ch 242, the Friday of week 83 — a sump pump that is not the fault, and a list nobody asked for.** Ten on the docket and nine in date. A sump pump in a basement in a district whose name is not in this book ran nine hours on a night with nothing heavy enough to call for it, has a float switch set by somebody who is not named here and was never asked, and was reported by two people inside a week, both right and neither the fault; he attended in nine inches of standing water and wrote that the fault is not the pump and that the water is about nine inches deep against a wall with no opening on this side, and did not write where he thought it came from. A district man of thirty-eight called it the first entry in about five months to have stated what the fault was not and to have declined to state what it was. **A man of fifty-one with a tool bag said he has put about nine hundred door numbers on buildings in this city in about nine years, that a number on a door finds a building and not a person, that in about four years somebody will put a number on that door that finds a person, and that he has wanted it written for about four years and has never once wanted it written more than he wanted it not to be true. A cook of twenty-seven said one word and would not say which word.** **A man of thirty-eight in a district jacket said three districts take water off that row, two off a river about nine miles off and one off a hill, that he will not give a date having been wrong about that twice, that the only list worth anything in a fortnight's time is a list of who is in which building, that he could produce one in about a day, that he is not asking, and that not knowing who is in a room is the only thing he has got.** A first-year of nineteen said the list could be written in a day and would run to about four hundred names of which about half could not give a district, that the first line on that wall forbids it, and that no version of the man in the doorway could be made to forget four hundred names. Nobody thanked him. A man came in out of the rain, would not say what he had come out of, was given tea and a chair, and left into it.
3. **Ch 243, the Monday of week 84 — the ask, the fifth set of nine words, and the sixth hold on.** Eleven on the docket and eleven in date; the same basement pump reported a second time inside four days; standing water nineteen inches at the second attendance. **A person of thirty-four who buys training for a company asked, in front of about nine people, for a name, on behalf of an internal request for a flood affecting three districts in about four days of which she does not know the date and has declined to press for, having asked in three buildings and been told in each that no such person exists, which she reported is correct in the way a locked door is correct.** He gave the fifth set of nine words in this case after the disclaimer — *one name for a whole city is a post* — and a cook of twenty-seven counted it aloud as five words and identified it as the ninth of a sentence she has heard him begin, and was not the person he was talking to. **Then about four minutes uninterrupted: a key is not a post because the giver can take it back and the holder can be relieved; his own key cannot be taken back because a company is not a person and that has not been fixed in two attempts here; a name is a key left out on a counter; about four hundred people in this city have said *I was never in that room* this year and about four hundred could not take it back afterwards, and he is one of them; a person who answers a line cannot be relieved of answering it and he would have to say *this is not my district* about nine hundred times before it stopped being true.** He invited four people to find a hole and said he would rather it happened in that room than on a service road; nobody answered and nobody thanked him. **What he offered instead: the number on the plate, not his and not a name, answered by whoever is in the room, who will not know the caller and will not ask for one.** **A cook of twenty-seven named the consequence: on a Tuesday at two in the morning the person who picks that up is whoever is standing there, which on that hour is her, and she is a line cook who has never run anything, and she refused money a week earlier and said the heat was what the refusing costs, and this is a larger version of the same and she has a cloth and a kettle and not nine words, and she asked for it to be written down by somebody other than her, and it was not.** At about twenty past seven the plate rang, a letting clerk of thirty-nine gave her sentence about a vacant frontage and said *hold on* for the sixth time in about twelve weeks and put the call through, and a wet man with a dog was given the address of a hall with a hole in the roof and told the front of it was dry. Nobody thanked her and she is not thanked on any of the five.
4. **Ch 244, the Wednesday of week 84 — the tenth sitting, the book shut, and a plate on a door.** Ten sittings, nine gaps of four weeks, the tally of sittings at which nothing has been added is nine. **The holder stated for the first time in nineteen years that the room is above the line.** The rule was given whole and unimproved — a book of what a caller said cannot be written by the people who did not call — given ten times at ten sittings, asked for nine times in nineteen years, and given unasked on two occasions, the ninth and the tenth; a thank-you was refused on the ground that the rule should be thanked to whoever asked for it. **The telephone count moved from thirty-seven to forty-one, four in four weeks, the highest four-week movement since the count began, announced unasked; it has now been announced fifteen times, ten of which correspond to the ten Wednesdays this book holds and five of which predate it on a separate sheet never shown to anybody, a fact never before stated in that room; the subtraction was done on the fingers twice and refused for the future.** The tenth reason the book did not open, and the first about a door rather than a person: on the Friday a man telephoned and asked whether a room behind a door carrying a plate with a number and no name on it was in that book, having been put through by a clerk who said hold on; he gave a street and not a room's name; she cannot answer without opening the book on a table in front of nine people and will not do that to find out whether a man in a wet street is on a page; she has had the reason since about eleven o'clock on the Friday, will not ring him, and will not thank anybody in that room who does. **A man of about thirty-one asked in advance whether she would be the person a flood telephones instead of a room; her answer was fifteen words, counted as fifteen and as fourteen in the same room, and she said it was the longest sentence she has spoken there in about four years and declined to explain the difference and called it a book.** The ninth chair was reported moved again by a young man of twenty-one who is keeping a count of how often she has said that a chair is a chair; she did not confirm the count. Her last sentence, written in a private notebook that is not the book: the book is ahead of the telephone for the first time, it is not a coincidence, it does not fill while it is shut, and the day it is opened is the day the room begins to fill.
5. **Ch 245, the Thursday of week 84 — four pieces, no owner, and the first public refusal made on purpose.** Water over the pavement at the low end of three districts, rising at about an inch an hour on the figure of a man of fifty-one with a tool bag and nobody else's. **Four people each took one piece, agreed in about eleven minutes, and no person took all of it and no line had an owner: a man of twenty-nine who works on lifts, about four hundred buildings a person cannot get out of and about nine hundred that they can, able to get into the four hundred and not to get anybody out of them, and declining in the same four sentences to sit on a landing being fine at it where a person might see him being fine at it; a cook of twenty-seven, a kettle, a gas ring that came with the unit, about nine hundred cups, a table and a chair, about four hundred cups in about two days, and hands if the cups run out, with the finding restated by her and said back in nine words by a first-year of nineteen that this is the piece and it is all of her piece; a first-year of nineteen, the door and the plate, who will answer, will not ask who is calling, will not write anything down, and who distinguished answering a telephone from being a key-holder and a key from a telephone and who can be asked to stop; and a woman of twenty-four, a nurse, with a clipboard nobody asked her to bring, about nine stairs from a bed into a dry hall and not a building, not a street, not a night, a list of about nine people spoken to this year, one man in a first-floor flat in that row who will not open his door to a stranger, and the statement that what a flood will actually want is a person and not a room and that this is a rota and not a moral question.** The man of twenty-two declined a piece, gave the reason twice in public, and said unprompted that he has been waiting about two days for somebody to ask him to run one and that nobody did. **A first-year of nineteen telephoned the man of thirty-four who works nights at about eight, having told him that a cook had offered him a standing Tuesday, and said on return that she had taken the offer and was not sorry. He said he wanted about four seconds, and then said unprompted that a telephone call was not what he wanted, that he wanted a witness, that company and a witness are different things, and that he has got neither and has instead got a first-year of nineteen on a service road in the rain, which is worse. A cook of twenty-seven said one word that was not agreement, and the word was late. The first refusal, at about half past eight, on purpose, before about nine people: the man of twenty-nine put his employer's folded Friday list face up on the counter and refused to read a single name off it, take it off his employer's book, photograph it, or enter any person from it on any list that room makes, gave the grounds including that a system in about nine places chooses job numbers, that about eleven people a week are on that page for nothing they have done, that his own name is on it and the man with the docket is on it and the woman who answers a telephone in another building is not and he has checked, and said he was saying no on purpose, at night, in front of witnesses, when saying yes would have been easier and would have worked.**
6. **Ch 246, the Friday of week 84 — the evacuation, the second public refusal, the shoes, and four hundred and eleven cups.** Eleven on the docket and ten in date; the fire door in Nine Bridges was attended for the first time since the water went down and could not be reached and the reason was recorded, and a district man of thirty-eight read that entry and said nothing, which is the first silence in about five months. **A man of fifty-eight with a bad hip came in having come down four floors in the dark with a pair of shoes in a plastic bag and asked that they be given to somebody in that room with wet feet and no shoes. A cook of twenty-seven did not touch the bag and said the sentence she gave about a pair of shoes about five weeks ago: the room can only return it, not transfer it. He took the bag, declined to leave it, was offered no help, and returned at about six to say that a man whose size it was now has them and that he is not going to say where, and that a first-year of nineteen said so first. The bag did not stay on that counter.** **The second refusal, at about eleven in the morning, unasked and before anybody had worked out that she was the obvious candidate: a woman of twenty-four would not be the person the plate's telephone reaches, on the ground that in about four years somebody in an office will use her as a reason and the man in the first-floor flat will not open his door either; and she named the cost herself, which is about four minutes during which nobody could have telephoned her, which she will carry, will not hand to anybody, and will not check. A cook of twenty-seven told her she had just cost that room its evening. She answered that the four minutes is a fact and not a price, a price being something somebody else pays, and that she had paid it and was calling it a price so that she would be able to tell afterwards whether she had been right.** A man of thirty-four who works nights accepted a night of door-opening work on a sheet of about nine addresses from a person he does not know, for the first time in about nine years, and reported it to that room and not to his employer. **Nobody died, said once and true; a woman of about thirty-four with a district jacket said afterwards that in about nine years of that job she has never been able to say that sentence and mean it, that on this occasion she could and could not enjoy it, and that she has thought about the two not fitting together every day since.** The man of twenty-two ran no piece, was asked twice for a decision, and gave the same seven words both times — ask whoever is running that piece — and both were obeyed, by a woman of twenty-eight with two children and by a man of about sixty with a dog. **The tally of cups since the Wednesday was four hundred and eleven, refused as a headcount and not to be coyed about, on the ground that it is the best thing that room has ever done, said once and not to be said again.**
7. **Ch 247, the Monday of week 85 — the removal, in nine words, and then a kettle.** Ten on the docket and ten in date. **The supply was due back that morning and was not restored: a landlord's message of the Tuesday had said it would be when the water went down, the water went down on the Friday, and nothing came on. A man of thirty-eight in a district jacket said there was a form for it and nobody in that room wanted a form. A cook of twenty-seven said she had asked three times, had been told three times that the supply is restored when the water goes down, and is not asking a fourth.** **A woman of about thirty-four with a district jacket and a clipboard telephoned a man of fifty-one named Amiss, who has stood on a page of about forty-one people since the Tuesday of week thirty-eight and is the one of that page that was never rung, and established that he left his flat at about seven on the Friday with a bag and a neighbour's dog, reached a sister's in another district, was not hurt, is fifty-one with a bad chest, and did not answer a door for two days; and that she could have rung him on the Friday and did not, because the number in front of her was a man's list of buildings and not a page, and by the Tuesday it was not useful; and that the page is the most useful object in this city that week and the one thing that cannot be used; and that there is no job in this city whose work it is to ring people; and that about four rooms full of people this year could have held that job and none of them did. Nothing was corrected, closed or referred onward and no letter was written.** The man of twenty-two said he had had a whole weekend on about seven words and could make nothing of it. **A first-year of nineteen came round the chairs, stood in front of him, and said in about nine seconds: nobody should have to find you to stop me. He refused, when somebody else asked, to have her say it again; said he had nothing; said this is the first time in about two volumes that a person has said the thing he has been saying at length; and asked for about nine seconds and then for everybody to stop looking at him.** She then gave her own half, unprompted: he is not the central owner of anything, he is not on the four pieces, he is not one of the four who can be stopped because he is not running one, and that is the entire reason he has to go; a cup of tea has a person in front of it and that person can be asked to stop; and the two of them are the two ends of a sentence they both wrote and neither can ask to stop. He asked for the practical part and received it: seven words, and a true sentence about who is running a piece, which about nine people have now said and which has never been printed. She did not apologise and went to the sink. **A cook of twenty-seven put a cup of tea in front of the woman of about thirty in the chair nearest the door and said nothing at all, and nobody has said anything to that woman in a fortnight, and an apology would have been a question.** A cook of twenty-seven said the whole of what she had to say about the heating in nine words and then, asked whether she wanted it written down, that she wanted the other one written down and nobody ever does; nobody wrote either down and she has now said the same thing three times in this volume.
8. **Ch 248, the Thursday of week 86 — nine people with a list, no minutes, a thirteenth line, a thumb, and a public lesson he was not in.** Twenty-four days into the unheated fortnight. The room had refused three corridor boards in about nine seconds each. **Two other rooms had asked to do what this room does, in a hall with a hatch in it and on a Tuesday, and a woman of about thirty-four with a district jacket delivered the request and gave the rest: that neither of them can be taught by anybody in this case and that the only thing on offer is the answer.** Nine people sat with a list of what the room would not do. **A man of about fifty-two in a plain coat proposed minutes and was refused in about nine seconds — nothing is decided in that room, nine things are said, about nine objections are taken, at four o'clock everybody stands up, and no record of any of it will exist — and was told that in advance, deliberately, so that the absence of a record could not afterwards be attributed to surprise. He said that was fair and did not ask again.** A man of twenty-nine named what he could not, which is about eleven buildings, on the ground that a list of buildings is a list of people and that he has decided the page in a ring binder is the most he will be on. A cook of twenty-seven gave the numbers of her piece to the other room and kept the four hundred and eleven, and said the other room will count its own, wrongly, in the correct order of wrong, which she prefers to handing over. **The thirteenth line was written by a first-year of nineteen at about three in the afternoon, in about nine seconds, in her own hand, after announcing the cost: that a person may say they were never here and the room will not argue. Thirteen words. Her cost, unasked: that it is the first thing she has written there that concerns a person merely being in it, that it is on a surface anybody may photograph, that she has been on about eleven hundred leaflets, and that she wrote it without asking the person it is mostly about and has done that once before at a market and the two are the same size.** **A woman of about thirty said one sentence, standing, to about nine people: you did not ask me. A first-year of nineteen said no. She said that was the correct answer and that she was going to say it anyway, because in about four years there will be a line on that wall about a person who sits in that chair and it will be a good line and she will not have been asked. Nobody apologised to her and a cook of twenty-seven said from a sink that an apology would have been a second question. Asked once whether she wanted the line kept, she gave one answer once: that they are not allowed to keep asking, that this is not a refusal, and that this is the line.** At about half past three the cook of twenty-seven took her own thumb to the board over the door and in about four seconds removed the eight words she had written herself, which had been untrue for twenty-four days, and left the four in a sign-writer's hand and replaced nothing, and asked that it be recorded as taking about four seconds and not about a term, and said of the sign-writer's four that the man was about sixty, was paid four pounds, has not been thanked, is not a character in that room, and that the four words are true. **A public lesson was given on the Wednesday, about forty minutes to about nine people, by the man of twenty-nine and the cook of twenty-seven, nothing shown working, four of the nine asked to do it badly on purpose, nothing written down, and the man of twenty-two was on a job in another district, telephoned once to ask whether it was happening, told it was, asked whether it had worked, told it had not finished, and did not telephone again; at about nine he was told in the doorway four things in order, and that he is not permitted to keep asking.**
9. **Ch 249, the Friday of week 87 — one thumb, four words in chalk, nine keys, and about nine seconds.** Ten on the docket and nine in date; the fire door in Nine Bridges was not attended and the non-attendance was recorded, and a district man of thirty-eight said nothing, the first silence in about five months. **A letting clerk of thirty-nine with a ring file gave the sentence about a vacant frontage for the sixth time, and volunteered unasked that the landlord has not asked about that unit once since the water, that there is nothing in that file about the people in that room, and that if the reason the first year is free is ever needed it will not be in that file and never has been, and that she has said so for about sixteen weeks and will not be asked about it by anybody in this city.** **A woman of about sixty-eight, in four attendances in three weeks and silent in all of the other three, removed the four words in a sign-writer's hand with her own thumb in about four seconds at about a quarter past four, in front of about nine people, without apologising and without being asked to; a grey ghost of the first word remains and cannot be got off that board; and she wrote four words in chalk in her own hand: anybody wet, come in.** **Her stated cost, given before she was asked: that she is sixty-eight, that she has been in about four rooms in about nine years where a sign on a door said what a person was allowed to do, that in three of them she could not take the sign down and in the fourth she could and did not, that this is the whole of her experience and she has not said it to anybody, and that she has now become a person who can take a sign down, and that in about four years somebody will stand in that doorway and ask her to put a different one up and she will be the one who knows where the chalk is; and that the four words are true about eleven days a year and false the other three hundred and fifty-four, that a sign does not have to be true all year, and that it has to be true on the day somebody is standing in the rain with nothing. A cook of twenty-seven said nine words about it, including that hers were eight and hers was wrong, and would not say so for her.** **A woman of twenty-four gave her name at the door as Talia Venn, the first time in that room, and about four people heard it and the man of twenty-two did not turn round. She said the thing is still able to be taken over, to a room in which the person most likely to be taken over by is standing with his back to her, having said first that she holds no position in it and has not asked for one; that it will not be taken by a villain, that there is no villain in it, that there is a company, a district and a woman with a clipboard and all three are right, and that what takes a thing of this kind is a Tuesday, a tired person and a version of it that is obviously better for about nine people. And that it cannot yet cross a district line, not because of a rule, because there is no rule in this city about a room, and not because of money, since a room in a hall with a hatch in it has been doing it since the flood with a cook's numbers and a man's list of buildings. Asked what is stopping it: a man of twenty-nine who can get into four hundred buildings, a cook who can put four hundred and eleven cups into wet hands, a first-year who can be asked to stop, and no fourth thing, and the fourth thing is nine keys, and there are two.** He turned round and said the count himself for the first time in the volume, and added unprompted that the other seven do not exist. **He asked the docket question aloud of a woman of thirty-one with a diary, who came in her own coat and brought nothing: she can be asked, in about nine seconds, it has been true since the Thursday it was handed over and will be true on a day he has not chosen, she would have it without thinking about it, she would come in her own coat and bring nothing, and she would mind about nine seconds' worth and then she would be in a coat. A first-year of nineteen asked whether any of that could be written down and was refused in about four seconds, on the ground that a written answer would be a rule about who may be asked, and a rule about who may be asked is a rule about who is findable, and there is no such rule in that room and she will not help write one on a wet Tuesday for a reason that will feel like tidiness in about four years. Nine words were identified in that sentence and were not counted. Nothing was written down and the binder holds seven things and did not gain an eighth.**
10. **Ch 250, the Wednesday of week 88 — the close: one line, forty-six lines, a binder off a shelf, and four minutes in a corridor.** The eleventh sitting, four weeks to the day after the tenth. **The book opened for about eleven seconds and one line went in and it is the forty-sixth. Nobody was described, nobody was counted, and the number of words was refused and not asked for. The condition for writing anything in that book is that the caller said it to her face, which she has never once said aloud in nineteen years and said today, and the first line of that kind took four weeks because she asked the caller whether she was sure, and that was the act and not the hesitation.** The count moved from forty-one to forty-four, three in four weeks; the count has now been announced sixteen times, eleven corresponding to the eleven Wednesdays this book holds and five predating it on a separate sheet never shown; and at forty-four calls and forty-six lines the book stands two lines ahead of the telephone and was one ahead four weeks ago, the gap has not closed, and she does not know what makes a gap and will not look in front of nine people. **She named her own delay: the book was shut for the first eleven minutes of the sitting and about four people watched her not open it, and the question put to her by a man on the Friday about whether a room behind a door with a plate and no name is in that book is true and is also a way of not doing anything.** A young man of twenty-one reported the ninth chair moved again; she said a chair is a chair for the fourth time; he said he keeps a count of how often she has said it and has never been asked to stop, and was told not to. **She said she had said a thing in that room in the week of seventy-two about a book that grows and what it does to the person who has to hold it, that she has not repeated it, that she is not going to repeat it today when it would have been about nine times easier, and that she would like about four people to notice and is not going to explain why because explaining it is a minute.** Asked whether the book has to be carried anywhere: it does. Asked whether there is a room in this city it could be carried to: about nine, she has been to none, she will not be carried anywhere by anybody who has not asked her first, and if that happens the book is a thing being moved, and she has watched that happen about four times in her time, without noticing while it was happening and without anybody else noticing either. **At about half past four in the room off the service road, a man of about thirty-one who had not been there before asked for a book of what people had said, and was told it is a ring binder with seven things in it, not a book, not a system, not emptiable, that no person who wrote in it can be found in it, and that he may take it off the shelf and read it and put it back, and that if he is the person who wrote one of the seven he may take that page out and nobody will stop him and nobody will be told. Asked who says he is allowed to read it: nobody, come and read it. Asked who is not allowed to know that he read it: that it is a different question, that nobody in that room has an answer, that about nine people have been asked it in the last fortnight, and that there is no paper anywhere in this city that says, and that this was decided in about nine days and nobody in the city agreed to it. He read for about nine minutes standing up, wrote nothing, asked nobody anything, put it back, and said one sentence on his way out that about four people heard and nobody wrote down.** At about half past five, in a corridor, for about four minutes, he said he had been asked to carry the binder somewhere by a woman with a diary who has not been carried anywhere in nineteen years and who has now been asked by a stranger, that he does not think it should go, and that he has thought about it for about an hour; he was told he had not asked her, that the reason had not been given, and that he will not be the reason anybody asks her again; and she said that was the first correct thing he had said about it in a fortnight, that she was not going to do anything with it, and that if he tells anybody she will deny it and he will not be able to prove it, and that she has wanted to be able to do that for about a year and it has not come up. **No scene, no declaration and no kiss; neither said a word about it afterwards to anybody; neither has said it again.**

## What Movement V of Volume 05 did not do, and what it leaves for the volume close and for Volume 06

**It did not teach, demonstrate, assess or assist anything; nobody was shown anything by him; the condition is quoted in none of the ten chapters and paraphrased in none of them; and a room off a service road is not a practice in that department and the reason is not that he is the exception. The only thing that came near it is the public lesson, and he was on a job in another district for the whole of it, asked one question by telephone and did not telephone again.** It did not stage the relay, ask the woman who carried it, or name her, and the word *carrier* does not appear in any of the ten files. It put no panel and no `* * *` marker, the volume's budget being one and Chapter 209 having spent it. **It did not put a name over a door and it did not create any title, office, post, committee, minute, notice, report, form, registration, constitution, list or register; the only object this movement added to the manuscript is the thirteenth line on a hardboard that was already on two nails.** It let nothing be settled by any of them: the four pieces were agreed in about eleven minutes in a room and minuted nowhere, the two refusals were made out loud in front of witnesses, the opt-out is a sentence somebody wrote by hand, and the list of what the room will not do was read and not copied. **It did not open the Exchange book anywhere but Chapters 244 and 250, the book was at forty-five lines for the first nine chapters of the movement, went to forty-six at the eleventh, and Bea Nunn did not open it on a Friday and no Friday in this batch is an Exchange day. It did not sign anything, and no person was described in that book and no person was counted.** It did not resolve the standing problem, invented no rule about who may be asked, attached no number to one, and **did not state it in any of the ten chapters; it declined it once, in Chapter 249, in the shape of an answer to a practical question about whether a key can be written down.** It did not fix the first key, create a mechanism, a holder or a list, and it did not answer the docket question in writing. It did not let the master leave the fourth pocket of the tool roll and nobody decided anything about its fifth line. It did not name the licensor, name the person who inserted the page, name the clause behind the public version, put Iona or Sorn in any file, or let any message arrive from anybody. It did not put Rafi Pell in a room, name Steve Akers, find the woman of thirty-four with a hip, resolve the hundred and forty-one pounds, empty the box in pencil, or cross a city line. **It did not resolve the fortnight of heating and it did not resolve the offer's price, and both are unpaid at the close of the volume. It did not resolve the relationship, and the volume's outline reserves that for Volume 15 and says so.** It did not end the standing debt of a page of about forty-one people, and it did not make anybody's telephone call unnecessary, and it did not say who would telephone it next time.

---
