## What Chapters 211–220 added to the record, in the order it is established

1. **Ch 211, the Monday of week 71 — the second key, and the test that does not hold.** A woman of thirty-one with a key and a diary and six years of running an exchange room on a first floor in Bower Street, who has been told twice this term that she has no standing, put a cut key on a counter with a sheet of typing paper on which one reason is written in her own hand: you can ring her, and she will come and take it out of your hand inside a day. **A first-year of nineteen turned the reason into one question and wrote it on the back of a docket: *who can be asked to take this key back*. Four people answered it in about nine seconds — ring him at work and he comes; ring him at work and he comes; ring her and she comes and it is a day — and the answer about the first key is *nobody*.** The finding, in his own order and not an improvement on anybody's: a person holding a key is not a post until in about four years a person with a key is a post, and the whole difference is whether the people who handed it over can take it back, and the people who handed over the first one are a company with a ring file, and a company is not a person and cannot be asked to take anything back, and **a key nobody can take back is a key whose holder can never be relieved, so the first key is not the one that gives the room a door — the first key is the one that makes him a holder, and it made him a holder the day it came off that counter.** Nobody invented a holder, no lock was fitted, no list was written, the first key did not go back in an envelope, and the first-year said the hard version first: the best key in that room is the one nobody can take off you, and the one anybody can take off is a key that is only as safe as a telephone. The giver named her own cost unasked: her name on a sheet of typing paper in a room with a door and a bell-push on it, after four years of being careful, and she is going to be findable about one thing and about nothing else. **A woman of about thirty was in the room for the fifth time, and a first-year said the reason out loud, and the woman gave it back and asked to be left in the chair and not be the thing a person is careful about.**
2. **Ch 212, the Tuesday of week 71 — the first composite thing anybody has done in that room.** Four people, about twenty-two minutes, timed on a first-year's wrist and then rubbed at with a thumb for about a minute. Five beats: the kettle and the strip first, at the plug socket that works; nine chairs out of the third room in two trips because one person cannot carry nine, curved with the two ends turned in and then moved about four inches each after standing back; counter, sounder and the hardboard turned on its two nails; the outside of the shutter, the door and the bell-push; and the kettle last, because a kettle on early means somebody is standing about waiting and the room is being got ready instead of being ready. **The four hands are a cook of twenty-seven on her own time from a kitchen that does not open until the afternoon, a man of twenty-nine who works on lifts who came on a Tuesday for the first time, a woman of thirty-one with a key and a diary, and a printer of fifty-eight with a shop four feet wide.** At about eighteen minutes the printer began a sentence about where something went and three other people finished it, and the arrangement is as old as the room, and four people in a room heard their own arrangement nine seconds after the man of twenty-two did and he said nothing. **He stood at the back with a docket for twenty-two minutes, did not take part, was not asked and did not offer, and the reason he did not comment was said out loud by the cook with her hands in a sink and not by him: it is not modesty, it is that she has worked behind a man at the front of a kitchen for nine years and knows what it costs, and a room that waits for the man at the front is a room with a man at the front of it. He agreed in about four seconds, said he was not going to improve on it, and was sent out to stand on a service road for ten minutes.** The four of them then wrote one page in four hands in about half an hour, with two words argued over for about nine minutes, **and a first-year of nineteen held the page flat and wrote none of it and put the pen down beside it rather than in her hand, because if she writes any of it then it is her words about their thing and not their words about their thing.** She then argued for about four minutes that the page ought to say the room was not empty, was told that the page she is holding flat is the line that says the room was not empty, and gave the reason: a page which says who was standing in a room is a page which describes a room, and she has been losing that argument to two other people for three weeks and has now lost it to a page.
3. **Ch 213, the Wednesday of week 71 — nine chairs, a sheet, and a kettle.** A woman of thirty-eight who has folded sheets for nineteen years in four places about eleven miles apart offered at about twenty past one to show anybody who walked in how she folds a sheet corner to corner in three, flat, in about eleven seconds, with a stated time limit of about four minutes and a bus after it, and got the bus. **About eleven people came through the door in about an hour and nine were in the room at once, which is the number of chairs and was an accident, and four of the nine were asked before they came to do it badly on purpose, and nobody in the room knew which four, and the person offering the thing did not know which four and never knew and never would have, and that is the only review of the demonstration she is going to get.** The reason for the arrangement was given out loud in about nine words and is the only sentence in this movement nobody argued with: a demonstration in this city is a demonstration because somebody in it can tell whether you did it right, and the test is not the fold. One offer, four demonstrations, nine attempts, zero correct, about four results that were not cloth any more, one triangle produced by a young man who had come in to be warm, which the woman said was a thing. **Nothing in that room was corrected for half an hour, and the two people present who could have corrected something did not, and a cook of twenty-seven named the cost afterwards at a sink: about four wrong folds inside the first four minutes, one of which was going to end up as a sheet somebody's child had slept under, nine minutes of silence, the longest nine minutes she has spent in that room, and she is not going to be told it was the right thing again.** A man of twenty-two stood by the back wall for the whole half hour with a docket, was asked nothing by anybody, did not go round the room, and said nothing about whether any of the nine was right. **The thing that worked was the kettle, on at about half past two at a fixed point in that room's day that does not depend on anybody's turn, and about eight of nine people seated by about ten past three, and two mugs short, and somebody has mugs at home and nobody was moved. A first-year of nineteen asked to record it and was refused in front of about nine people, and the refusal is the finding: a technique in that room is a thing whose wrongness hurts a person, which is her own definition from a list of four words in a binder in the second week of this volume, and if the kettle is recorded then in about four years there is a person somewhere not doing it right, and that person is a person who made somebody a cup of tea, and there is no card anybody can put in front of her saying that she failed.** She said it has happened to her about nine times with heat and twice to a woman she worked with, once about a pot and not a knife, and the pot is on a paper in an office she has never been able to get back. The binder stayed shut. The four came to the door at about ten past four and told the woman of thirty-eight, and she said she had taught that fold to about four hundred people in nineteen years and in every one of those rooms somebody was frightened of getting it wrong in front of her, and the fold does not care, and she did not know that until a man she had never met put the kettle on.
4. **Ch 214, the Thursday of week 71 — the sixth person brings something and it comes back out.** A man of thirty-three, heating, nine years on a company's rota, in twice before and bringing nothing, brought the first nine minutes of a job: stand in the hall about four seconds, look at the ceiling in the hall, look at the outside of the nearest radiator, find the consumer unit and read the labels without touching anything, and only then go and find what you came for. **A first-year of nineteen wrote it down in his own words in about four minutes, kept the two words he replaced with more accurate ones, did not tidy the sentence about the four seconds, and it went into the ring binder as its fourth thing, and he read it back twice and said it was what he said.** Ninety seconds later he said that the nine minutes are printed on his employer's job sheet with his name in the box at the bottom since he was twenty-four, and are on about eleven thousand pieces of paper in about nine hundred vans, and that the second they are on a page in a binder they are that sheet. **The larger half of his reason is his own and not the company's: he has been in that room twice and could not tell anybody who is in it — whether the cook is there three days a week or one, whether two of the four people present are written down anywhere and two are not — and a page in a room he cannot describe, in a building with a company's telephone number on the outside of it and no name, is a page he cannot take back, and if his company ever hears that their job sheet is on a shelf in the Lower Wards then the first thing that goes is that number and the second thing that goes is a year four people have signed.** A first-year of nineteen read two lines off the wall, that it will not keep a copy of a thing nobody asked to keep and that it will not be printed, and he agreed in about four seconds, took the page out of the binder himself, one handed, folded it once and put it in his inside pocket. He named his cost unasked: he is the only person in this city who knows the four words by which his own version differs from the printed one, and at ten that morning he was the only one and now four people are. **And the first-year said out loud that this is her own argument, made on a floor three weeks ago and on a counter the week before, that she lost it twice, and that she is not going to stand in a room and act as though a man of thirty-three has taught her something — and he agreed, and said that it is the reason he mentioned the telephone number on the outside of the building before he mentioned his own name at the bottom of a sheet, and she had no answer and about four people in that room watched a nineteen-year-old not be able to answer.**
5. **Ch 215, the Friday of week 71 — eleven jobs, a stair fitting, and a woman on foot.** Grade three from twenty to eight, thirty-four degrees in a stairwell in Nine Bridges, nine in date out of eleven: a stair light, two lift calls, three landing lights of which two were not faults, a fitting on a stair off a service road, a cold-store door, a tap. **A cold-store door that closes, two readings taken with his own instrument because he always takes two, both correct, logged, and a man of about forty-four in a white coat who said the figure had held for a month and that this is the fourth visitor in a month and the other three were also right. A lamp inside tolerance that he replaced with an identical lamp, which made no difference and is the third lamp fitted to that fitting in a year of which the first two were also sound. And a fitting of a type not manufactured since before his apprenticeship, of which about nine hundred stand in four buildings in this city and of which about nine hundred of the current type have already gone in over about two years, changed although the job sheet did not ask for a fitting and nobody asked him to, because the old one is what will be a problem in four years — and it made no difference that day and it will be the reason nobody ever examines that stair, and he will not be the person anybody asks.** The word went from nine a minute to ten at about one in the afternoon and was still ten at ten at night, and about four people in a canteen off a service road at about twenty past four said nothing to him. **A man of twenty-nine who works on lifts came in at about seven on his own time on the day his employer's list runs on, with a bag of spanners he has never opened in that building, had nothing and wanted nothing and was asked nothing, sat down in the fourth chair at about eight and left at about half past nine, and said at the door that this is the best hour and a half he has had in four years, and did not explain it, and has not been asked.** And a woman of about sixty-eight walked from a bus stop because the bus does not come to a service road, having read a leaflet that carries one sentence and a first-year of nineteen's name at the bottom in her own hand in about eleven hundred pockets in about a fortnight, put in none of them by anybody in this case, and asked whether she could be told what happens in there, and got nine words counted on the page: *I could not tell you what happens in there.* **He said out loud, before anybody else spoke, that it is not an improvement on the nine words he said in a market nine days before, and gave the difference: the first set was nine words about the room and this set is nine words about himself, and a man who has run out of sentences about a room starts talking about himself.** She said she had come to the wrong place, and was told that she had come to a room with nine chairs in it and about four people in it who had not asked her a question, and that if she wants to know what happens in it, that is what happens in it. She stayed about forty minutes, asked for nothing, drank from the one mug in that building that has no chip in the handle, and said at the door that they have got a good room and that what happens in a good room is nothing. **The page stayed empty, for the second time in a month and for the second different reason: the first time a category would have been created, and this time there is no column in this city for a person being right.**
6. **Ch 216, the Monday of week 72 — the seventh thing offered, and the first line read aloud.** A man of forty-nine, nine years in a canteen in a building off a service road with about eleven hundred people in it, taught about a hundred and forty people in six years to carry four hot plates at once — two hands each, never four up off the pass in one go, the second two starting before the first two land, a rhythm and not a trick, about a hundred and twenty of them can still do it — and put a laminated sheet on the counter and said before anybody touched it that it is not his. **The structural reason, given unasked: if the room holds it in his own words then there are two versions, and the one in a binder with a person's name at the top of it is the one that will read as the correction, and nobody has to say that out loud for a correction to exist, and in about four years somebody in an office finds out their sheet is not the only one and changes it, and about nine canteens have a superseded thing on a wall and about a hundred and forty people were taught by a man who is not the man on the new sheet. He has never wanted his name on either sheet.** A first-year of nineteen put a hand flat on the ring binder on the shelf behind her and did not open it, went and read the first line off the hardboard, and said the man had worked it out at the table before she reached the wall, which is why she said it anyway. The sheet went back into its pocket and the binder stayed on the shelf with three things in it. **At the door he said the thing that is the finding of the movement: the nine lines have no force, there is nobody in this city who could make him obey them, he would have been within the law for about four years, and he did not argue with a wall because in the previous week a man took somebody else's page out of that binder and he does not know the man's name and does not need to.** She answered in about nine words: that is not a rule that works, that is four people who happened to be in a room, it has worked twice, and one of the times it cost the person it was done to. He said: then it has worked twice, and he is going to go and be a canteen. A cook of twenty-seven said at about twenty past twelve that there are nine lines, that one of them stopped somebody that morning, that it is not a sign anybody put up, that it is not enforceable, and that it is the only thing in that building that has ever stopped anybody. **A woman of about thirty was in the third chair from the door for about two hours and was asked nothing.**
7. **Ch 217, the Wednesday of week 72 — forty-five, and a price.** Seven sittings on the Wednesdays of weeks 48, 52, 56, 60, 64, 68 and 72, six gaps of four weeks, six of the seven at which the book did not open now counted as six and a half, and the woman of sixty-one said out loud that nobody in this city can say what a half is meant to mean and that she has not gone to find out. **The telephone count moved from twenty-nine to thirty-one to thirty-three and was said out loud because it is the only thing in that room that has moved all year; she said she has said that number out loud in that room every four weeks and twelve times, and reconciled the twelve against the book's seven herself and unasked, in one go, before anybody asked: seven of those are in this book, the first five are not and are not going to be — so the twelve is a figure about her and not a sitting count, the room existed before the book did, and a later writer must not read it as one; two people rang in the four weeks since, she told both of them to come on a Wednesday, one of them is in the room and one is not, she has not telephoned the one who is not coming, and she is not going to say which of the four callers in eight weeks is the one on the morning, and gave the same reason as in the spring and refused to give it a third time.** Two items at four minutes each — a man of about thirty-eight who has been asked to sign something at work and wanted to know what happens if he does not, and a woman of about twenty-six who wanted to know whether a person may say no to a thing that is good for them — and neither is recorded. **The tin, which has no lock on it and is not a system, was opened for about eleven seconds for the first time in nineteen years, and only after the caller had declined to say her words standing up, on the ground that a word is not taken off anybody, a word nobody meant is not taken either, and a word said in a car goes into that book as *she said it in a car*, which got into it nineteen years ago and is the worst line in it.** She had telephoned in the spring, had been told to come on a Wednesday, had said she would think about it, has thought about it for about four months, said before sitting down that she nearly did not come, and said that she telephoned because somebody wrote down something she said, that it was not a doctor, that it was a room with a form in it, that she was not asked anything, and that she was not going to say what she said because if she said it in there then it was in there. **Line forty-five is her own words in the first column — *they wrote down what I said and I never knew they were writing*, thirteen words, counted on the page and not estimated — nothing in the second, and her own name in the fourth, and the book was turned round and pushed four inches across the table and read back to her before she was allowed to sign it with a pen off that table.** The price was named out loud by the woman of sixty-one and nobody asked her to: for nineteen years the answer to the question why the book had forty-four lines and not forty-five has been a rule, and a rule is true and has never cost anybody anything, because there has been nothing in the book to take out, and **from this morning the answer is not a rule, it is that the book grew once, and an answer of that shape means a person is attached to it, and anybody in this city may now telephone and ask when it is going to grow again, which is the only question she has ever been asked that she has no answer to, and it has taken forty-five lines and nineteen years.** She did not thank the caller and she did not thank a man of twenty-two who has been in nine of these sittings and said nothing for about two hours, and was told she could not stop saying it and he could not either, and that this is the arrangement. Nobody wrote anything down. A first-year of nineteen heard the book open and has not said one word about it, and the woman of sixty-one has still never once used her name in that room.
8. **Ch 218, the Thursday of week 72 — a person in a room and a person in a sentence.** A man of thirty-one, hearing aids, nine years, three rooms and a van, about four hundred people a week, had been in the room twice the week before and folded a sheet badly on purpose both times without knowing it. **What he does is listen to a room for about three seconds without asking anybody anything: a person who has stopped wearing an aid does not go quiet, they go on answering on time, they turn further than they need to, they look at a mouth about nine inches below the mouth, and none of the three is a fault in the aid. A first-year of nineteen asked what it is for, and he answered, at her request and for about a minute and a half in front of about nine people, and the answer is a claim about a use: it is for when somebody has stopped, because a person who has stopped will go on answering for months and nobody finds out, and the moment of finding out is the moment somebody tells them they have been talking to a wall for a year and that cannot be given back; and it is for the ones who cannot tell you, because they have been stopped a long time and have got very good at it, and the good ones are the ones you will not catch.** Nobody in that room can say the question was wrong and everybody can say it was the right one. She wrote a page in his own words in about four minutes, in the order he said it, not tidied, and it reads as a set of three things to look at, which are the three things he has been doing since he was twenty-two and have never once been the three seconds. **He read it back twice, about eleven seconds each time, and said it was not what he said and said immediately that it was in his words and in the right order and could not have been written better in about eleven minutes by anybody, and that nobody had done anything wrong.** He had given the use and not the practice, and the use is the easy one and nobody can do the easy one badly, and the practice cannot be printed, cannot be put on a wall, and cannot be learned off a person in four minutes, and is acquired by repetition on the order of nine hundred times until the ear has it. **A nurse of twenty-four at the back of the room said the finding once: her own work is the same shape, about nine times a year she reads back what a person said to her and it is not what they said, and it has never been because they lied, and a person in a room is doing a thing and a person in a sentence has got a reason attached and you cannot get the reason off and keep the thing.** The man of thirty-one said that the carefulness is the reason the page is wrong and that it is the most careful anybody has been to him since he was twenty-four. **The first-year named the fifth thing in that binder herself, out loud, to the room: a use. She has watched a cure and a promise take about nine months off somebody's life and has been careful for two years and her carefulness made a false page. She then asked for a rule fixing which question gets asked before anybody brings something there, and was told no before she had finished the sentence, on the ground that every rule written in that room gets carried to somebody who was not in it and a rule about which question gets asked is a rule about which people get asked; she put the pen down and did not pick it up again for about a minute, and the answer was to let the thing be wrong in front of the person who said it, not to put it in the book, and that it costs about nine minutes and the nine minutes are the price of the book being worth having.** The page went into his own inside pocket and the binder went back on the shelf with three things in it.
9. **Ch 219, the Monday of week 73 — one stroke.** At about eleven, at a sink, before the kettle, with about nine people in the room and the shutter up, **the first person ever recorded in that room asked to be taken out of it.** The item is the eleventh of eleven on the first sheet of the binder: a person's own name, spelled as its owner spells it, entered in a second hand at a cook of twenty-seven's request because she cannot spell it, standing there for about a month. **Its owner telephoned the cook at work on the Thursday of the week before, having been told her name is on a piece of paper in a building, and asked for it to be taken off, and the cook reported that request in the room in about nine words and declined to enlarge on it. The reason is about the record and not about the person and she insisted on the distinction in the room: she is the person who entered the name, she entered it on a judgement that a name is a courtesy, it is not a courtesy, she has been on the receiving end of a name on a page twice, the other ten items on that sheet are hers, and the eleventh was not hers to give, and a name on a page in a binder on a shelf in a room with a door and a bell-push on it is a name that can be looked up, and no form in this city takes a name off a page, and the only route left is a pen.** Three obstacles were raised out loud: the name is not in the cook's hand, so the only person who could cross it is the woman it belongs to, who is four miles away at work, has not been asked to come and would not come; writing the question out first is a page and this room has agreed not to have those; **and a first-year of nineteen said plainly that she was not choosing him — there is exactly one person in that room whom anybody in this city can telephone, everybody present knows it, and he said it himself on that floor the Thursday he signed the hardboard.** He asked for the question to be put aloud so that he would not be the one deciding it, said he cannot answer it as a rule because a rule is not enforceable against the sister who is not in the room, and said he is going to do it because there is nobody else and because his own argument about findability is not true until somebody applies it to him. **A biro out of his own pocket, not the first one within reach; the first sheet; he did not read the sheet, did not touch the other ten items and did not go round the page; one stroke, top left to bottom right, through the middle of the name, in about a second and a half, not retraced and not tidied. A woman of about thirty was in the third chair from the door and watched it and was asked nothing then or afterwards. The room was silent for about eleven seconds and the cook said one word. Nobody stopped it and nobody was asked to.** It cannot be undone: a finger takes the ink off and leaves the paper, which is a different operation from taking a name off a page, and if the stroke is rubbed out at about four months then at about four years somebody opens that binder and finds a name with a ghost through it and nothing to show that anybody ever asked, and the sheet becomes a document about a man striking a name out in the Lower Wards on a Monday, and the only trace anywhere of the request would be a smudge. The first-year said out loud that she is not going to argue with him about that and is going to argue with the fact that he is the only one who can be asked, and that this is a separate argument and she does not have it. **The cost, in two voices: he named a box ruled in pencil in a building in Bower Street in the spring with a name in it that cannot be extracted, cannot be corrected and may not take a second, said he has been the reason it is the only one of its kind in this city for about a term and a half, and that he has not been able to fix it and is not going to tonight; she priced the whole operation at four minutes, a five-mile walk each way, a sandwich and roughly half a morning of somebody's Saturday, called it the cheapest thing anybody in this case has ever bought, and said immediately that she does not think she is allowed to say that as a happy thing, and said it.** A cook of twenty-seven said one word and went out to take a break she has not had for about eleven weeks. **The wall gained a tenth line in the cook's own hand, four words and a conditional: it will take it back if you ask. A first-year of nineteen said there is no procedure and gave the room's only procedure, which is that somebody is asked. A man of twenty-two said the room cannot have a procedure because a procedure is a rule and a rule is unenforceable against somebody who is not in the room, and then said something he had said in Chapter 206 in different words without knowing it: a wall is not a set of rules, it is a set of things people wrote down because they needed them written down, and the second of the nine happens to be the truest thing in the room. The first-year then said out loud that a person being wrong about what they said is a fifth thing in that binder, and that in a month of daily attendance she has never seen a page go in about something anybody in it got wrong.** The stroke was not read aloud, not explained, not made an example and not minuted. A nurse of twenty-four said the cook's surname from the back of the room once and did not come back for about an hour.
10. **Ch 220, the Thursday of week 73 — six things in a binder.** About eleven people in the room at about half past seven with nine chairs in a curve and four of the eleven standing in about two feet of floor between the counter and the hardboard, and nobody asked them to sit because there was nowhere to sit. **A first-year of nineteen did the arithmetic out loud and got it right: busier than it was, smaller than it was, both at once, and a person standing in a room is a person who is not staying.** The binder came off the shelf and was opened flat on a mat in the middle of the floor, and it has **six things in it: eleven items in a cook's hand with the eleventh struck through in a single stroke; four words in a first-year's hand being the four things the room will not call a technique; the four hands of the first composite thing; a page in a printer of fifty-eight's hand about a proof and the line on it that will be read wrong; about nine words in the hand of a man of twenty-nine who works on lifts; and a page in the hand of a woman of thirty-one with a key and a diary.** Five of the six are in five hands and one has four. **The last three were written on days that are not in any chapter of this movement, in their own hands, by nobody who was asked, and no day has been set for any of them in any file.** The first-year read the six out loud and read the contents of none of them and said that anybody who wants to know what is on the struck page can go and look at it, and nobody asked to look. **Five of the six carry the answer to a question one man asked, because the room's whole method is a question and the only person in this city who can be asked to answer for it is him, and he said that out loud on that floor a fortnight and a half before this when he signed the hardboard. The sixth has four hands on it and not one of them is his: he was in that room for twenty-two minutes with a docket, was not asked to take part, did not offer, was given the reason for staying out by a cook of twenty-seven, agreed in about four seconds, and would not have permitted himself to be put on the page, and nobody did.** So he is on five of the six, and he is the only one of the people whose hands those five are in that anybody in this city can go and find. **And a woman of about thirty, who is not a practitioner, who came in because it was raining, who has been asked for nothing since the Friday of week sixty-nine and has come on every working day since, and who is not on the wall, not in the binder, not on the scrap and not in any file in this case, said the finding once and said it to him and not to the room: a man with one of nine keys is a man with a door and the whole defence is that nobody can produce the list, and you have put the same name on five of six pages and nobody does that by accident, and in about four years somebody is going to put the five pages together and find one name on all of them, and a man on five of six pages is a document, and there is no version of this that is not a trade, because if yours is not on them then somebody else's has to be, and she has listened to a cook give her reason and to a printer of fifty-eight give hers and neither is putting theirs on and neither is she.** He did not answer it and the first-year did not write it down and it is not on the wall and nobody asked her a question about why she was there. A nurse of twenty-four, who had been on her knees with a mat and had not been in the room for any of it, said that that is the finding and has said nothing else in this movement about anything anybody brought in. **A printer of fifty-eight with a shop four feet wide was not there and sent nothing. A man of twenty-nine who works on lifts sat in the fourth chair at about nine with a bag of spanners he has never opened in that building and was asked nothing for about an hour and a half, and has said once what that hour and a half was worth and is not going to say it again. About eleven people a week in this city are still going to be taught that a release waits for a man in a lanyard, about four of them are not on a rota, the figure is on the back of a woman's own card and is on no form and is not a rate, and a name on five of six pages is not a rota.** And at about half past nine a man of about thirty-eight who took four minutes at the Exchange on the Wednesday of week 72 and wanted to know the consequences of not signing something at work signed it on the Thursday of week 72 and has not slept, and came to the door to say that he had spent eight days deciding whether to ask the only person in that room anybody could ask, that he got to the door with the man about nine feet away, and that he did not come over, **and that this is the first useful thing anybody has done for him in about a month**; and he gave, for nothing, that the person who handed him the paper said that is all right we all sign them, that he has been repeating it to himself on a bus and in a flat for about five nights, and that the thing he cannot work out is that a room four miles away has a wall on it with a particular line on it and about four people in that room know the cost of it. **He could not answer that and said goodnight, and a cook of twenty-seven told the man at the door that the binder is not his, that it belongs to about nine people and about half of it is in his hand, that he is the only one of the six on five of the six and the only one the telephone rings for, and that if that is a fault the fault is about this city and not about him, and that she had said all of it once and was going to bed.**

## What Movement II of Volume 05 did not do, and what it leaves for Movement III

**It did not teach, demonstrate, assess or assist anything, in any of the ten chapters, and no page in any of them implies he could. The fourth condition is quoted in none of them and paraphrased in none of them. It did not stage the relay, imply that it got easier, or ask the carrier again, and the word *carrier* appears nowhere in the ten. There is one panel in the volume so far and it is in Chapter 209 and it does not help, the prose never refers to the apparatus, and there is no `* * *` marker in any of these ten chapters. It did not put a name over the door, and it did not put a title, an office, a post, a committee, a minute, a notice, a report, a form, a leaflet-that-is-not-the-market-one, a registration or a constitution on anybody or anything, and the only documents in existence are a ring binder with six things in it, a piece of hardboard with ten lines in six hands under a board with four words in a sign-writer's hand, a four-column book at forty-five lines in a back room with the shutters up, a schedule folder, a timetable in an inside pocket, a laminated canteen sheet in a pocket, a card in a rail in Lattice Ward, and a folder with a question on the back of a docket. It did not empty the box in pencil and did not touch it. It did not let the master leave the pocket and nothing was decided about it. It did not name the licensor, name the person who inserted the page, name the clause behind the public version, put Iona or Sorn into any file, or let any message arrive from anybody. It did not say the standing problem, invent a rule about who may be asked, or attach a number to one. It did not open the Exchange's book anywhere but Chapter 217 and did not have a caller sign anywhere else. It did not test whether a holder can be removed by a person who can be asked to do it, and it did not get past two keys of nine. It did not put Rafi Pell in a room, name Steve Akers, find the woman of thirty-four with a hip, say Asha Reed's retired sentence, write the word *pattern*, or invent a second nineteen-year-old. It did not spend the volume's midpoint: **the photograph on page two of a company's own handout, a certificate course in another city citing the board in a prospectus, a family who are right to withdraw, a strike, and the words *our method* on about nine hundred leaflets are all still where they were, and the person who exposes the photograph is a printer of fifty-eight and a clerk of forty-four and not the protagonist.** And it did not find the woman in this city who has been waiting since the spring because nobody told her a man could refuse to come.**

It supersedes nothing in the `VOLUME 04 — MOVEMENT V` block below it and it inherits every line of it, of Movements IV to I, and of the `VOLUME 03 CLOSED` block, subject to the four re-bases recorded in `outline/volume-05.md`, which is the one Volume 05 must be written to. The batch's own account is `workspace/volume-05/batch-0001/SUMMARY.md` and the volume's dated hand-off to Volume 04 is `workspace/volume-04/batch-0005/VOLUME-CLOSE.md`, which this block does not overwrite. Load-book entries 204 to 213, continuous, one per chapter.**

**REPAIRED IN THE SECOND REVIEW PASS, AND A WRITER MUST NOT RE-DERIVE ANY OF THESE.** **A THIRD AND THEN A FOURTH REPAIR PASS RAN AFTER THAT ONE AND THEIR FINDINGS ARE IN THE SAME CATEGORY: NOT ONE OF THEM CHANGED A SCENE, A BEAT, A DAY, A NAME OR A NUMBER THE READER HAS BEEN GIVEN, AND EVERY ONE WAS IN A LOAD BOOK, IN A FIGURE, IN A DAY MAP, IN A STATE FILE, OR IN MARKUP IN THE WRONG VOICE. THE FOURTH PASS FOUND EIGHT AND THEN A REVIEW OF THE FOURTH FOUND FIVE MORE, AND ITS FULL LOG IS THE FOURTH PASS SECTION OF `workspace/volume-05/batch-0001/SUMMARY.md`; WHAT A WRITER NEEDS FROM IT IS THIS: the room has been open fourteen days at Chapter 208 and sixteen at Chapter 209, the sign-writer's letters are four inches high and not four letters high, a colon in front of a reported clause is not a quotation mark, the Vault under a building in Crown Terrace is three weeks before Chapter 203 and not two, Asha Reed is in the FIRST year of her maintenance-track course and the bible has said so since Volume 01, and Chapter 202's floor scene is the morning before Chapter 203 and not the evening.** The third pass's eight: **fifty-three bold spans that were in the narration, where the rule is that nothing in the narration is bolded at all** — removed, the ten files being word-for-word identical to their pre-edit copies with the markup stripped from both sides; **four more date intervals**, so that the card in the rail is four days old at Chapter 201, six at 202, seven at 203, eight at 204, eleven at 205, thirteen at 206, fifteen at 207, eighteen at 208, twenty at 209 and twenty-one at 210, and the room has been open eleven days at Chapter 207, fourteen at Chapter 208 and seventeen at Chapter 210 — the fourteen being the fourth pass's correction of a figure the third pass carried forward from Chapter 207; **Chapter 210's load book calling the sign-writer's letters *four letters high*, and Chapter 206's prose saying the same about the board, where the letters are four inches high;** **a bold span nested inside a load-book frame in Chapter 208;** **an eighty-character run in Chapter 202's load book against Chapter 192's own load book, reworded;** **a within-batch attribution in the batch summary that named the wrong sentence as the largest shared run;** and the measurement figures themselves, which were stale in the summary and are now 28,010 words, 413 raw, 291 true hedges on the tight reading, 158 bold at 5.64. **A load book is written to be read by a writer who does not have the day map, so a load book's intervals and a load book's object dimensions have to be recomputed from the day map and from the prose, or they will be inherited wrong — that is this pass's finding and it is a different finding from Volume 04's, which is about a copy nobody opens.** The full log and the method for every figure are in `workspace/volume-05/batch-0001/SUMMARY.md`. Five facts in this block were wrong in the files and are now right, and the batch's chapters were corrected to match, and no chapter was restarted and no scene was rewritten. **1. ASHA REED IS THE FIRST-YEAR OF NINETEEN, and there is no second nineteen-year-old in this case; the batch wrote her under both names in fifteen chapters without printing that they were one woman, Chapter 202 now names her where she first appears, and the name at the bottom of the leaflet in Chapter 209 is hers, which is what every state file in this repository already said.** 2. **The wall in Chapter 210 has nine lines in six hands and seven names, and there were eight people in the room: six who wrote a line, a woman of about thirty who was not asked to sign, and him.** 3. **The market in Chapter 210 had eleven people stop, about four spoken to and about seven who took a piece of something, and Asha Reed's speech now says seven and not nine.** 4. **The card went into the rail on the Thursday of week sixty-seven and is twenty-one days old at the end of Movement I, and the batch said eight days, nine days and about six weeks in three places; the true intervals are four days to Chapter 201, eleven to 205, fifteen to 207 and twenty-one to 210.** 5. **Chapter 206 does not name Chapter 225, Chapter 209 does not count itself in two hundred chapters, and Chapter 205 does not count volumes, and the word *about* was removed from six prose measurements that were not uncertain.** The full log is the second section of `workspace/volume-05/batch-0001/SUMMARY.md`.

## What Chapters 201–210 added to the record, in the order it is established

1. **Ch 201, the Monday of week 68 — a room, a ring file, and a pen that went back in a pocket.** A converted shop unit off a service road in the Lower Wards: four rooms and a back one, a shutter, a counter, nine chairs counted twice on the same morning by two people who did not know the other was counting, a ring binder with nothing in it, a kettle belonging to a woman of twenty-seven, two plug sockets of which one works, a bell-push four inches to the left of the door that has never been connected to anything, a window onto a wall about four feet away, a floor that is not level, and a back door that sticks about a foot into the room and wants a millimetre off the top edge and nothing else. A plate outside carries a company's telephone number and no name. **A letting clerk of thirty-nine with a ring file and a coat with the company's name off it put the file on the counter and did not open it, and stopped a pen, and said why: four people writing down what they decided in a room with a door in it means that in about four years the page is the room and the page can be carried to somebody who was not in the room, and then the four of them are the four people who decided it, in a flat, with no way to say they were young.** He put the pen away in about four seconds. The counter-argument was made by him in one sentence — if nobody writes it down it stops when the four of them stop coming — and she agreed with it and gave the other half, which is a fire in her own office in the spring and about nine boxes of paper and one man's handwriting. **The freehold is an entry on a schedule of title, the person who owns the entry was never met and is not a character, the first year is at no rent with no second-year figure on the page or the schedule or the file, the frontage on this side of the row has been empty eleven years and there is a plan in a council file she read and would not describe, and the reason is a sentence about a vacant frontage to four sets of people and not improved.**
2. **Ch 202, the Wednesday of week 68 — the Exchange, the sixth sitting running, and the book shut.** A back room, shutters up, a table, a tin with no lock, a four-column book at forty-four lines, four minutes an item, no vote for anybody, eight chairs round the table and a ninth against the wall with its back to the room. **Six sittings at which the book has not been opened — the Wednesdays of weeks forty-eight, fifty-two, fifty-six, sixty, sixty-four and sixty-eight — which is six sittings, five Wednesdays in between them and five gaps of four weeks.** The fortieth line is three years old and was put there by a man who rang, said nine words and signed his own name. Line forty-four has a hole in it where a woman of sixty-eight in bay four ought to be. **The telephone count moves from twenty-nine to thirty-one for the first time in a term: two calls in four weeks and neither caller came, and she has not telephoned anybody, and the reason is given out loud — the day she starts ringing people to ask them to come to a room in there, the room is a woman on a telephone, and a woman on a telephone can be asked where a room is. She offered the name of neither caller and gave the reason for that offer as well: the answer to *why did nobody come* would then be *because a woman rang them twice*, which would be true and would be the whole of it.** Two items, four minutes each. A card out of a catalogue with a year on it and nothing else. **And a first-year of nineteen, sitting on the floor because the eight chairs were taken, says nine words and they are aimed at him and not at the woman of sixty-one: *You have a room, and you speak for us.*** And then the reason in four parts, the best of which is that nobody in a room has to say anything to be described by the person who has the room. The woman of sixty-one said nine is not a number she would use in that room and used it anyway, and gave the rule in one go and did not shorten it. **The book did not open, the tin went back into a drawer with no lock on it, and nobody wrote anything down.**
3. **Ch 203, the Thursday of week 68 — four lists, and the volume's first private friction.** Four people, a ring binder, about nine sheets of paper, two hours and forty minutes, and nobody settled anything. **Dessa Kwan's eleven items, which are what she cooks, the eleventh written by somebody else because it is her sister's name and goes on the wall and not in her mouth. Asha Reed's four things that will not be called a technique in that room: a cure, a promise, a result, an improvement. Talia Venn's five things the room has to refuse to do, the last of them that it will not keep anything overnight, because a thing left overnight has a morning in it and a morning is when somebody decides what it was for. And his one line, about a person running a practice with a boundary and a fourth step and somebody watching the fourth step happen — and then the fourth condition, whole and in the order it has to be said in, said once in this case by the man it is on, and the finding: he cannot do that line, not in that room, not on a Saturday, not with the door shut, and the reason the condition exists is a man of twenty with two cut tendons in a forearm who is alive and has a kitchen.** The friction is about public testimony and not about a technique. He wanted the room described in public and gave a draft sentence with no name on it; she said that a description in public is a description of the people in it and that he had not got round it by not putting a name on the front. **He did not drop the leaflet, she did not agree to it, and neither of them said one word about anything that happened under a building in Crown Terrace three weeks ago.**
4. **Ch 204, the Friday of week 68 — eleven jobs, nine in date, and a sentence a man had been holding since the spring.** Grade three from eight. A stair light already switched off at the isolator by a man of about thirty-eight in a district jacket who wrote it on his own job sheet. A door closer on a fire door being adjusted for the fourth time in six weeks by three contractors who did not know about each other, where the fourth adjustment was right. A lamp ballast done for the fourth time. A tap with a stopcock needing nine turns more than it got. Two lift calls. Three lights on landings. **Nine out of eleven jobs on a Friday being a light or a fire door, and that is a fact about the job sheet and not about anybody.** The fifth job is a shared lighting circuit on the fourth floor of a block in Nine Bridges, dropping out at about six in the evening for as long as anybody on that floor has been there; nine working tubes and one not; the one that is not has a third-party fitting on somebody else's correct order from about four months ago; he wrote *see order, third party* in the box where a job gets described and wrote no name. A woman of about twenty-six with sixteen Fridays written on the back of her own card in a hand that gets smaller towards the end says the thing to that floor about nine times and says she has got better at saying it and worse at believing it. **He did not ask her what the thing was, and that is the correct decision.** A woman of about thirty-one is in the Exchange that week and is not asked. **And at about twenty past ten, a man of thirty-four on nights, eleven years on the same rota, standing in his own doorway with his coat on, after a count of two, says the sentence he has been sitting on since the spring: he has told four people on that floor for a term and a half that a release waits for a man in a lanyard, he has never once said that man's name, and if that man were killed on that service road tomorrow the sentence would come out of his mouth on Friday exactly the same.** He wanted about four minutes and had them and was going to sleep.
5. **Ch 205, the Monday of week 69 — the movement's midpoint, and four names on a sheet.** Seven of nine clauses read out in about nine minutes and all seven true; then the two she has not read to anybody, which are the absence of a second-year figure and a ninth that says if four people sign they are the four people with nothing about what happens if one stops. **There is no clause nine(b) and she looked, and every set of people in four years has asked her about clause eight and not one of them about clause nine.** The sentence about a vacant frontage is given four times and not improved, and the fifth time she gives a different one: the unit has been a void for four years, a void has been on a weekly list since the spring before last, a void on a list is visible to somebody above her, she has never been told to do anything about it and has never asked, and the reason she has never asked is that the last time she did, a manager explained to her for about nine minutes why a question about a void is a problem for whoever wrote it. **Then four people signed, and the first document in this case with four names on it, and each of the four named the cost out loud before signing** — a cook whose registered work address and this address would become one address to a licensing man with a clipboard; a nurse who has spent four years making sure nothing anybody can look up has her name at the top of it, and whose own reason is that a room with two names on it is a man's room; a first-year for whom it is the first thing with an address on it that she has ever signed, out of two years of being careful, and who would rather stop being careful in a room with a bell-push in it than in a common room with a kettle in it; and a man of twenty-two who named the card in the rail, said the reason given for it does not help, and signed. Nobody thanked the woman with the ring file and she left before anybody could. **On a service road he said four words — *that is a mistake* — and was told the arithmetic is one name and not four, that she has just signed, that he has just watched her sign, and he has no answer to that.**
6. **Ch 206, the Wednesday of week 69 — there is not going to be a name over the door, and there is one key.** A sign-writer of about sixty, forty-one years, about four thousand of these and about three thousand nine hundred the same six words in a different order, offered a proper plate with a name on it and a telephone number whether you want one or not. **He wrote six letters on a sheet of A4 on the Monday night in the repair shop, tore them up, wrote them again on the Tuesday morning, and a first-year of nineteen found the sheet face up on the counter, read it, put it in her folder and did not ask him what it was.** Four words instead, in his hand, on a piece of hardboard about the size of a tea tray with the letters four inches high, inside by the door at the height of a person's shoulder, not screwed to anything: *Bring what you do.* **A name cannot be taken off aluminium with anything and a word can be taken off a board with a thumb, and that is the entire difference, and he has never in his life had anybody ask him for the second one. He left a piece of sandpaper on the counter, was paid four pounds, and was not thanked.** The argument took about half an hour in three positions and the man of twenty-two was not the strongest of them: a room with a key and a bell-push is the same object as a card in a rail and he can see the larger one from the doorway; a nurse of twenty-four against, in nine words and then in forty, on the ground that a room that cannot be found is a room for people who already know, and who four days earlier had said the reverse and been told the conclusion was wrong, so the two of them now have one each; a cook of twenty-seven against on a different ground, that a kitchen of hers was found twice in nine years by a man with a clipboard and both times there was a name on the door and both times it was the owner's; and a first-year of nineteen agreeing in one sentence, then saying her sentence of the Wednesday, and then one sentence too many about people ringing him. **And one key, handed over on a counter at about eleven in the morning, not a set, with the reason given for a locked door on a Sunday: a room in this city that is open every day of the week is a room that somebody is in every day of the week, and she does not know anybody who is and has not met the person who is.** A key is not a post, which is Chapter 186's finding, and the whole of the difference is that the people who handed it over can take it back — **and the people who handed this one over are a company with a ring file, and a company is not a person and cannot be asked to take anything back. That is untested and it is not solved in this chapter.**
7. **Ch 207, the Friday of week 69 — the first person to use the room, and she did not come for a technique.** A woman of about thirty, unlet in by anybody, not on a rota, not on a course, not referred, with a carrier bag holding two things that stay on her lap and are never taken out. **She states her own reason in about eleven seconds: it had been raining since about four, it was warm in there, and she had thought it was a letting agent's office with the lights on. And she states a second true thing about herself, that a person who has been in a room twenty minutes and been given a cup of tea has not thought anything, and that a person who wants somebody to have thought something is going to be disappointed and she would rather say it in the first minute than the fortieth.** She asks for one thing in the whole visit and gets it, and it is a cup, in a mug with a chip in the handle. **A cook of twenty-seven made the tea and said the thing she has repeated about four times: nine years is what a cup of tea is, nine years of doing it for people who are somewhere they did not plan to be, not one of them able to pay her, not one of them taught it by anybody, and about four of them a week are somebody doing it alone on a Tuesday with no hot water. She did not say the word *technique* and nobody in the room said it for her.** A man of twenty-nine who works on lifts and is on his employer's Friday list took about a millimetre off the top edge of the back-room door with a hand plane in about four minutes, because a man of twenty-two said on the Monday that it wanted that and nothing else; nobody asked him and nobody paid him and no line anywhere carries his name against it. **And a man of about forty-one from the letting agent's own list fitted a second bell-push and a sounder in about forty minutes, and the bell rings, and nothing changes: it was tested from outside only, because from outside is where a person is when they press it, and a sounder makes a noise at a man on a step and does not make anybody come, and about four thousand of those decisions are made in this city every day.** Asha Reed does not write the twenty minutes down and gives the reason: the day a woman who came in wet is written down, the binder has a category in it, and the category is the thing she has been trying to get for two years, and she would be having it because somebody turned up on a Friday and not because anybody came to be taught. **The page is left empty. The cook agrees. He writes nothing either, which is the third time in a term and a half.**
8. **Ch 208, the Monday of week 70 — four steps of which three are portable and one is not, and a nine-word refusal.** A man of fifty-eight who inspects buses, thirty-one years on the same round, a flat cap and a bus timetable used as writing paper, who waits until about eleven and does not say he was going to. **His four steps: get a door that has dropped into its own seal; do not force it, you will take the hinge off; two hands on the leaf, low, at the hinge side; take the weight of it onto your hip and let it find itself; and then somebody on the far side takes it off the seal and walks it open.** He will not perform it because he would do it wrong, and a man of fifty-eight doing a thing badly in a room in front of a first-year is the one thing nobody can put back afterwards. When the first-year rewrites the fourth step in her own hand he says that is four of his words and a half and the last one is not in his voice, and gives the cost: a lad of nineteen on his round wrote his four steps into a book, a man off another route read it, and within about two years that depot had a poster with three steps on it and a photograph of a man in a lanyard, and the fourth step came off it entirely, and they were not doing it wrong, they were doing three quarters of it, faster, and it worked — **and the fourth step did not go missing in that depot, it went missing in about four hundred depots and there was no man in any of them who knew which one they had lost it in.** He keeps it on a timetable because a timetable goes out of date on a Sunday and nobody has ever been proud of a timetable. **And then a line cook of twenty-seven puts a knife down and says nine words, once, without improving them and without explaining them: *You have taught a door to need a person.*** He agrees in about four seconds, says he had forty minutes with a timetable to get a better sentence and did not get one, and says he is not going to argue with a cook about a door. **The three steps are not written down either, because a page with three quarters of a thing on it is the leaflet, and the leaflet is in about four thousand pockets with five jobs on the front of it and none of them a room.** A schedule comes round from the company whose number is on the plate, six items for that unit twice a year, about four hundred items in the folder, and the bell-push is on it again as *external push, serviceable* on a new number with the same wording, and a woman of about twenty-six says out loud in the room that it is the same number twice and that both of them were true on the day. **He says he will not be in that room on a Wednesday or a Monday because his round starts at four in the morning and the room shuts at ten at night, and that he will be at the market, because there is a trestle in that market with an amplifier on it and about forty people go past it in an hour and he has gone past it about nine times and never stopped.**
9. **Ch 209, the Wednesday of week 70 — the standing problem an eighth time, in a market, with the one panel of the volume.** A trestle in a market in Saltmarket put there by a company that does not know what is on it, holding a stall-holder's licence it uses about nine times a year which carries a trestle and a power supply. **A first-year of nineteen wrote one paragraph in the second week of week sixty-eight and signed it with neither a room nor a name; the answer came in about eleven days on a piece of paper with a job title on it and no name, and that paper is in a folder in a room off a service road and is a different object from a lease, from a card in a rail and from a board on a wall.** A leaflet with one sentence on it and a name at the bottom of it in the first-year's own hand, put there on the Tuesday on purpose, having been told twice by two other people that a description of a room is a description of the people in it. **Nobody can take it off: there is no form on which a name can be taken off a leaflet and no mechanism in this city that can do it.** He is not a speaker and is not introduced as one; he is nine feet away with a bag of cable ties, and the first-year says so out loud and adds that it is on purpose. **A woman of thirty-four with a district jacket and a clipboard put the trestle there, told two people that she had and would not say why in more than one sentence — the people who need a room like that are the people already standing in a market with a bag — and turned about four people away by saying she was busy until four.** About forty people go past the table in an hour; about eleven stop; about four are spoken to; about seven take a piece of something and go on their way; nobody is paid to be there. **A woman of about fifty-two with a carrier bag of fish asks whether what people say in that room stays in that room, and he says nine words, counted on the page, which are true and do not answer her: *Nobody in that room can promise you that today.*** Then he gives both halves out loud: the first half is a sentence said in a corridor by a woman of twenty-four about a page nobody may read dying with the person, and about nine days of a room with four chairs in it; the second half is nobody, and nobody had ever asked him the first half, which is that the room is the smallest room anybody has built for this and is not going to be the one that fixes it. **And then the apparatus, once, in the space where the microphone stand is, four feet from her: *The condition stops you doing the thing. It does not stop anybody asking you about it.*** It is the sixth time in two hundred chapters and the only one that has appeared where somebody was asking him a question. It is true, it answers nothing, it offers no next step, and **he looked at the rule instead of at her face, because the rule was true and it was the most useful sentence anybody had put in front of him in about eleven days.** The woman of fifty-two then said one more sentence and he did not hear it. He said sorry. She said it again once and slower: **there is a woman in this city who has been waiting since the spring because nobody told her a man could refuse to come, and she is not going to say her name in a market and has not said it to the girl on the table either.** Nobody wrote it down. **Talia Venn was nine feet away for about eleven minutes and did not intervene, and the reason is not forgiveness and is not coldness: somebody in front of forty people would have stepped in, and then the thing that went out of that table would have been hers, and a nurse saying a room is careful is worth about half of a cook saying it, and she is not spending hers on about half — and the other half, which she gave when it was put to her, is that she could have said something smaller, and he would have looked at her instead of at the rule, and in about four years somebody could say that the woman who made a room careful was a woman who is in a corridor with him.** The bus inspector from Chapter 208 stops at the trestle at about ten to four, having gone past it about nine times in four years, and says out loud that this is a sentence somebody has already printed. **He comes back to that room in about nine weeks with something different.**
10. **Ch 210, the Thursday of week 70 — the close, and a list on a wall.** A piece of hardboard about the size of a tea tray inside by the door, under the sign-writer's board, not screwed to anything, about nine lines in six hands, no date, no number, no reference and nothing at the top. **Two things had happened in nine days and neither was planned: a woman of about thirty came back three times after coming once because it was raining and had been in the room for about six hours across three visits, and a market acquired a sentence — eleven people stopped, about four were spoken to, about seven took a piece of food, a leaflet went out with a first-year's name on it, and on the Thursday morning a woman telephoned the number on the plate outside the unit and was put through to a letting clerk who said her sentence about a vacant frontage to a fifth set of people who did not know that was what she was doing.** Asha Reed says the wall is needed because on Tuesday nobody could tell afterwards whether a woman had been in the room, and on Wednesday about seven people who do not know them got a piece of food off a table, and those are two rooms about four feet apart — **and a wall is less than a form, and she has had that argument twice this week and lost it twice.** The nine lines were written out loud one at a time and a line was not a line until the person who had lived it said it. **The first of the nine is the cook's, said with her hands in a bowl of water: it will not keep anything it cannot take back.** Two lines are the cook's, two a first-year's, two a woman of thirty-one's, one a bus inspector's, one a man of twenty-nine's and one a printer of fifty-eight's, and the printer's is four words — *it will not be printed* — and the cost she named out loud for that one line is a hundred and forty-one pounds in an envelope under a till with no name on it that cannot be sent back and has been there since the spring. **THE PERSON WHO IS NOT ASKED TO SIGN IS THE WOMAN OF ABOUT THIRTY, AND THE REASON IS GIVEN OUT LOUD IN THE ROOM BY ASHA REED, WHO IS THE FIRST-YEAR OF NINETEEN AND IS THE ONLY NINETEEN-YEAR-OLD IN THIS CASE, AND IT IS GIVEN BACK BY THE WOMAN HERSELF: if a wall has her name on it, then in about four years the only line anybody reads out is a person who was in a room because it was raining.** Nobody asked her twice and nobody argued and the pen went past her. **And he signs it, having written none of the nine, and says the reason out loud once: he is the only one of the seven anybody in this city has learned how to find in about three weeks; the other six are at a counter, a shop four feet wide, a depot, a first floor with a key in it and a kitchen, and one of the six is on nine Fridays a week across four employers and is not in the same place on two Fridays running, and you would have to already know all six; and if his name is not on that wall then somebody has to be in that room every day of the week, and neither he nor a woman with a ring file has met the person who does that.** He added that putting his name somewhere findable on purpose is the bill, and that the bill came due in a room off a service road instead of a corridor and he did not choose which. **And the last thing said in that room is a first-year of nineteen's: it is much smaller than we wanted it to be — four chairs in the back room, two things in a binder, nine lines on a wall, and one of them on nine Fridays a week across four employers — and she wants that written down somewhere, and she is not writing it on the wall, because it is not a thing the room will not do.** About eleven people a week are still going to be taught that a release waits for a man in a lanyard, about four of the eleven are not on a rota, the number is on the back of a woman's own card, and **a name on a wall by a door is not a rota either, and nobody in that room said otherwise.**

## What Movement I of Volume 05 did not do, and what it leaves for Movement II

**It did not teach, demonstrate, assess or assist anything. It said the fourth condition whole and word for word once, in Chapter 203, by the man it is on, and put its consequence on the page: he cannot run the practice he wants the room for, and the room has to be run by somebody who can be asked whether they are competent and who can lose something when the answer is no, and there is nobody in that room like that. It did not stage the relay, imply that it got easier, ask the carrier again, or name her; there is one panel in the volume so far and it is in Chapter 209 and it does not help, and the prose never refers to the apparatus. It did not put a name over the door, and it did not put a title, an office, a post, a committee, a minute, a notice, a report, a leaflet-that-is-not-the-market-one, a registration or a constitution on anybody, and the only document the Commons has is nine lines on a piece of hardboard that six people wrote and one person was not asked to write. It did not open the Exchange's book, which is at forty-four lines and has now not been opened at six sittings; it moved the telephone count from twenty-nine to thirty-one, which is the first movement in a term, and no caller came. It did not resolve the standing problem, invented no rule about who may be asked, and attached a number to it nowhere except as the eighth saying in Chapter 209. It did not test whether a key is not a post, and it did not get to nine keys. It did not empty the box in pencil, and it did not touch it. It did not let the master leave the pocket and nobody decided anything about it. It did not name the licensor, name the person who inserted the page, name the Crown Clause, put Iona or Sorn in the file, or let any message arrive from anybody. It did not put Rafi Pell back in a room, name Steve Akers, find the woman of thirty-four with a hip, or say Asha Reed's retired sentence. It did not spend the volume's midpoint: the photograph on page two of a company's own handout, a certificate course in another city, and the words *our method* on about nine hundred leaflets all arrive in Movement III. And it did not resolve the relationship: two beats, both about public testimony and both argued, no scene, no declaration, no kiss.**

---

# VOLUME 04 — MOVEMENT V (Chapters 191–200), "The Ledger", and VOLUME 04 IS CLOSED

**Volume 04 is CLOSED at Chapter 200 and the load-book run ends at entry 203. This block is the state at the end of the fifth and last movement. It supersedes nothing in the `VOLUME 04 — MOVEMENT IV` block below it and it inherits every line of it and of the `MOVEMENT III`, `MOVEMENT II` and `MOVEMENT I` blocks and of the `VOLUME 03 CLOSED` block. The batch's own account is `workspace/volume-04/batch-0005/SUMMARY.md`, and the volume's dated hand-off to Volume 05 is `workspace/volume-04/batch-0005/VOLUME-CLOSE.md`, WHICH SUPERSEDES THIS BLOCK for anything about what Volume 05 inherits.**

## What Chapters 191–200 added to the record, in the order it is established

1. **Ch 191, the Monday of week sixty-four — a page comes out of a skip in about nine hundred pages of other people's purges, correctly, with nobody at fault.** A maintenance schedule in Crown Terrace running on eleven weeks; a strip of brown tape across the corner of a bundle at about seven; a line into a disposal book in a hut at about half past seven; about nine hundred pages over a wall at nine. A man of about sixty counts bundles with steel weights, a clipboard, a roll of tape and a pair of steel weights, and says the count out loud to nobody. **Bundle nine was counted as a hundred and six and held a hundred and seven, because he counted a hundred and six, wrote it on the corner of the top sheet, and taped the corner, and both operations are correct and either one on its own puts that sheet in, and a bundle of a hundred and six with a hundred and seven sheets in it is not an error anybody can see from the outside. Nobody weighed it and nobody was going to, and four people each did the right thing.** The page came out flat on the concrete, face up, when four sheets went over on the corner. **It is a service sheet, about the same age as a school exercise book, with a location in the top corner described in words and not by a room number — *continuity hall, sub-level plant, chamber 3* — eleven dated marks down a printed column, all the same mark, and nothing on the back.** Petar Vance, a field service engineer of fifty-one who services the exchange rooms and prints the plates, was in the plant room behind that wall with a tool bag because a heating circuit on that floor had been losing pressure since Friday and nobody had written one word about it. **He is the only person in this city who can say what a plate belongs to, and about nine hundred of the plates in four buildings came off one line of his own company's list and carry a number off a different line of the same list, and he has given that number out loud in about four rooms and nobody has ever asked him for it, and he cannot change nine hundred doors because a district nurse wants one room.** Chamber 3 is in no plan he has seen, out of four he holds; a plan of the tunnel in a drawer on a hook in a basement stops at the first chamber; and no company in this city holds a contract for anything in a tunnel, because a tunnel is under a building and a building is a landlord. **He put the page back into the skip, squared the four sheets that had gone over, took no photograph and made no copy, and wrote four words and a week — *ch 3, sub-level, wk 64* — in the margin of his own book, which is the first entry in four years in it that is not a part.** He said out loud, to a man with a docket in a service corridor, that the reason he put it back is that a page in a pocket in a van tonight would be the whole of it, and in about four years there would be a man in a shed with a page in a drawer and no way of telling anybody why he had it.
2. **Ch 192, the Wednesday of week sixty-four — the Exchange, the book at forty-four lines, and the fifth chair.** A back room, shutters up, a table, a tin with no lock, a book with four columns and forty-four lines, four minutes an item, no vote for anybody. **This is the fifth Wednesday on which the book has not been opened, counting from the Wednesday of week forty-eight, and the sittings are four weeks apart, so that there are three Wednesdays in between those five and four gaps between them, the sixth falls four weeks from this day and is outside Volume 04.** Ten people in the room, two of the twenty-nine who have telephoned her. **The number of telephone calls is twenty-nine and has not moved in four weeks, and she said the reason out loud: she has not telephoned anybody in four weeks, the last time she rang anybody it was the fortnight after a Wednesday in the spring and it was a frightened woman and she told her it was all right to feel frightened, she was the only person in this city who knew that woman had rung, and she cannot tell whether the second call was a habit or the third was the thing she was afraid of. She offered to ring one and did not.** The rule, in one go: **a book of what a caller said cannot be written by the people who did not call.** The fortieth line is three years old, put there by a man who rang, said nine words and signed his own name under them, and there has been no line since; line forty-four has a hole in it where a woman of sixty-eight in bay four ought to be, and she was never written down and she is not going to be. Two items, four minutes and nine minutes, and neither went in the book: a man of forty-four who wanted a room and got a sentence and said that in about four years somebody will be able to say that about forty people asked for a binder and that the number of them who asked for that one is smaller than the number who asked for a different one, which is not true and which he knew was not true and which was the reason he could say it; and a woman of about thirty who asked whether a caller who did not come ought to be written down, and was told that no rule exists for that half and none will be made at eleven at night in a full room. **The fifth chair was in the row, put there at about half eight by Ianthe Roke, thirty-one, with a key, a diary and no standing, who has told two people about the key in a corridor in the spring and nobody else. Her reason, given before she was asked twice: a chair against a wall is not an answer to anything and a chair in a row is, and she has not got a rule for who is expected, and the rule about who may be in the room is the rule about the book and she is not going to split it into two so that she has one.** She sat down in it and did not mean to. **Bea Nunn put it back against the wall herself, at the front, with both hands, and said the fifth thing she has said out loud in that room in a term and that none of the five is a rule she made up tonight. Nobody wrote anything down, the tin went back empty, and the book did not open.**
