3. **Ch 193, the Friday of week sixty-four — the first Friday rota of this movement, the fourth location gone, and a refusal that is word for word the same sentence.** Eleven jobs, nine in date, grade three from eight, the word at nine a minute until about one and at ten after one, nobody asked him where a release was going to come from, and it is not a number he keeps. **The fourth location on a docket of his own in his own shorthand and on nothing else is no longer in that building.** The stair window's frame was measured on the Monday, ordered, and fitted on the Wednesday in about four hours by two men and a boy, because the old frame was soft at the bottom and let water in when it rained hard; the frame came out whole; **the groove somebody cut into the inside of the reveal about forty years ago, two fingers wide, painted over since the summer of the fifty-fifth, went out with the old frame in a skip on the Wednesday afternoon, and nobody who did the work knew it was there, and the man who ordered the window had put his hand on the old sill to test it and did not know, and the job sheet says the window was replaced and nothing else.** The man of thirty-eight in a district jacket told him he has now been given two sentences from the same man on the same floor, identical, nine weeks apart, that he wrote the first one down because he was standing on the landing, and that he is not asking for a better reason. **The refusal is the same sentence, word for word, about eleven jobs and a Friday, and it is not improved.** Then Ines Kolar, forty-six, in a basement with a trolley and a ring file: a woman of thirty-six who does the sheets up turned one over because everybody does, read fourteen lines of pencil on the back of a condition sheet, and asked what the pencil was, about four weeks before. **She has not asked again. The reason she has not answered is that the day she answers, that woman is the person in this city who did not turn a sheet over, and in about four years somebody will want to know how she knew and the only version available will be a woman with a trolley.** The other half, said on a Friday in a basement: **there is no column on that sheet for what is on the door; there are forty-one lines and a column of room numbers and a column for conditions; a man of twenty-two asked her a question in a corridor in the week of the fifty-two and she put eleven more rooms in pencil in the space where the conditions go, because there was nowhere else; the sheet went back on the trolley and went to a building and nobody has asked for it and nobody is going to; and she is not going to be asked to say his name to explain the pencil, and if she is asked again she will say what the pencil is and stop there.** The only record of the groove in this city is fourteen lines in pencil in a ring file in a cupboard nobody has ever opened.
4. **Ch 194, the Monday of week sixty-five — the room is found, the tunnel, the hatch with no lock on it, and a third declination in writing with a count and a reason.** Petar Vance's four words in a margin became a door at the back of a plant room that is on none of his four plans, forty steps down, a landing, and **an old steel hatch with a lift ring on it, standing four inches open, with nothing on it and no lock on it. A maintenance chamber in this city has never had a lock on anything, and the reason is structural rather than admirable: the authority to lock a room is the same authority as the authority to say who goes in it, and that authority has never been written down anywhere in this city.** A man of about sixty with a trolley comes down that landing every Monday to see whether the hatch is where he left it. **Neither of them opened the hatch; they stood on a landing.** A man of twenty-two counted four doors on the way down and none of them was an exit, and said the sentence that says a room is a room, and nobody was listening. **A contractor holding the cleaning and the paper contract for that building has offered in writing, for the first time in fourteen years, to have the table on the ground floor moved into a locked room on the first, and the reasons given are insurance in the second paragraph and the impossibility of insuring a document in a corridor in the third.** Iven Sore, fifty-eight, a soft voice, a coat that has never been in a workshop, answered the first question with a smaller one, read the letter out in the first person, which nobody in that corridor has ever heard him do, and declined it — the third of three, and he told them about all three, having told nobody about the other two. **The count: eleven weeks between rounds; nine hundred and six sheets across nine bundles on the Monday of week sixty-four; one bundle entered as a hundred and six and holding a hundred and seven; nine hundred and five sheets in that building accounted for, the sixth not looked for and not going to be; one table; one bound register of about nine hundred lines in four columns with a job title in the fourth column of every line.** The reason, given the third time and different from the first two, which were about the end of the corridor: **a locked room is a room that things go into and do not come out of, the only thing that office has ever been good at is counting what goes in and what comes out, and a round that finds nothing is the same as a round that did not happen, and he would rather have nine hundred sheets in a skip than a building where nobody knows what was thrown away.** At the door he gave away the date of the next round — the Tuesday of next week, same round, same eleven weeks, same man in the yard, same item, strip of tape, line in a book in a hut — and said he did not know why he was telling them the date of a routine disposal, and that a count which is not given out loud is only a thing he has. **Nothing was agreed to, nothing was signed, no post was created, and the office retained everything it held on Monday morning.**
5. **Ch 195, the Thursday of week sixty-five — THE VAULT, ONCE, and series milestone 4, paid here and nowhere else.** The old continuity hall has a plant room on the ground floor and a service tunnel under it. **A woman of twenty-four was in that building because a mobile clinic's stock returns go to a counter there on Thursdays, and she went down the tunnel with three others because there were four of them and she was one of four, and she said why before she was asked: that a woman of forty-five had said no to her once and she would like to be in the room when she said it again, and that this was not Marek Senn's business and not the other woman's either.** The chamber is called the Crown Vault on the plans that stop at it, is historical, and nobody in it used the name; it is a maintenance chamber about eleven feet by nine with a low ceiling, a steel stair down into it, a hatch in the floor of the landing above, a painted grey convergence cabinet the size of a wardrobe with a plate on the front and a keyhole with no key in it, and about eleven conduit runs into the top of it. **Nobody locked the hatch behind them because there is nothing on the hatch to lock.** In a shallow drawer in the wall at the end is **a partial maintenance ledger, five columns, about the size of a telephone directory, cloth gone at the corners, running four years and ending about a year and a half ago with no notice at the end of it, which is not how a book ends and is how one stops when nobody is writing any more.** The fifth column has a word at the top of it that means *who gave the word*, and it is blank in every line. **He got to the floor before anybody else and stayed on the floor and did not read it, and gave the reason: that he would have read it in nine seconds and brought back two words he liked and four hundred pages of somebody's arithmetic.** **Two people he did not choose read it between him.** Sera Quill, forty-five, lead repairer at Civic Spine 7, whose plant round covers that hall, with a metal braid of tools under her shirt, turned the pages with two fingers and did not touch the writing, and about a minute in stopped with her hand on a page and said **the nine words she has said for a term and a half, word for word: *And I am not going to tell you why*** — with the reason she has always given, which is a thing about a person in this city and not about a practice, and **nobody in that chamber asked her a second question, and the name was not said there, in the corridor afterwards, in any load book, or in this volume.** Halla Rennick, forty-one, records clerk at a counter in a municipal annexe two streets away, twenty-two years, was asked in a corridor on the Friday before and said yes and said at the same time that she did not want to, and both were true on the same morning; she read the columns out in the voice she uses at a counter, and said that the fifth column being blank in every line is the first one she has read in twenty-two years where the whole column is the point. A folded run sheet behind the last leaf, about forty lines, **nine of which are the same eleven words in the same order**, which Halla Rennick read out and identified as not an instruction. **Then he asked Talia Venn to carry the page, in front of both of them, on purpose, because he wanted a room to have heard it. She said no, in nine words that are countable on the page: *I will not carry what I cannot answer for*. The refusal is about the page and not about him** — a piece of paper in a hand she cannot read goes into a coat on a bus and then into a room that is not hers, and in about four years there are two people who know what is on it and neither of them has got it, and being the person who happened to be in the room is a coincidence with a strap on, and she has been the cheapest person in a room more times than she can count in four years and has said no three of those times and this is the fourth — **and she picked up her bag off the stair and went to a counter that was opening at eleven.** **The records clerk put the leaf in her canvas satchel in front of four people on purpose and said that she was signing nothing, that there is no slip and no form, and that if anybody asks her for it in a corridor in a fortnight the answer is a list of three people and it is not one.** **At the foot of the stair, in a corridor, Talia Venn told him he did the asking in front of them on purpose, told him not to call it trust, and called it the cheapest one in a tunnel; he had no answer and she said it was all right.** He wrote down four words: *four people went down. One page came up. The one who is carrying it is not me.* **No name, no hand.**
6. **Ch 196, the Friday of week sixty-five — the second Friday rota and the eleventh and last of the eleven sessions.** **Thirty-one chairs and fourteen people**, said out loud at a quarter to ten by Corin Vasska, forty-four, an officer of a fund, who has never minuted an attendance figure, and this is the lowest in four years. **His reason for not minuting it: a minute is a thing a committee reads and this committee does not meet again, and the number would be written for about nine people who will never be in a room together.** His reason for the attendance was given four Fridays earlier and was not given again, on the grounds that a reason given twice is a habit. **His second reason is about him: a series of eleven that ends because a man of forty-four read a sheet out loud eleven times is not a series, it is a habit, and he has been the habit, the eleven was a number somebody put in a specification section three years ago, the specification does not expire, the levy does not stop, the sessions stop, nothing in this city is going to write down what replaces them, and he has decided that is better than the alternative.** He read the third line out as a third line at about ten past ten, about nine people in the room did not hear the difference, which is the fifth time that has happened and the third time this term, and **the line was not counted aloud and neither the printed count of seventeen words nor the nine a man of forty-four said in Chapter 177 is repeated anywhere in this batch.** **He did not read the five jobs off the front of the leaflet — about four hundred of those leaflets by the door, five jobs on the front of each, two of them his, put there by a system in a company out of two job sheets in the week of the thirty-fourth — for the eleventh time, having said in front of everybody four Fridays earlier that he was not going to, with the reason that about eleven people a week are being taught to wait for a man and not one of those eleven has ever heard him read it. Nobody asked him to.** One man asked him to read the times and the fee aloud for people still coming through the door and was refused in one sentence. **A man of fifty-two, the depot manager, came at half past nine and left at about eleven and said nothing**, having said four Fridays earlier, standing, in the same room with a microphone on a stand, that he had stopped asking whether anybody was going to offer to take the wording of a condition to somebody who could change it; **a man of twenty-two in the fourth row noticed both facts and used neither, and coming was the whole of it.** A woman of twenty-nine, Marguerite Okpara, on the Friday list because an employer pays and not because anybody chose her, said that about four of the eleven people a week are not on the rota for that reason, and **refused to say what sixteen Fridays written on the back of her own card since the spring are for, because the day she writes what they are for it becomes a claim and a claim is a thing somebody can ask her to give up.** A woman of twenty-six at her table by the wall, at the eleventh of eleven sessions she has attended, having not stood in any of them, said the numbers out loud for the third time in a term: eleven letters in a folder, nine answers written, none sent, the ninth the best of the nine and taking most of a Sunday, two letters she is not going to answer at all, and **it is still not a decision, because there is nothing on the paper to answer it with.** **The standing problem was not said, nobody asked him anything, and no rule about who may be asked was invented.** Seventeen chairs were empty and the microphone was on its stand all night. A book of about four hundred leaflets, four sections and three lines, one funded, and a committee that does not meet again.
7. **Ch 197, the Tuesday of week sixty-six — the purge, and the carrying out, and about nine hundred pages into a skip.** Half past six in the morning, a man of about sixty with a trolley, a clipboard, a roll of brown tape and a pair of steel weights, and a hatch standing four inches open. **Iven Sore said in his own corridor, in the first person, that he authorised it: on the Monday, in writing, in one line, in a file in a locked room on the first floor, which is the only locked room in that building, and that the file does not know what is in them. His stated reason: a maintenance record has a life, the life is eleven weeks, a chamber under a building has no lock on it, and a piece of paper in a locked room is a piece of paper in a building nobody can count. And then the sentence that is not a defence: that he did not sleep badly about it, and that a man of fifty-eight telling a corridor that he did not sleep is a thing a corridor will hold for about four years.** Halla Rennick was in the plant room with the leaf in her canvas satchel on a chair, and said out loud, in front of the man with the clipboard, at about ten past seven, that she was not putting it back in the drawer, and wanted it noted that she said no out loud on the day it was going. The round took the drawer. **A folded sheet came out of it, and a man of about sixty unfolded it because it was folded, read it quickly, and laid it flat on the top of the stack, which is also what a man does. There was no second reading, no check, no witness and no slip, and nobody in that chamber had been asked to look at anything.** Eight bundles, about nine hundred sheets, eleven weeks, the count on the corner in a block hand, brown tape across the corner, a line in the disposal book in the hut — bundle, count, tape, the Tuesday, the item, and an authority column with a job title in it, because a job title is what a man can write without stopping to think about who he is. **Every part of it was correct and the man is not a villain and was not asked to be careful and went home at about one.** Then the carrying out: up the steel stair, along a service corridor with a fire door at the end of it, forty steps, and then it stopped. **Halla Rennick stopped on a second landing, said she had got it, and gave one reason of five words — *because the last one did* — said once and not argued with. Nobody asked her who, she has never said, and Petar Vance said nothing about it then or afterwards, in a corridor, a canteen or a yard.** He then took a canvas bag off a landing rail, checked that the bag was not his, and carried it the rest of the way up and out through a service door on foot; she stayed on the landing until the door had shut and then went down. **The bag belonged to a man who left that building's contract in the spring and whose locker nobody has cleared, and neither of them asked whose it was and both of them knew by about the second landing that they were not going to. There is no mechanism in this city that can make a person hand a bag back and no form on which a person can say whose bag it was, and the only thing either of them could have done was not pick it up. Nobody thanked anybody.** The last bundle went in at about twenty past and the count was said out loud to nobody. **About nine hundred and four of the sheets are about relay plates and cable runs and valve schedules and fire doors and a heating circuit in a hall, and every one of them is a record of a thing that was done by a person who was not asked to be careful, and every one of them was destroyed correctly. One of them is the only copy in this city, and a bag on a hook behind a door in a building is a thing that can be found by anybody who has a reason to be in a plant room, and a woman of thirty-nine said that in another city in another year, and everybody in that building knew it while it was happening, and nobody has ever written down that they knew it.**
8. **Ch 198, the Thursday of week sixty-six — the tray, and nine names in it, and the sentence the volume lays its stone with.** A room off a street in Lattice Ward, a counter with a bell that is a push, four drawers under the counter with about four hundred cards in them, a trolley with a bad third wheel, a tray on the counter. **Thirty-one slips on the Friday of week fifty-nine, not one of them about a box, and fifty-eight on this Thursday, counted and said aloud every Wednesday in between, and nine of the fifty-eight have a person's name written at the bottom of them, the first about a fortnight before this day, and there were none in the spring and none in the summer, and the nine are all different people, and none of the nine has been answered.** The other forty-nine are pieces of paper and she has never asked who put them there and is not going to, because the day she asks who put a piece of paper in her tray, the room has got a column, and the room does not have a column. **A slip with a person on it is a request and it goes in the tray; a slip without a person on it is a piece of paper, and there is no tray for that either.** Four of the nine came in between half past nine and about eleven, one at a time, and none knew the other three: **a foreman of fifty-eight, Bryn Maw, with five lines of pencil in a site diary and a telephone on a wall and a stranger's box number in his hand since the spring, asking whether he is allowed to keep writing them, and told that a diary is a diary and that what he is allowed to keep is in a document behind a counter and is not going to be guessed at; a woman of twenty-nine with sixteen Fridays on the back of a card, asking whether a card is a record, and told that it is a record if somebody keeps it, and that nobody does; a care worker of thirty-eight, Doreen Abbiss, nights, six years, who came because a woman of thirty-four told her about the room once in a corridor, asking the one question whether a person can find out who has read a page, and told no, with the reason — a box in a building in Bower Street with about two hundred pages in four stacks and no system behind any of them, so there is nothing in it that could answer — and told that the difference between a no and a true thing is that somebody can be angry with a no, and that she did not sign the slip and the name on it was written by the woman of forty-one; and a printer of fifty-eight, Sunniva Bellhouse, with four feet of shop in the Quiet Quarter and a hundred and forty-one pounds in an envelope under a till that she cannot move, asking whether a person can print something and then not be the person who decides about it, and told, correctly, that the registrar does not know the answer and would rather say so than say something that would be repeated in four years as though she had thought about it.** A folder went up onto the high shelf of the trolley on the Wednesday: **a slip is required for a box coming down off that shelf, and a folder going up onto it is not that, and she is not going to start a record of what goes up, because a record of what goes up is a list, and a list is a column, and the room does not have a column. She has not read it and nobody in the room has read it.** And the sentence, said once, at about a quarter to twelve: **you cannot ask for a binder nobody has told you about. About forty people in this city have come through that door in nine years and every one of them asked for a binder they had been told about, and not one of them has ever asked for the one on that counter, and that is not because it is a secret, it is because nobody has ever said out loud that there is a second one.** Nothing was adopted, distributed, minuted or noticed; the tray is a drawer.
9. **Ch 199, the Monday of week sixty-seven — a bound register, nobody behind the table on a Thursday, one sentence with a count in it, and an authority retained by a person.** The register is on a table on the ground floor of a building in Crown Terrace, bound in a canvas cover, ruled into four columns, about nine hundred lines, the oldest from the week of week thirty-eight, no rope and no sign, and **the fourth column is a job title in every one of about nine hundred lines and has never once had a person's name in it. Nobody has ever been on that floor on a Thursday. A woman of thirty-four was behind it on this Monday with a cardigan and a cup of tea and a folder she had not opened**, and said she had not asked him the question she promised to ask and that she had noticed, and asked the second one: whether the register is going to be moved, saying before she asked that she is the frightened one and not him. **He answered neither way and gave the reason: the answer to it staying is not his and the answer to it going is a thing about a Tuesday, and two offices in that building learned that the answer to everything is yes until about four years ago. He said afterwards that it was not an answer and that he had given it anyway, because nobody had asked him anything for four years.** Then the one sentence with a count in it: **eight bundles in a yard behind that building on the Tuesday of week sixty-six, about nine hundred sheets into a skip, one of them a folded leaf that came out of a drawer because a man of about sixty unfolded it, carried out by a records clerk of forty-one on her own time and put on a high shelf in a room off a street in Lattice Ward; he has known that since the Wednesday; he is not going to ask for it; the number of people in this city who could tell him where it is is two and neither of them works for him.** And the reason for not asking, which he asked the woman of thirty-four to say in her own voice so that it would be a thing a room had heard: **a page that has left a building is a page somebody is carrying, and the second that an office asks for a page is a man in a corridor with a piece of paper to hand to a person, and then the person is findable, and he has spent fourteen years declining two things in writing and both of them were about not making a person findable, and he is not going to do it a third time in a fortnight.** A man of twenty-two on the landing at the foot of that stair, on a fault since eight in the morning, asked one question and it was the only one he asked and it was asked badly — does the requirement still stand — and was told that **it stands, it has stood the whole fourteen years, nothing in this city has ever withdrawn it, the only way it could be withdrawn is by somebody who can be asked whether they intend to, and there is no such person, and nobody has ever asked him whether he is it; and that he could be, and that he would decline it in writing, and that the fourth column of about nine hundred lines has held a job title instead of a name for fourteen years and has worked exactly as well as it looks.** Then, standing up, in a corridor, in the first person: **he is retaining emergency authority and is telling them so in a corridor on a Monday morning and is not going to soften it and is not going to put it in a form**, because a form of that can be produced by anybody who has ever seen one, and in about four years somebody is going to write down that the Continuity Office retained emergency authority on a Thursday, it will be true, and it will be the only true sentence anybody will ever have about him, and he would rather it was said by three people in a corridor on a Monday and that it is a man saying it and not a stamp. And the whole of his position in one sentence: **he has not seen nine requests, he has been told about a tray by a woman of forty-one, he has been asked nothing, he has agreed to nothing and refused nothing, and those two are not the same, and there is nobody in this city who has to make them the same, and he has not been asked to be like this in fourteen years.** He wrote down the number three, because there were three people on that floor, and no names.
10. **Ch 200, the Thursday of week sixty-seven — the close, and a card in a rail.** A rail of card slots along the end of the counter, about nine cards in it in nine years, a name on one side and a day on the other, and **the woman of forty-one who runs that room has never once taken one out and took one out on this Thursday with two fingers, slowly, which is the first time in nine years one has come out of that rail, and put it on the counter. She was not at work: she comes on Mondays, Wednesdays and Fridays, today is a Thursday, and she came on her day off to look at a folder she had put on a high shelf two days before, and said so before anybody asked.** Five people in that room — a registrar of forty-one on her day off, an officer of a fund of forty-four, a woman of forty-six with a trolley and nineteen years of a condition sheet signed at the bottom, a courier of twenty-one with three practices in three hands on a sheet of typing paper in a drawer and a worse answer he prefers, and a man of twenty-two — and none of the other four was asked to be there and none knew the other three. **He put the card in at about eleven in the morning, in his own hand, on purpose, and on the worst possible day in the week, when there was no counter staff and no bell within reach of anybody's hand and nobody in the room who had to do anything at all.** The front of it carries **a name in a hand that gets smaller towards the end of it and a week at the bottom left in the same hand, and nothing else: no title, no office, no second name, nobody's permission, anybody's agreement, and there is a woman of thirty-one with a key to a room in another building who did not agree to anything and is not on it.** He turned it over before he put it in, and there is nothing on the back of it. **It is renewable by him, in his own hand, without asking anybody, by writing the next week on it; it is removable by a clerk, because a clerk can take it out of a rail; nobody has to do a form about it and nobody has to be told; and the entire mechanism is a drawer with a rail on the end of it.** Then the reason, out loud, once, to four people: **a ledger nobody may read is a ledger a person owns, and a ledger a person owns dies with the person** — there is a folder on the high shelf of that trolley and about forty lines on it in a hand, and he did not read it and neither did two of the four people in that chamber, and the only copy in this city is on a shelf in a room anybody can walk into, and if it stays with one person then the day that person stops coming it is in a drawer in a flat and then it is a skip, and he would rather it was a card in a rail in a room off a street. **An officer of a fund of forty-four said: that does not help** — and then said what the sentence actually does, that it is a true sentence and it is also a sentence that puts his name on the front of a piece of paper in a building with a plate on the outside of it, and that in about four years the only thing anybody in this city will be able to say about the case is where to find him, and about eleven people a week are being taught that a release waits for a man in a lanyard — **and asked him to do the arithmetic out loud, and he did, in about nine seconds: about eleven people a week will go on waiting, about four of the eleven are not even on a rota, none of it is because of anything he did, he has traded the last thing he had, a man of fifty-eight in a corridor told him a term and a half ago that a person who says a thing is a person who can be asked whether he said it, he was right, he did it anyway, and the arithmetic is his and he is not going to hand it to a fund. Nobody said it was the right thing.** A woman of forty-six with a trolley said the only question anybody has ever asked her that her own sheet could not answer was about a corridor in the week of the fifty-two, and that nobody thanked her then and nobody has since. **Then the woman of forty-one said she is not handing it to anybody who has not filled in a slip, in either direction, that the card is going in the tray with the fifty-eight, and that she is not starting with a man who is good at rooms, and that was the only thing anybody said in that room that the man of forty-four could not improve.** Then the custody of the sheet, said out loud: **a high shelf in a room off a street in Lattice Ward, a plain folder, no card and no slip, put there on the Wednesday by a records clerk of forty-one on her own time; and the binder with the page stapled into it is a different object, is three shelves down, and is not to be put in a sentence with this one, because the day they are in the same sentence somebody will ask which one is a record and the answer will be whichever one a person is standing next to.** **A rack is not a rota, and nobody in that room said otherwise.** Then the bench: a docket with nine job numbers, a drawer with five things in it, four of them other people's sentences and one a letter with a reference whose closing line says it is not to be produced unless that office is asked first, a jacket on a chair with a photocopy of a photocopy in the inside pocket and the decision unmade after a term and a half, and the original in the fourth pocket of the tool roll with a fifth line nobody has ever asked him to fill in — **and the card that went into a rail is a third object and is about the size of a playing card and has a name on the front of it and nothing at all on the back, and the two were not confused today.** Then the rest of it, item by item: the binder with the stapled page and the public copy three shelves down; a building plan rolled in a drawer on a hook in a basement with a date in ink and room 2-07; a service plate in a contractor's typeface and about nine hundred of them in four buildings; a trolley with four sheets on it and pencil on the back of the week's own sheet in the space where the conditions go; a hundred and forty-one pounds in an envelope under a till with no name on it which cannot move, and which was on a coat hook in the spring and is under the till now because the hook was above the damp; a box in pencil on a counter with a woman's name in it which cannot come out, and a woman of thirty-one with a diary who is not thanked, and a clerk of fifty-three who ruled it and has not mentioned her since; a book in a back room with forty-four lines in it, the fortieth three years old, a hole in line forty-four, and no caller; a care worker of thirty-eight who will not sign her own words and whose name is at the bottom of a slip because a woman of forty-one wrote it and not her; a man of fifty-eight with a soft voice and a count; a man of fifty-one who has been rung, and who says the thing about the call, and whom nobody writes down; a courier of twenty-one with three practices in three hands and a worse answer he prefers, who was in that room and was not asked anything; **and about eleven people a week still going to be taught that a release waits for a man in a lanyard, and there is a rack now, and a rack is not a rota, and nobody in that room said otherwise.**

## What Movement V did not do, and what it leaves for Volume 05

**It did not identify an authorship and the name is not said; Sera Quill's other half is not given and does not hint at it. It did not name the licensor, the Crown Clause, Iona or Sorn, and the field is still blank and the holder of the postbox in Brin was not named and that counter closed nothing. It did not defeat Threadline, and a company is a service list, a contract and a man's own schedule, and Petar Vance is not a villain and is not the author. It did not have the Continuity Office agree to anything or refuse anything, and the man in the corridor was not defeated and was not turned. It did not put a title on anybody: the recognition is a card in a rail, in his own hand, with a week on it, and there is no office attached to it and nothing on the back of it. It did not grow the Exchange's book, nobody signed, no caller came, the book is at forty-four lines, and the next Exchange is the Wednesday of week sixty-eight, which is four weeks after Chapter 200 and outside this volume. It did not resolve the standing problem, invented no rule about who may be asked, said the standing problem in none of its ten chapters, and attached no number to it in any of them. It did not stage the relay, imply that it may be staged, ask the carrier again, or tell her she was right; there is no panel and no `* * *` marker anywhere. It did not touch the master and nobody decided anything about it, and nobody held out a hand for it. It did not open the box in pencil, did not put a second name in it, and did not correct it. It did not find a woman of thirty-four with a hip, did not give the nine words to anybody, did not say Asha Reed's sentence, did not put Rafi Pell in a room, did not find Steve Akers's nine words, did not name the holder of a box in Brin, did not attach a number to the standing problem, and does not use the word *pattern* anywhere. It did not use the words *we*, *our* or *us* in any of its ten chapters, which is checked mechanically. Talia Venn appears in the prose of Chapter 195 and of no other chapter, and Chapter 200 does not touch the relationship at all. It did not act on the volume's own finding: nine people have asked a question with their name at the bottom of it, nobody has answered any of them, the requirement is in print and cannot be withdrawn, a folder is on a high shelf in a room off a street, and there is no post in this city whose job it is to look at any of it.**

---

# VOLUME 04 — MOVEMENT IV (Chapters 181–190), "What People Are Owed"

**Volume 04 is open at Chapter 190. This block is the state at the end of Movement IV. It supersedes nothing in the `VOLUME 04 — MOVEMENT III` block below it and it inherits every line of it and of the `VOLUME 04 — MOVEMENT II` block and of the `VOLUME 04 — MOVEMENT I` block and of the `VOLUME 03 CLOSED` block. The load-book run is 184 to 193 and the batch's own account is `workspace/volume-04/batch-0004/SUMMARY.md`.**

## What Chapters 181–190 added to the record, in the order it is established

1. **Ch 181, the Monday of week 59 — he is asked to be somewhere else and goes, and the room does not stop.** A first-floor room off a street in Nine Bridges, a table, two chairs, a window that opens about four inches, and eleven names on a card in a plastic wallet that Sinead Corrigan, thirty-four, wrote herself and that is not a district list, in the same wallet as a file with her own name at the top of it that she put there on a Friday in the spring with her own hand in the box that says *completed by* and is not disciplined for. **She did not ask him to leave. She asked him to be somewhere else for eleven minutes and said out loud that she wanted to know which of the two she had done before he went through the door, and gave the reason: a person who is asked to leave has been asked to leave, and only one of those two is in a file anywhere.** The reason for the room, given to the card and not to him: with a man in it the answer to her one question is given in a voice people use on a man they have been told about, and in about four years the only record of the morning would be that a man of twenty-two was outside the door. He went out. **Four people went up and down that landing in eleven minutes and every one of them noticed where he was**: a man of thirty-four on nights going in for a shift, who used the one sentence he is allowed once a week, which is the one that says a room is a room; a woman of about thirty-eight in a district jacket who asked whether it was a queue, was told no, and sat on the step at the bottom and was still there when the door opened; a foreman of fifty-eight with a site diary and no reason to be on that landing, who said out loud that he was going to say nothing about what he had just seen and would rather have said it then than later; and a boy of nineteen carrying card offcuts up the wrong stair on purpose. **One of them left a folded sheet wedged in the fire door overnight so that the door would stay where it was last pushed, and it was gone in the morning and is not on anything.** Behind the door, eleven minutes, **five of the eleven asked: four yes and one no, six not asked, and she is going back on Monday. The no was not followed by a reason and was not offered a second question.** Her cost, at the door, is the method and not the answer: **those four may have said yes in a voice she cannot account for, she has six people she has not asked at all, and there is no form in this city that takes an account of either.** And the harder half, on a landing: there is a woman whose name is in a box in pencil four miles away, and the half of that where nobody did anything wrong, and she has not got the other half and has a car park to get to. He wrote nothing down and the reason is in his own book and is not integrity and he says so.
2. **Ch 182, the Tuesday of week 59 — a share ended by the person it belonged to, carried out of a building by somebody else's hands.** A floor share running since the spring, four people on it, all four asked for it, one of them after a fortnight because she wanted to be asked twice and was not. **The man of thirty-four ended it and said before anything else that he wanted it noticed first, because the only things this city believes are the things somebody asked for, and that it is the first time in about a term that something in this case has been his.** There is no form and the reason there is no form is that a form would want a reason and he has not got a reason, he has a man who is going to have to stand on his own floor at ten past four in the morning and say a thing four times. **It went on the back of a work card in biro, forty words, read back to him, and he said those were the words he wanted and not the words he would have used, because a pen is a decision and a biro is a note.** The warning he was given before he carried it out is the finding and it is on the card: **he has spent a term teaching four people that a release waits for a man, ending the share does not un-teach that, and if he does it properly then in about a week one of the four will not do the fifth step at all, because a step a man ends for you is a step that waits for a man.** **Marguerite Okpara, twenty-nine, six years and about nine hundred lifts, on a Friday list because her employer pays for it, came up the stairs with a tool bag for a noise in the lift and was not asked for anything.** She wrote the card, put it in a plain envelope with nothing on it, and carried it out through a fire door into a stairwell at about ten past two; nobody in that building saw it leave. She said yes **because nobody asked her to**, and said that if he thanked her then in about four years somebody would ask her whether she would do it again and she would have to think about a favour, and she would rather it was a lift. **Her cost, in her own words: sixteen Fridays on the back of her own card in the same wallet, never totalled because a total is a number somebody can quote, and both of those cards have been in that wallet since the spring and if a person opens it in about four years they have got both at once.** He said the thing he had been keeping, that he has said no twice to being the word on his own floor, that the second no had no reason on it at all, and that he has wanted for about a fortnight for somebody to notice that the no came first both times. Nobody noticed and he noticed it himself in a corridor and said he was aware of how small that is. **Neither was thanked.** At ten past four the following morning he stood on his own floor and said it four times.
3. **Ch 183, the Thursday of week 59 — Doreen Abbiss, the name in the pencil box, and the count of one in about two hundred.** The counter on the ground floor of a building in Bower Street, about two hundred district withdrawal pages in four stacks with a rubber band on each and no collection since the spring, four fields, one of them a tick, and on the top page of the first stack a box about the size of a stamp ruled in pencil under the fourth field with three words over it in a clerk's hand. **The three words are *who ended it*.** Petra Lindqvist, fifty-three, nineteen years at that counter, is the only person alive who knows that the line at the foot of the district instruction and the box on that page are the same document. **Doreen Abbiss, thirty-eight, a night care worker with six years of nights, had never been in that building and came in at about eleven on the Thursday because the daytime is the only part of her week that is hers and she said in the spring that the next person this city needs is her.** She brought the page she has carried since before the spring in a plastic bag. The two sheets were put nine inches apart on the marble and read out twice: **the same form and not the same sheet, one of them with a telephone number at the top and a man on the Friday and her underneath it, the other with the box on it and the four fields and a date and a time, and neither of them has anybody's name at the top.** **A SECOND NAME WAS NOT PUT IN THE BOX AND THE BOX WAS NOT TOUCHED, and the reason is a reason.** Ianthe Roke, thirty-one, with a key, a diary and a list, who states her standing before she says anything else and has been told twice this term that she has none: **the box is a box nobody has ever offered to anybody, the only reason the first name is in it is that she rang a counter and a woman answered and gave it to a stranger in about nine seconds, and the moment she asks a second person she is a person who asks people for names, and in about four years she is a woman at a counter with a diary and a list who does that, and that is not a post, it is a habit of hers, and a habit of hers is the most dangerous thing in this case because she is the only one holding it. If somebody else asked there would be two, and then nine, and the ninth would be a woman with a clipboard in a district saying she was only doing her job.** She put the pen down flat. What she was given instead is a second sheet, in pencil, at the counter, with a date, a time, a place, what was shared, who with, a line in a clerk's hand saying the name was given by the person and written by another, and her own name at the bottom in her own hand — **and she said, unprompted, that it is true, that it is not a correction, that it is not going to help with the one in the stack, that she is keeping it, and that she is not confusing the two to be polite.** The count, which is the finding and took the clerk four stacks: **one name in about two hundred pages.** And what the person it is for said at that counter, to nobody in particular, unprompted: **nobody took it from her, she gave it, another person wrote it, both of those were the right thing to do at the time, and if she does not say it at a counter then in about four years the only thing anybody knows about her is that a name of hers is in a box.** She went to catch a bus. Nobody took it from her. Nobody wrote anything down. **Marek Senn was four miles away on a docket for the whole of it and was not told and did not ask**, and the reason he did not ask is that he has known since the Wednesday of week forty-seven that the only reason a name is in that box is that he stood in a corridor outside the room and did not knock.
4. **Ch 184, the Friday of week 59 — the first Friday rota, and the second of the two public method disagreements, spent and the last of the two in this volume.** Eleven jobs, nine in date, the word at nine a minute by half past seven and at ten by eleven, and no question asked about a release for the seventh Friday running. **Three of four locations on a docket of his own are unchanged and the fourth, a cut in the wall inside a stair window the width of two fingers painted over, is under two more coats of paint, done on the Tuesday in about half an hour by a man on a ladder who did not know — which is what a man of thirty-eight in a district jacket said in a corridor four weeks ago, and a job sheet with four locations on it went into a district system and nobody read a job sheet.** Then the reading room in Lattice Ward: a counter, four drawers with about four hundred cards in them, a bell that is a push, and **thirty-one slips in the tray, counted twice that week and the number said out loud twice, the second time slower, and not one of them is about a box behind about forty binder read-outs in nine years.** **The disagreement is between Wendla Ossick, forty-one, and Corin Vasska, forty-four, in front of four other people, about method and not about either of them.** His: a rule that can be changed is a draft, four sections and three lines, one funded, one committee meeting that is not going to happen again, about eleven thousand premises paying, not one word improvable, and the reason about eleven people a week are unchanged is the copy nobody opens. Hers: a slip is a piece of paper and a person, four questions in about six weeks that were not about a box and no rule for answering a question, about two hundred people through that door since the spring and no idea what any of them took out of it, and then the difference: **his sentence cannot be asked anything, and that is the whole difference and she did not mean it as a compliment to hers.** Where the argument actually is, in his words: **one rule is true on a Tuesday in a building with nobody in it, and the other is also true on a Tuesday in a building with nobody in it, and it is a different rule, and there are about eleven thousand of one and one of the other, and the only thing they have in common is that two people were standing in the room.** She said that was not an answer and he said it was not an answer and it was the whole of what he had and he had had a fortnight. **Neither improved the other's, neither mentions Volume 03, no scene, no declaration, no kiss, and the words *we*, *our* and *us* appear nowhere in the chapter or in any of the other nine.** The residue: a man of thirty-four on nights asked for one of them to be wrong for the second time in a fortnight, said he is not going to ask a third time, and gave the reason — **the asking is the part he would like back.** Nobody offered him anything, and he went down the stairs with nothing, because he used one sentence on the Monday and the other on the Tuesday and is allowed each once a week; **this is the first Friday in four terms on which he has had nothing to say and he said so to nobody.** The man of twenty-two was in that room for nine minutes and said nothing at all, and the reason is in the chapter.
5. **Ch 185, the Wednesday of week 60 — the Exchange, the book at forty-four lines, and the rule given in one go.** A back room, shutters up, a tin with no lock, four columns and forty-four lines, four minutes an item, no vote for anybody, and the book shut since the Wednesday of week forty-eight, which is the fourth sitting running. **The rule, in one go, not shortened: a book of what a caller said cannot be written by the people who did not call. It is older than her and it was there before the tin and it is not a mood and she is not going to be asked whether it is a mood.** The fortieth line was written three years ago by a man who rang and said nine words and signed his own name under them. Line forty-four has a hole in it where a woman of sixty-eight in bay four should be and she is never going to be written down. **Twenty-nine telephone calls, said out loud at about half past nine: nine in the spring, twenty on the Wednesday of the fifty-sixth, twenty-nine on this Wednesday, and she has said yes twenty-nine times and did not know what she was agreeing to for the first eleven and knows now. Three of the twenty-nine were in the room, and she has not asked the other twenty-six why they have not come and is not going to, because the only way to find out is by asking, and asking makes her a person who rings people to come to a room.** A man of sixty-ish asked for four minutes and opened by saying he had not telephoned, that he knew what the book is for, and that he wanted somebody to notice that he had noticed; he was told he was noticed and that there is nothing on the front of the table that will say he was here. **His four minutes were about a card with four correct steps and a fifth line going round three buildings where he works, and about the sentence he has been saying for about a fortnight in two corridors and a canteen: a thing can be safe because a lot of people have been given it. He said he had never said it into a machine, and was told nobody can tell him why that makes him different from a man being handed leaflets.** Then her cost, said to somebody who put it to her that she had been talked into it: **she was talked into it by about twenty-nine telephone calls, one of which was in the spring from a woman who was frightened, and there is nothing in this city that can find that out except her.** The rule came round a second time when a woman of thirty-four asked what a caller is, and the other half of it was admitted in front of eleven people: **she has not got a rule for the other half and is not going to invent one at eleven at night, because the second it sounds like a rule somebody in about four years can point at a rule she made up on a Wednesday because nine people were in a room and liked her.** The close, said standing up, to nobody: **that the emptiness of the book and the fact that a person may speak here without it being used against them are the same rule, one sentence, older than the tin, and that the day it is removed for the other reason this room was a service before it was a room and will have been one for about four years with nobody noticing the day.** Nobody signed, the tin went back empty, and the fifth chair was taken twice by a man who was not a caller and offered no reason.
6. **Ch 186, the Thursday of week 60 — a person is removed from a room, and the instrument is a diary and the holder is the person who put him in it.** An exchange room up a flight of stairs in the Bower Street building that is not on the first floor according to anything anybody has written down, a woman of thirty-one with a key, a diary and a list and nothing else at all, and a man of thirty-four on nights who has been in it nine times since the Monday of week fifty-eight and on eight of them nothing was happening. **She did it in a corridor, not in the room, and said afterwards that was a decision and not an accident, and told him she had written the nine days in the diary because there is nowhere else to write them, and would rather the first version of it had a diary in it. One week. Not a ban, not a complaint, not a district.** The reason, given before it was asked for: **she started leaving the door on the latch a fortnight earlier because he was in there and did not ask herself who the latch was for; an unoccupied room with a person in it puts a person in a record; and she is the only person who writes that room down and had entered him nine times without putting one question to him about any of it. There is no form for it and she looked, and the honest reason there is no form is that a form would want a reason and she has got a fact.** The half that costs her: **after the week he can come in if he asks and she will say yes and will not make him ask twice, and that version is the one where she is a woman with a key, and in about four years a woman with a key is a post, and there is no post in this city whose job it is to decide who goes in a room.** She went down and told the clerk and said afterwards she should not have, and Petra Lindqvist said **that in nineteen years she has never been asked to know anything and is not going to start, and that being told a woman in that building has turned a key on somebody makes her a person who knows, and a person who knows is the beginning of a record, and she has spent nineteen years making sure that counter does not have one; and that a diary is a diary, and if anybody ever treats it as a register then there is a woman in that building who has written nine lines about a man in a corridor and will not know why she did it.** He named his own cost in a corridor: five places he has, none of them his, every one given by a person. She refused to tell him about a piece of paper concerning the key because he has a floor at ten past four in the morning and she is not going to be the reason he has to carry a person he has never met. He asked one question — is the counter still open — and was told that it is a woman of fifty-three and four stacks every morning, and said **then he has lost a room and has not lost a floor.** Nobody stopped it and nobody was asked to.
7. **Ch 187, the Friday of week 60 — the tenth of the eleven, four weeks after the ninth, and the standing problem is not said.** Thirty-one chairs and twenty-one people in the same room in Crown Terrace, a stack of about four hundred leaflets with a fee, a session title and five jobs on the front, two of them his, chosen by a system in a company out of two job sheets in the week of the thirty-fourth. **The third line was read as the third line and about nine people did not hear the difference, which is the fourth sitting running, and the first session of the series had nine chairs and a man in each of them.** The officer of the fund put the arithmetic on the table himself: the lowest attendance in four years, never minuted, and the reason is a Friday at half past nine at night and about eleven thousand premises paying a levy that closes at six; when somebody said somebody should tell them the hours, **he said the hours are on the front of the leaflet, and the five jobs are on the front of the same sheet, and he reads that sheet aloud every Friday because a system put two of those jobs in his hand and nobody has ever been able to find out what it wanted.** **He was in the fourth row and he did not stand up, and the reason is in the chapter: he has said the standing problem in that room twice, once standing and once four minutes later when asked, it did not get better the second time, and he did not go home and find a better version. Nobody asked him anything and a man of fifty-two in the second row looked at him once at about ten and then looked at the front.** Then **the second removal of this movement, and it is the officer of the fund who makes it**: at eleven, with the room half empty and the microphone still on its stand and nobody having asked for it to be switched off, which is a thing nobody in this city is able to do, **he said he is not going to read the five jobs out loud an eleventh time, and gave the reason: he has read that sheet out loud ten Fridays, about eleven people a week are being taught to wait for a man, and not one of those eleven has ever heard him read it. Nobody stopped him and nobody asked him to.** A man of fifty-two, the depot manager, stood up and said out loud once, in a room with a microphone on it, **that he has stopped asking whether anybody is going to offer to take the wording of a condition to somebody who could change it, having been offered twice and said no twice, and that he cannot tell from inside the room whether that is a decision or whether he is tired, and that he is not going to say which** — and his reason for saying it is that a man of forty-four has just read a thing out loud ten times and he would like the other half of it said in a room at least once this term. Nobody wrote it down. **The woman of twenty-six, from her seat, having not stood up: eleven letters, five answers written, none sent, the fifth better than the first four, nine to go, and it is not a decision but a Tuesday and a pen.** A specification runs to four sections and three lines, the third funded, premises in five figures, a committee of about nine that met once in the week of the thirty-seventh and has not met since and is not going to.
8. **Ch 188, the Monday of week 61 — everybody pays something and not one of them is paid back.** A canteen off the service road off the depot, open at seven, the woman who runs it at a hospital appointment, the till and the urn done by a man of twenty-two on grade two since a quarter past six, and a hatch through which about a hundred and forty leaflets of the spring went in about an hour. **Five people came in between ten past seven and about ten, one at a time, and none of them knew each other, and every one of them told a man who makes tea the exact amount a thing in this city had cost them, and not one of them asked him for anything.** A boy of nineteen who paid a pound for a tea of about sixty so as not to have to count change, who had said no on Friday to being a name at the top of a district sheet about cards, and who named the cost: **about four hundred pieces of paper carry his handwriting and he is on none of them with his name, that was the right answer and it is worse than the printer's, and a no that is written down is a thing somebody can ask you about, and he had not known that was a thing he could have; and he can take the no back in a week and will then have to have a reason.** A man of thirty-four on nights, up since half three, who had used his nine words in biro on the back of one of about a hundred and forty leaflets the man of twenty-two printed in the spring for a company and did not write, unasked, on Friday, and who said he had come in to tell a man making tea about it because it is the only true thing he has and he is not going to give it to the four, **and named the thing he cannot undo: it is in the bin behind that door and it will be in a skip by Wednesday, and it is the only place in this city where a sentence of his is written down, and he can take it out and write it on a different piece of paper, and in about four years there will be a skip in this city with a sentence of his in it and nobody will ever be able to say whether that was a thing he did or a thing that happened.** A woman of twenty-six with a stamped envelope she did not put down the post, who asked for a witness and specified the amount — a man behind a hatch at about nine in the morning, because eleven people heard her in a room with a microphone on it and she wanted one person to see her not do a thing — and who said the reason twice unchanged: **there is nothing on the paper to answer them with, that was true in the spring and is true now, and it is not a decision.** A printer of fifty-eight with a plastic wallet, a bag of card and a quarterly return, who put four pounds down for four teas and did not take the change and said in a canteen what she has said in about four rooms: **that she is still the person who decides what a card is for, that nobody has offered her a way of not being that, that she has not asked for one and is not going to ask a man behind a hatch, and that a hundred and forty-one pounds has been on a coat hook since the spring in an envelope she has looked at about nine times and has not opened, because opening it is a decision.** Five teas, no change taken, five numbers written down and no names.
9. **Ch 189, the Wednesday of week 61 — Peter Amado, about fifty-one, is still coming, and the microphone question is answered by nobody.** A market in Saltmarket on a Wednesday, a trestle at the end of the middle aisle put up by a company and not staffed as a post, a leaflet, a name at the bottom of it, a small amplifier and a microphone on a stand with a lead in it, about forty people past it in an hour, four years. **A man of twenty-two carried the trestle in at half past seven because he has a van on a Friday and nobody asked him on the Wednesday, and stood at the end of it for two and a half hours and was not a speaker and was not introduced as one.** At half past ten the man of fifty-one arrived with a card he did not put down, said he had one item and four minutes, said he had asked for the microphone to be switched off and had asked twice before and would ask a third, **and asked for the answer to be a yes or a no this time rather than nothing.** It was not switched off. At eleven he put his hand on the stand the way a man does when he is about to speak into something, the amplifier was making the noise it makes when nothing is going into it, and eleven people stopped in the aisle. He said the funded sentence, that a practice in this city belongs to nobody, and then said the other one, fast, and nobody in reach had a stop: **that he is the man being paid to say it, that he has said a true thing six times in this city and has never once been asked where he got it, and that he would like it noticed that a man of fifty-one has just said a sentence he does not believe into a microphone he asked twice to have switched off, and that nobody in this market has answered the question about whether it was off.** Afterwards a man of about sixty standing four feet away could not tell, a woman of about thirty said the same, and the woman at the trestle, thirty-five, said **the switch is on the amplifier and not on the stand, that she had not known it was there until a fortnight ago, that she has asked once herself and been given nothing, and that she did not switch it off because the last two times somebody else did and she did not do it, and that she has not worked out whether that makes her the one who left it on.** A man of forty-four told a man of twenty-two on the far side of the trestle in about nine seconds: **the asking was in front of people both times, the first time somebody switched it off and nobody wrote it down, so the only provable thing about that microphone is that a man asked for it to be switched off; the asking is the part that lasts; and in about four years the only quotable sentence of that morning will be a man of fifty-one asking for a microphone to be switched off in a market** — and why he was telling the man of twenty-two and not the man of fifty-one: **because the man of fifty-one is being paid and he is not going to be a man who says it to him for nothing, and because about a fortnight ago a man of fifty-two stood up in a room and said out loud that he had stopped asking to be checked, and he has not been able to sit on it since.** **It was not told to him.** The man of fifty-one looked at the man of twenty-two twice in about four minutes and did not ask him anything, which is recorded because it has never happened in a market in this city. **Nobody offered him a way out, there is not one in this city, and there has not been one since the week of the twenty-four.** The woman at the trestle told him the sessions finish after this one and the autumn fee schedule had not come down, said that is not a way out, said she had said no to about nine people this month who wanted something out of a Friday night, and said he was the first who had not asked her for anything.
10. **Ch 190, the Thursday of week 61 — the close, and one at a time.** He counts exits before entering a room, and on the Thursday morning of week sixty-one he counted three doorways, two in one building in Bower Street and one in a different street in Nine Bridges, and he was in a corridor for one of them and on a step for another and at a counter for the third, and he did not ask for any of the three. **The first: the counter, a woman of fifty-three, and the same true thing a third time five weeks after the second, with no better version of it in five weeks, and then told that he is here for the other half of it and there is nothing to have.** The second: a shut door up a flight of stairs, and a woman of thirty-one with a diary with nine lines in it who has thought about tearing them out about four times and is not going to, **because the second she tears them out they are gone and the first time anybody asks her about that week she has got nothing to read out** — and who refused to say it out loud instead, to about four people once and then to whoever they tell, because she has watched a woman of thirty-four have her file put in a corridor in this city and she is not doing it for a week. The third: a door in Nine Bridges at half past two, a work card handed to the man with the van, and a man of thirty-four who has no reason why it is going to happen in a fortnight and has said the word fortnight in a corridor to two people in about a month and has still not got a reason, **and who says the not getting a reason is the only thing he has got, and that you cannot write it down.** And then the finding: **a share can be ended by the person it belonged to and can be put back the same way, by the same person, on his own, whenever he decides to. A room can be closed by the person who holds the key and can be put back the same way, by the same person, with the same key, and it is the only one of the three where the person who shut it will still be there in about four years. A name, in pencil, on the top page of the first of four stacks, cannot be taken out by anybody — not by the clerk who ruled the box, not by the woman who wrote it, not by the woman it is for, and not by a district on its own cycle — because a thing that was never in a system has nothing in it to correct, and the three correct actions that put it there are the reason there is nothing to correct.** And why nobody stopped any of them, which is not a failure of nerve and nobody to blame: **a share can only be ended by a person in it, only a key can shut a room, only the person who answered a question can answer it, in each case the person who did it was the person standing there, and stopping them would have meant becoming the person who does it, and there is no such person in this city and there is not going to be one. The three people who put those three things there are a man of thirty-four who asked for one and was answered, a woman of thirty-one who has a key, and a woman of thirty-one who rang a counter in the spring and was answered by a stranger. None of them is a company and none of them is a district, and two of the three cannot be taken back, and there is not one item on a list of about nine thousand that says any of this, because nobody made the list.**

## What Movement IV did not do, and what it leaves for Movement V

**It did not identify an authorship and the name is not said, and Sera Quill is not in the movement and her other half is not given and does not hint at it. It did not name the licensor, the Crown Clause, Iona or Sorn, and the field is still blank. It did not defeat Threadline, and a company is a service list and a contract and a man's own schedule and a trestle table. It did not have the Continuity Office agree to anything, and the man on the floor above was not referred to and did not appear. It did not put a card in a rack and no title is attached to anybody; the recognition is Chapters 198 to 200. It did not grow the Exchange's book, nobody signed, no caller signed, and the next Exchange is the Wednesday of week sixty-four, which is the last one in this volume. It did not resolve the standing problem, invented no rule about who may be asked, said the standing problem in none of its ten chapters, and attached no number to it in any of them. It did not stage the relay or imply that it may be staged, the carrier was not asked again, and there is no panel and no `* * *` marker. It did not touch the master and nobody decided anything about it, and nobody has held out a hand for it. It did not open the box in pencil, did not put a second name in it, and did not correct it. It did not find a woman of thirty-four with a hip, did not give the nine words to anybody, did not say Asha Reed's sentence, did not put Rafi Pell in a room, did not find Steve Akers's nine words, did not name the holder of a box in Brin, and does not use the word *pattern* anywhere. It did not touch the relationship at all: Talia Venn appears in the prose of none of the ten chapters and is named in none of them. It did not act on the finding, and the finding is that three people in this city can remove a person from a share, a name and a room, one at a time, and that the same three people can put a share and a room back, and that a name in pencil has neither a party nor a key, and that nobody has done it and there is nothing on any list that says they can.**

**A FLAG ON THE SIZE OF THIS FILE.** The growth is Movement IV's ten new dated facts and its numbered canon block, not bookkeeping. **A Volume 04 batch 0005 writer should compact the three Movement repair-pass blocks, whose full record is `reviews/volume-04-batch-0001.md`, and should not add bookkeeping of any kind to this file.**
