# VOLUME 04 — MOVEMENT III (Chapters 171–180), "A List And A Lock"

**Volume 04 is open at Chapter 180. This block is the state at the end of Movement III. It supersedes nothing in the `VOLUME 04 — MOVEMENT II` block below it and it inherits every line of it and of the `VOLUME 04 — MOVEMENT I` block and of the `VOLUME 03 CLOSED` block. The load-book run is 174 to 183 and the batch's own account is `workspace/volume-04/batch-0003/SUMMARY.md`.**

## What Chapters 171–180 added to the record, in the order it is established

1. **Ch 171, the Monday of week 55 — there is no person in this city with authority over a building's own records, and the three who could be have never been asked.** Petar Vance, forty-four, a field service engineer, on a landing with a roll of plates and a caulking gun: a plate is a service plate, the number is a service number, the schedule of doors is not his, and the company's engineer has twice told a foreman that the schedule is the building's, which he called the only sentence in his trade that is both true and useless. **The freehold of two of the four sits with a landlord on a schedule of title with a letterhead, a solicitor and no hours, who has been inside one of them twice and both times it was a plate; one is held by a management company that is not the freeholder; the fourth has the basement in it and two keys, one of them a foreman's that has not been turned.** He gives the number — about nine hundred doors — and says he cannot change nine hundred of them because a district nurse wants one room, and asks for the number not to be given to anybody else, and asks not to be thanked, and says being thanked would give him something to think about for the rest of the week. **He also gives the fault ticket of the week of the thirty-seventh: read against the wall, answered with the number already on the wall in the typeface already on the wall, closed, correct, and no version of the world in which a correct ticket fixes a number.** Ines Kolar, forty-six, with a trolley, is asked what she thinks a sheet is for, because that is the second question and the first one takes her three weeks: **it is for a Thursday, a thousand of them in a ring file in a cupboard on a ground floor in another part of this city, two thousand in about four years, and nobody has ever opened that cupboard.** She tells him to put the docket down because he is holding it like a man about to open something; he puts it down on the floor; she refuses the rest of it and says she is not going to be the reason it becomes a document, and does forty-one doors. **The chapter's finding, in narration: a schedule of doors, a schedule of title, and a line at the bottom of a form, held by three people, and not one of the three has ever been asked to decide anything.**
2. **Ch 172, the Tuesday of week 55 — Series milestone 3's public half, paid once, in front of nine men, and neither of them says *we*.** A gate off the Meridian corridor, a prefab office, a heater, a kettle, a packer lamp that has gone for the second time since the spring with the ballast the ballast. **A woman of twenty-four is in the corridor for her own round and says the thing he has built is the only version of a person holding a rule she has seen work in four years of telling rooms it was possible, and that he built it by refusing every thing that would have made it a job. He says the difference is who pays, and nobody pays, and the day somebody is paid to stand behind that counter it is not a rule, it is a post, and a post can be given things to do.** Both are right; neither improves the other's; neither mentions Volume 03; no scene, no declaration, no kiss. **She gives him a ward and a person and asks what his version costs and who signs it, and he gives the rota, and the number on the back of his own card, and says he put it there on purpose in a room with nine people in it. She says the honest position is that there is one of him.** She names her own residue: in about four years there is going to be a room where somebody can say the name of the woman who said that, with a hundred and forty leaflets on the table, and she is not going to do it a third time. **The residue is carried by a man of thirty-four who works nights, who came four hours before he starts, and who has four people on his own floor taught every Friday that a release waits for a man, and two true sentences he may not use twice in a week, one that says a man is a man and one that says a room is a room, and who asked one of them to be wrong and was not given that by either. Nobody offered him a third sentence.** Talia Venn is named in the load book and not in the prose.
3. **Ch 173, the Thursday of week 55 — the plans are open and the first person down those stairs was not Marek Senn.** A light out on a second landing: a district service, a lead repairer and a repairer on a Friday exception. **A foreman of fifty-eight says, before anybody asks, that he has got a key to that building and has not turned it and is not going to be the one who turns it, and that he said the same thing in a reading room a fortnight ago and the thinking since has not improved it. Ines Kolar says no in nine seconds, and gives two reasons: she did not put it there, and if there is a sheet with a date on it there is no column for that either, and in nineteen years nobody has ever asked her to find anything.** A woman of forty-five comes up the service stairs with a braid of tools because the light has a ballast in it, and says the next part before she is asked, and says it to him and not to the foreman: **a basement is a room and she goes into rooms, she has not asked why and he is not going to tell her why, and she wants that clear before she goes.** She says the cost out loud on a landing with three people in it: **in about four years somebody can say a woman of forty-five went into a basement in this city on a Thursday and did it for a man she has said no to seven times, and there is no version of that that stays a fact about a drawer, and if it becomes a thing where the woman who went down is a person in this case she will know inside a month.** She leaves the braid on the top step and goes down for six minutes and nobody goes with her. The drawer is not locked and never has been: **one roll of plans with a date in ink on the outside, nineteen years old, the ink older than any manufacturer's line on any plate in the building, the service contract four years old. The room is 2-07 on the plan and 2-14 on the door and both are correct about themselves, and the two records have not been in the same room since before a service contract existed.** It went back in the drawer. She came up and gave twenty words and would not be asked what she thought of it, and said she has been handed a piece of paper twice in eleven years and both times it was in a corridor with nine men in it and she is not doing it a third time in a basement.
4. **Ch 174, the Friday of week 55 — the first Friday rota, and the first question anybody has put to him about a return path in four terms.** Eleven jobs, nine in date, the word at nine and then at ten, and nobody asked him where a release was going to come from, which is five Fridays. **A man of thirty-eight in a district jacket has been standing on a second landing for an hour: one way off it after six o'clock and the way off it is through number twelve, and the woman in number twelve is on her feet all day and is not a way out of a building.** He answers as a repairer, on a fault on a docket. **Four things and one that is not: the landing door is not an exit but a way of being in a building; a stair going up is a route and not an exit; a fire door at the top with a bar on it; a second stair that does not exist and which everybody in the block believes in; and a cut in the wall inside a stair window, painted over, the width of two fingers, the same age as the frame.** He says he is not able to say whether any of it is legal and that if there is something on a form it is not his form. **The cost, named by the man of thirty-eight: a job sheet with four locations on it goes into a district system and nobody reads a job sheet, and in about four years the plate on a door in this city is still the wrong number and the groove is under two more coats of paint, and he has nine correct actions this month and would like to be told the day one of them becomes a different thing.** He is then asked to say it again, names a room with a microphone on it, and is refused: **a room with a microphone is the same sentence with the volume turned up, the fourth condition is quoted entire, the landing is none of the four verbs in it because it is a landing and a fault on a docket, and the day he is asked to say it twice, or to say it to somebody who was not on the landing, is a different thing.** He wrote the four locations on a docket of his own and put them on nothing.
5. **Ch 175, the Wednesday of week 56 — the midpoint, and the Exchange.** A slip comes off a card in a reading room in Lattice Ward with Sera Quill's name on it, the first such slip in nine years; the card says *Marrow Falls, emergency protocol, working copy, four volumes*. **The box comes off the top of a trolley whose third castor has been bad since before the registrar came, so the whole thing moves about an inch and four inches of the third shelf down comes with it — and the Civic Compact is on that shelf, in print, in the original words, not in a box, with no card and needing no slip, three shelves down for nine years, while about forty people in nine years have asked for a binder in a box and had every card put back.** The working copy has a page stapled in over the printed fourth line; **the staple went into the sheet before the sheet was printed, because working copies are run off again, and it is older than the ink around it, and there are no initials, no date and no name on the page anywhere.** The printed line: *4. Every technique must show its exit, in a place the participant can find it, before the technique begins.* The stapled page: *4. Where a technique does not answer within the time set for it, the last approved civic sequence for that practice shall stand in its place, and a participant who does not answer shall be treated as not having withheld.* **Nobody has broken a rule. The requirement is in print in the original words in a public room and has been for fourteen years, and the illegality is a city operating outside a live requirement because the only copy anybody has ever opened is the one in the box.** A woman of forty-five reads the printed line first and the stapled page twice and puts her hand flat on the counter away from the page; nobody asks her how she knew, because the man of twenty-two has known for a term and a half that the answer to a question of that shape is a sentence that cannot be unsaid in a room with a counter in it. **She says the same nine words and gives the same reason in the same words, and the name is not said and the name is not in this volume.** Then, unasked, she asks for the other one to be taken down. **The registrar says she has not read it in nine years and gives the reason — it is a big book, it is heavy, there is a bell on this counter, it is nine years of her own habit, and nobody ever asked her about it — and then the volume's sentence: a rule is only as real as the copy people open, and she has been behind that counter with the rule three shelves under her elbow, and the person in this city who has said the most about that rule in a term and a half is a man of twenty-two who has never once been in that room when it was open.** She ruled no column and will not, and the record is a sentence said once out loud to two people and no one else: **a woman of forty-five and a man of twenty-two had two documents out of this room at the same time this morning. It is in a head in a cardigan on a Wednesday, and in about four years one of the two will have forgotten it and the other will not, and that is the whole of the arrangement she has.** The two lay nine inches apart on a marble top for about eleven minutes and went back four inches apart. **And at eleven that night the Exchange: ten people, the tin, forty-four lines, shut since the Wednesday of week forty-eight, the rule given in one go and it is a rule and not a mood — a book of what a caller said cannot be written by the people who did not call. A first question, which is whether anybody has ever read it, and the answer is that nobody has in nine years, and Bea Nunn said that a book with forty-four lines in it is the most honest object in this city because it is nearly empty, and that every week it stays nearly empty is a week somebody sat in a room and decided not to say a thing in order to be in the room. Nine more telephone calls since the Wednesday of week fifty-two, which makes about twenty, and she has said yes twenty times and now knows what she was agreeing to. Nobody called and nobody signed.**
6. **Ch 176, the Thursday of week 56 — the volume's argument turned on its own hero, and a clerk who obeyed a line for nineteen years and broke it once.** A counter in Bower Street, about two hundred district withdrawal pages in four stacks with a rubber band on each, four fields and a tick, a clerk of fifty-three who has been there nineteen years and has never once been asked to make a decision, and about nine minutes available. **A district instruction turned round and not handed over, with a line printed at the foot in a type size designed to be read once: *no local additions*.** He asks the second question — what do you think of that line — and nobody has ever asked her that, and the answer is: the line is there for the day somebody wants to be able to say that nothing was added, and nobody has wanted that once in nineteen years, and about four hundred people a week come past that counter, and the reason is that the line is at the foot and the foot of a page is not read by anybody who has read the top. **She calls it a door that has not been locked since the year she started, and says she is the only person standing next to it and has never once been asked whether it is a door.** Then she says she broke it: **in the week of week forty-five a woman of thirty-one with a diary came down and stated her standing twice and asked for a box, and there was no box, and the clerk ruled one in pencil under the fourth field at the size of a stamp, wrote three words over the top of it in her own hand, and pushed the page four inches along the counter away from herself. That is a local addition and she has told nobody since, and had about eleven weeks to work out what to do about it and there is nothing to do about it.** She says the whole nineteen years came apart in about four seconds when he asked a different question. **She names the cost in one go, at greater length than she means to: in about four years nobody will be able to tell that the line at the foot of that instruction and the box on that page are the same document, and she is the only person alive who knows, and he is the only other one, and he came in with a docket.** She says she has never once written down a person, so either he writes this and she has handed nineteen years to a man with a lanyard, or he does not and she has told the first person who has ever asked her, and she cannot tell him which she wants. **She says the name in the box is not hers to take out, the name at the bottom of the page belongs to the woman of thirty-one who is on the first floor and comes down on Thursdays and did not come, the page goes back in the stack with a rubber band on it, and there is no way in this city for a thing that was never in a system to be put right and she is not going to invent one.** He says he has not written it down and is not going to and is not going to call that integrity. **She says: that is a man who has been standing next to a door since the spring and has decided that standing next to it is the same as having to open it, and I have been standing next to the same kind of door for nineteen years, and the difference between the two of us is not the door and it is not the character and it is that nobody has ever asked either of us which one we are.** She adds that whoever is behind that counter in about four years will be a person of about thirty who has never met her and there will still be no reason for anybody to want to say that nothing was added.
7. **Ch 177, the Friday of week 56 — the ninth of the eleven, the standing problem a seventh time, and the second half of it for the first time.** The third line is read as the third line and nine people do not hear the difference, for the third sitting running; the officer of the fund says he is not going to say it a fourth time in a better voice. **He stands at half past nine and puts the fourth condition into the first person for the second time in four weeks, and the six reasons, and says he is not going to say them a third time in a better sentence because there is not one, and the reason there is no rule about who may be asked is that a rule about who may be asked is a rule about who is findable.** Then the second condition, which is on no sheet and which he has never said in a room: **he is the only person in this city who can say what he heard in a corridor and cannot hear a corridor; nothing is wrong with him that a doctor has found and it is not going to be fixed this term; in a fortnight he talked over three people in three rooms and failed to hear one; and the one he failed to hear is the reason a thing in this case was not noticed, and it was not noticed for a fortnight.** About four people in the room had no shape for it. **A district nurse of thirty-four then stands up and gives her name and where she works into a room with a microphone on a stand, and says there are four people in this city with a file at the top of it that they put there themselves and she has met three of them and not the fourth, and the condition on his share is the only reason the four of them are not a group. She names her own cost — a file that was already hers, a man in a district jacket who heard her in the spring and put it in a corridor in Nine Bridges and did not do it on purpose — and asks for it to be noticed that she has said the price and that nobody has written it down.** A man of thirty-four who works nights says from the sixth row that he has a version of it in nine words in biro on the back of a card in his wallet and that his nine words and this one's are the same shape, and that he was not going to say his out loud in a room with a microphone on a stand. **The woman of twenty-six, from her seat, says that there are eleven letters in the folder behind her, all addressed to a man of forty-four and all asking the same question in a different first line, and that she has not answered one of them and nobody has asked her to, and that she has written the answers to four of them over three evenings and they are in the bottom drawer of her table and are not going out, and that both of those are true at once because there is nothing on the paper to answer them with and the four are good, and that she is saying it in a room of thirty people because otherwise in about four years there are four answers in a drawer and no record of why.** No rule about who may be asked was invented and the problem is not resolved. **There is now also no mechanism in this city for telling the difference between a man hearing a room and a man hearing a room and getting it wrong, and the man in question said so himself first, and neither sentence exists in print and neither was written down.**
8. **Ch 178, the Monday of week 57 — the printer's debt, and a correct action that changes nothing.** Four feet of shop in the Quiet Quarter, a sign over the door a woman of fifty-eight paid for out of her own money in her first year, a till, a folder of work, a boy of nineteen who has run the machine once and has run it every week since the spring. **A woman of about thirty brings a quarterly district return with a field for what a practice is currently in use and a field for whoever set it in type. The first is filled in with a refusal to put a number in it, on the grounds that a number on a return is a number somebody can quote; the second is filled in with the shop's name, because a blank on a return is a question and she gets nine a quarter.** The cost is not that morning. **She says it at eleven to a man of about sixty who came in with one of the four hundred and thirty-two and has still not given his name here or at a counter: in about four years somebody will walk into a building with a return in their hand and say the shop on that street printed about four hundred and thirty-two, and the shop has a name over the door that a person paid for out of their own money, and there is no version of that she can put in a drawer.** Three things she says to him, in her order: **the fifth line on the older card is the same five words as the company's and not the same sentence, she set it in type in the spring, and she has been told the difference is who the technique is waiting for and does not know whether that is true; the older cards are safer than the company's by the company's own argument, which is why people use them to come off a link a ward needs, and there is no way in this city to take them back off anybody; and she is the person who decides what that card is for, nobody has offered her a way of not being that, she has not asked for one and she is not going to ask him for one.** The boy of nineteen raises it himself: about four hundred pieces of paper carry his handwriting, he is on none of them with his name, and in about four years somebody going round shops will find this shop, and he is not going to be on the cards and is not going to be able to un-ring the bell. **She also says, unasked, that the hundred and forty-one pounds is in a coat on a hook in the back of that shop, that she has looked at it twice this month and is going to keep looking at it, that a form wants a name at the top of it and the only name she has is a postcode and a wet coat and she would have to write a stranger's postcode on a district return, and that in about four years she will be sixty-two and somebody else will be behind that counter and will not know whose coat it is, and that this is the correct outcome and she worked it out herself.** Nobody thanked her, she is not forgiven, softened, recruited or given a decision, and when the man of about sixty asks what happens if he puts the card down and says so and somebody says there was no report, she says then there was no report, that is what the line says, she printed the line, and she is not going to tell him whether it is enough. It is still in his pocket.
9. **Ch 179, the Wednesday of week 57 — a room with a counter and a rule, and three people who did not want a box, and nobody designed any of it.** Nineteen slips in the tray against eleven a month before, and a woman of forty-one who has counted it twice and said the number out loud twice. **A foreman of fifty-eight came into that room once, in the week of week fifty-three, with his own name at the bottom of a slip, and has not been back; he is in the chapter as something that has happened to the room and not as a person who was in it on the Wednesday.** **A man of thirty-four on nights fills nothing in, says he rang a woman of sixty-one to be told by somebody who has never heard of him, and asks what he does with nine words in biro he cannot hand over, and is told: then it is a piece of paper. He says he has stood in a corridor at ten past four that month with two sentences from two people who are both right and neither of whom will give way, that the rule is the same in both places, and that he would like it written down that he knew nothing would happen.** A woman of forty-three gives her name and where she works before she asks, and asks whether a room like this is a place a person goes when they are frightened, and is told no in one go: **three mornings a week and the hours are staying, the door is open on the other four days with nobody behind the counter, a frightened person at ten o'clock on a Tuesday would find a bell and a tray, and that is worse than not coming because they would have been somewhere.** She is told she is not going to be the reason a frightened person comes to a counter with a bell on it, because then in about four years she is a post, and there is no post in this city whose job it is to be here and there is not going to be one, and the only instrument she has is her own hours and that she has said so out loud to nine people. **She then gives the rest of it, in six minutes: she does the stairs at three addresses, one of them a house she goes into four days a week whose address is on the district leaflet that went out in the spring, there is a hall table in that house with a card on it and a fifth line that is the reason she can end something at work, she has used it twice, one of them said thank you in front of a colleague, she asked a question in Crown Terrace and it cost her that hall table in about nine words, and what she has worked out since is that the leaflet did not take the table and the microphone took the table, and she would like somebody to be able to tell her the difference and nobody has.** A boy of nineteen from the shop in the Quiet Quarter asks for the second time whether anybody has ever written down what a person said in a room like this and been believed, and is told two, in nine years, both wrong, that she has never found out how and has never asked. **Then she says what she came to say: she has decided to keep answering questions in that room that are not about a box; she has four reasons written on a piece of paper at home for not answering them and the fourth is that the argument went round this city in a corridor off a service road on the Tuesday of the week before last and she was not in it and has never been in it; a version of it was said to her in about nine words, which means her fourth reason is not hers, and if she refuses a question out of fear of what the room becomes then two people have already had that argument and have not let go of it either. They are not the only one, she is not going to find out whether they have been told, and she will answer the next question that is not about a box on the Friday.**
10. **Ch 180, the Thursday of week 57 — the close, and a column heading.** A bound register in a canvas cover, ruled into four columns, about nine hundred lines, fourteen years back to the week of week thirty-eight, on a table by a counter on the ground floor of a building in Crown Terrace, and **on a Thursday there is nobody behind it, because the woman of thirty-four who reads it out is there on Mondays, Wednesdays and Fridays, and about four hundred people in fourteen years have had a line read to them and not one of them has ever been there on a Thursday.** A woman of forty-one has come on her day off, and said why before she was asked: **her room has about four hundred cards with a box number, a date, a place and a hand in a shorthand on them, and not one has an authority on it anywhere, and this is the only table in this city where an authority is written down.** The finding: **this is a register of removals, a book of what was taken out of a room; a binder went down a corridor to a room with a register in it in the week of week thirty-eight and six lines in this book are about it, and the four volumes in a box on a counter in Lattice Ward are a working copy that has never been in that room; the Compact was never taken out of a room, so it is not in this book, and no line anywhere in this city says who may change it.** She went looking for about four lists in this city with a word at the top of them: three belong to a fund, the fourth is in this book. **He says there is a man on the floor above and she says: he is fifty-eight with a soft voice and a count, he has declined in writing twice to have that table moved into a locked room upstairs because a locked room would have been the correct decision about the pages and the end of the corridor, he told nobody that until a stranger made him tell it, he is a person and a person can be asked, he has been asked six times in fourteen years and has agreed to nothing, and if you asked him whether the requirement stands he would say it stands and say why, and that is not the same as anybody in this city being able to take it away. He is not the authority, he is the only person on that floor, that is not a post, there is no post on that floor and there is not going to be one, and nobody has ever asked him to be the authority over anything.** The six lines are read on a Thursday because there is nothing else to do with a Thursday: two have a hand in the hand column, one of those two has an author still in this city and the man upstairs will not say which, and the fourth column on all six and on all about nine hundred is a job title. **And the close sentence, which the load book records as a finding rather than a sentence, because the sentence is in the room and the finding is what the narrator has: the requirement is in print in the original words and cannot be withdrawn, the only thing that would have to be asked for it is a heading, and a heading is not a person.** She also says she has read all four hundred of her own cards in about four years and has never once wanted to know who was allowed to take one of them out, and has wanted to for a fortnight, and is not going to put it in a tray because that would give the room a column. At ten at night: the shutter, the lamp, the docket with the word on the back of it, the five things under the bench, the jacket, the corner of card in the inside pocket, and the fifth line in the fourth pocket of the tool roll that is blank. **Nothing was settled by a form, a minute, a notice, a report, a committee or a leaflet, and nobody was asked to settle anything.**

## What Movement III did not do, and what it leaves for Movement IV

**It did not identify an authorship and the name is not said, and Sera Quill's other half is not given and does not hint at it. It did not name the licensor, the Crown Clause, Iona or Sorn, and the field is still blank. It did not defeat Threadline, and a company is a service list and a contract and a man's own schedule. It did not have the Continuity Office agree to anything, and the man upstairs was referred to and did not appear. It did not put a card in a rack, and no title is attached to anybody; the recognition is Chapters 198 to 200. It did not grow the Exchange's book, no caller signed, and the next Exchange is the Wednesday of week sixty. It did not resolve the standing problem and invented no rule about who may be asked, and the count is now seven in this volume. It did not stage the relay or imply that it may be staged, and there is no panel. It did not put a second name in the pencil box and did not touch the box, and that is Movement IV's hardest object. It did not find a woman of thirty-four with a hip, did not give the nine words to anybody, did not say Asha Reed's sentence, did not put Rafi Pell in a room, did not find Steve Akers's nine words, and does not use the word *pattern* anywhere. It did not touch the relationship except one public disagreement about method in a corridor with nine men in it, and neither of them used the word *we*, or *our*, or *us*. It did not act on the finding, and the finding is that a rule is in print and a city has been living outside it for fourteen years, and the volume lays that stone and does not build on it.**

# VOLUME 04 — MOVEMENT II (Chapters 161–170), "The Reading Room"

**Volume 04 is open at Chapter 170. This block is the state at the end of Movement II. It supersedes nothing in the `VOLUME 04 — MOVEMENT I` block below it and it inherits every line of it and every line of the `VOLUME 03 CLOSED` block. The load-book run is 164 to 173 and the batch's own account is `workspace/volume-04/batch-0002/SUMMARY.md`.**

## What Chapters 161–170 added to the record, in the order it is established

1. **Ch 161, the Thursday of week 51 — the fourth column is asked about and answered.** A man of twenty-two and a woman of thirty-eight who works nights walk up to a register table in Crown Terrace and ask a question that is not about him. **The whole of the difference is one word: what do you think of the fourth column, not what is in it, and she told him the difference at a threshold on the Tuesday of week forty-eight and it had not been spent since.** She refuses to do it in a corridor in eleven seconds and makes him ask it at the table. **Imogen Bray, thirty-four, four years at that table, about four hundred read-outs, asked twice about the column and told once that it is how the register is written, has never once been asked the second question, and has never had an opinion.** Her answer in three parts: the column is the only one anybody has ever read out loud and nobody has ever gone quiet while she read one; a job title in a column is a sentence with the person taken out of it, and nine people a year have seen it and not one has written to her; and four hundred people have had it read out and not one has come back and said what they thought it was, and she cannot tell whether that is agreement or a queue. **She gave her name into the table, said it is not going on the register, and said a name on a register is fourteen years of a column with a job title in it and a name in a room is a Tuesday. There is no distribution, no minute, no form, no reference and no district. The only copy is in her head and anyone who walks into that building can ask her.**
2. **Ch 161 — the second question was the woman of thirty-eight's, and the answer was worse.** Has anybody ever been anything for a line in that register: no, in fourteen years, on both counts, and nobody has been praised for one because there is no line in it with a name on it. **She is not thanked on the stairs because she told him on a half landing at twenty past midnight on the Wednesday of week forty-eight that he was not going to and that she was not going to be thanked, and she said the cost has arrived — she is now a person who has been asked something, in front of a stranger, in a building, and in about three weeks this city is going to want a person who was asked, and it will be her, and she will come in the day, and the daytime is six weeks of it and then it is not — and then said that writing things down is what he does, and it is a good thing to be and it is not a use.**
3. **Ch 162, the Friday of week 51 — the fourth condition quoted intact in a corridor and not renegotiated.** Eleven jobs, nine in date, and nobody asked him where a release was going to come from, which is four Fridays. **A district nurse of thirty-four with a file with her own name at the top of it, which she put there herself in the spring and is not disciplined for, asks him to say the condition word for word so that she can check her own copy, and he puts the four names in before *at any time* on the first pass and she stops him at the word *this*, and both of them had it wrong in the same place, hers copied off a sheet in a room in Crown Terrace in about four seconds with about nine people in it.** The order is the only part of that sentence that matters and he cannot hold it after two o'clock and has not been able to since the Friday of week forty-seven. **A depot manager of fifty-two comes up nine floors, which he does not do, to put the wording to somebody who could put it to somebody, and is told no before the reason is asked for and given the reason anyway: the moment somebody starts looking at the wording it becomes a thing that could be worded differently, and the only thing that has kept it honest for a term and a half is that it is a sentence nobody can improve.** He says it is the same thing the nurse said four minutes earlier, nine words apart, and that neither of them had heard the other, and that this is now twice this term. The sheet stays as it is. **She did not write the condition down, because a piece of paper with a condition on it is a thing somebody can ask for, and she is using a sentence of his as a wall and he has decided not to be the one who improves it.**
4. **Ch 163, the Monday of week 52 — both relationship beats, and the lift engineer's list.** Milestone 1 is reported and not staged: on the Sunday he walked four miles to a converted shop unit and did not go in, and four miles away a woman of twenty-four spent the one clean call she has in a week on a counter in Bower Street and said a district and nothing else, **and neither of them has ever mentioned the other's Sunday to the other and there has been nothing to mention for a term and a half.** Milestone 2 is a corridor on the ninth floor of a tower block in Nine Bridges: she says **three weeks** first, so the gap is said out loud and is not fudged, then **you have made somebody askable who was not askable**, tells him it is nine words and that it did not get better, says she has two visits and a bag and a lift that is out, **does not say she told him to do it, and goes down the corridor and into a flat.** No scene, no declaration, no kiss, no *we* between them anywhere in the batch, the bench not reopened, Chapter 102's Tuesday not revisited, the Chapter 7 page closed. **In a motor room a lift engineer of twenty-nine, Marguerite Okpara, six years, about nine hundred lifts, on the Friday list because her employer pays for the card, shows him a piece of cardstock cut off something with a printed edge with sixteen Fridays on it in biro, two numbers to a line, the card saying eleven on all sixteen and the buildings having eight, nine, nine, ten, nine, eleven, nine, eight, ten, nine, nine, eleven, nine, eight, ten and nine, and she did not total them because a total is a number somebody can quote.** She shows it to him on purpose, and says: you are the only person in this city who can say a number out loud in a room and you are not allowed to be asked about it, so it is the only route that is not a route. **Her cost, ready since the Monday: the back of her own card, her employer on the front, no copy, and no form in this city that takes a number like that. He wrote no line of it on the docket and said why, and the why is the word and a woman of thirty-eight in Bower Street.** It is in her head and his, and a man of thirty-four who works nights has counted it too, which makes three, and he has told neither of them and has not been told.
5. **Ch 164, the Tuesday of week 52 — a second no with no reason on it, and a sentence that has stopped working.** A stairwell in Nine Bridges at ten past four in the afternoon, four hours before he starts, and he asked for the stairwell and not the canteen. **He has nine words in biro on the back of a work card from the spring about a doorway in Nine Bridges and a man who had gone, and he can still hold that, and it is what stopped him saying out loud that anybody handing you the other sort of card is a front.** What he cannot hold: **in the week of the thirty-eighth a woman with a lanyard asked him to take the word on his own floor for his own four people so that they would have somebody to wait for, and he said no in a second and a half, and he has tried the reason out loud four times in a stairwell and once on a bus and the sentence is different every time, and there is not a second reason, there is a door with something behind it he cannot get a hand on, and he stopped trying in about a fortnight because he was getting angry about it and angry is how he got the first one wrong.** He is not on the card at all, and asks which of the two he has got — a reason with the words gone, or no reason — and is told the number and that it is not a rate. **He keeps both cards, is asking nobody to take either off him, has said no twice, and wanted it noticed that the no came first both times, and said he wants it kept and not used and that there is a difference and nobody in this city has a word for it and he is not asking for one.**
6. **Ch 164 — the sentence that was available and was refused.** He could have said, out loud, in the man's own words, the reason the man could not say, taken out of his own book, and it would have worked in a corridor and in a canteen and in that stairwell and nobody in any building would have known. **That is demonstrating, and the condition covers all four verbs. He did it in his head, told the man he had done it, and did not do it, and said the reason: a sentence of his in a stairwell is not a reason, it is a sentence, and in four years it will be the only thing in this city saying the man was right, and that is the worst possible place for a reason to be.** A number went down wrong on a docket twice and the man said it three times and asked for that to be on the record.
7. **Ch 165, the Wednesday of week 52 — the Exchange, nine people, the book not opened, and nobody signed.** The reason in one go, and it is a rule and not a mood and it is older than the woman who runs the room and was there before the tin: **a book of what a caller said cannot be written by the people who did not call.** The fortieth line is three years old, line forty-four has its hole in it, and the fifth chair against the wall is still empty and one person offered to move it and was told the chair is not the room's business. **A woman of thirty-one who rang a number to ask whether the room was real was told that if that is right she is not a caller and the argument was not had and is not going to be had.** A man of thirty-four used two of his four minutes to say a thing he had said in a stairwell the day before and asked out loud that it not go in the book, was thanked for saying it in the room rather than afterwards, was not asked whether he would ring on a Sunday, and said he would not. **And then the number, unasked: in about three weeks eleven people have telephoned Bea Nunn to ask whether it is true that a person can say anything in that room, and she has said yes eleven times, and she did not know what she was saying yes to.** Her own account of the risk: a room where a person can say anything becomes a room where a person comes to be told something, and being told something by a woman behind a counter at eleven at night in a room with no job in it is worth a very great deal to somebody who has been frightened for about four months, and she has no rule about that, because the other half of her rule is that she does not decide who is a caller. A man of thirty-four told her that half is not a rule and she agreed, and said that is exactly why it must not sound like one. **The book is at forty-four lines and has not been opened since the Wednesday of week forty-eight.**
8. **Ch 166, the Thursday of week 52 — the telephone on a wall is rung, and a box number is a place.** A prefab office off the Meridian corridor, a second gate lamp, a panel open. **Rowan Teale, thirty-one, a stock clerk who fills in a box on a stock return every quarter whether anybody asks him to or not, rang it at about eleven because the box he fills in has held the same four-letter post town and the same box number for four quarters and he was told to re-verify a field that has not changed in four quarters before he could submit it, and there was nobody at his end to ask.** Bryn Maw told him the box is not his, that he has got a stranger's box number in a stranger's handwriting and a town with four letters in it, that he is the second holder of it, and that there is one other and he is twenty-two and is not there; and added that he was not going to ask the man for him and the man said he was not going to offer. Then the clerk did the ordinary thing and rang the town. **A recorded voice about opening hours, and then a woman at a counter called Bettina Rook in a town called Brin, which is four letters and is not Nacre: the box is real, it is on an end wall third bay, the card on it is the only record of who holds it, the card is not something she will look at for a man on a telephone, and she would not tell him standing there either. About four hundred boxes in that building and about nine in that town that anybody collects from, and the rest are company names, and a company name is not a person. And then, unasked and before the line went: it has not been collected from in about two years, because she empties the row on a Friday and has for eleven years.** **The licensor is still unnamed, the field is still blank on three documents in three buildings and on a company's own stock docket, and nothing in that town closes anything.** The cost, twice, in his own words: in about four years a person can stand at that counter with a reference off a card and a holder of this address will have something taken off them by somebody they have never met, on a Friday, with a card, and there is no way to find out it is happening. And the part that cost him: a telephone on that wall is the only addressable thing about him in this city, he chose it on purpose in the spring, and a man at a company has now heard a stranger's box number said out loud in that office twice. **He did not want the call, did not say so for about two hours, and gave the bad reason himself: said to a man at a company it would have been a sentence about a company, the man has a form with a field in it, and in about four years there is a note in a file about that office — and he said saying it to Marek Senn is different because that book is not a system, and that this is the same shape as the thing he would not say, and that he does not have a better account of it than that.**
9. **Ch 167, the Friday of week 52 — the standing problem a sixth time, said by him, and not resolved.** The eighth of the eleven sessions, forty folding chairs and nine of them empty, a microphone on a stand, four hundred leaflets by the door, five jobs on the front of which two are him. **A woman of forty-three, who gave her name and where she works before she asked, asked whether a card on a hall table at one of three addresses she works at — four steps, a fifth line, her employer's name at the top — is about her, and said the reason she was asking him is that he is the only person on that sheet who is a person.** The answer is nine words and it does not answer her, and then the shape of what he has got, and then the one thing he has: the fifth line is on the card and not in somebody's head. **He then stands up in the fourth row and says the condition himself, in the first person, for the first time in a term and a half of letting other people say it about him, and the microphone is on a stand and nobody asked for it off and nobody in this city can.** The six reasons are the six reasons: it stops him doing the thing, it does not stop anybody asking him about the thing, there is no rule in this company or in this city about who may be asked, he has checked four times, the answer is the same every time and the answer is that there is not one, and a rule about who may be asked is a rule about who is findable. **She put her hand up and asked who is allowed to ask him about this, was told nobody can tell him that, said then I am allowed, said she is a member of the public and has read the leaflet and has asked a question in a room with a microphone on it, said she would like that written down somewhere and does not think it can be, and would like somebody to try. Nobody tried.** And then her own cost out loud, unasked: she did three addresses on the Monday of that week and said a name and a street out loud in that same room on the Friday night, with the microphone on its stand, the address she works at is on the district leaflet that went into about nine hundred rooms in the spring and is not the same leaflet as the four hundred on the stack by the door, and there is a hall table with a card on it in that house and she goes in that house four days a week, and she has been asked nothing for nine years and is now asked something, and it cost her a hall table, and she would say it again and would also like it not to have been a microphone. **A woman of twenty-six wrote nothing down and said why: there was a man with a notebook in the fifth row on the Friday of week forty-eight and he took down three answers in her hearing and she did not stop him and has thought about it for four weeks, and this week there is nobody, which is not better for him and is worse for the woman of forty-three, and she is not going to tell the room which of those two matters more because she does not know.**
10. **Ch 168, the Monday of week 53 — Roy Amiss is rung, in nine seconds, and it is not a rescue.** A district nurse of thirty-four rang him from a corridor at about eleven, **and she is not Marek Senn and is not Talia Venn**, and she wrote nothing down before the call and nothing down after it. She said who she was, that she is the person who did not ring him, that she has been not ringing him since the Thursday, and that she has not got anything to give him. **He said the thing about the call, which he had practised on a bus: he read the card on the Thursday night, waited a week, nobody rang, and for about three weeks the only thing he has done with a phone that works and a hip and about a fortnight of stairs is wait for a telephone.** He said that he was not going to say thank you, and gave the reason — a thank you is what a person says when somebody has done something for them, she has not done something for him, she has done a thing at the same time as she was going to do a thing to about forty other people, and he is one of them and has not been singled out and has noticed — and that he would like his own sentence about there being no reason anybody owes him written down in whatever the nearest thing to a record is, and was told there is no record of it, and said then he has said it into a telephone and a nurse of thirty-four heard it, and that is the whole of the record, and it is in a corridor and not in a system, and he has got a worse one of those than a leaflet. **She did not fill in the form there is in that district for people who have been rung, and the reason is that the form is how the district finds out about a person, and the whole reason she rang is that the district did not find out about him in seven weeks and should have.** Her own cost, in a corridor, to nobody: she has rung one of about forty-one people since the Tuesday of week thirty-eight and did it once, in nine seconds, and she is going back on the Tuesday to try to do the other forty, and she has no rule for ringing forty-one people in a week and has not asked anybody for one. **He is not thanked, not found, not going to be comforted. The leaflet is still on his table and it is still about a practice and not about a man, and it is still wrong for him to have, and nobody has taken it off him and nobody is going to.**
11. **Ch 169, the Wednesday of week 53 — the reading room is becoming a place people walk to and nobody designed it.** A woman of forty-one counted a tray she has not counted in nine years: **eleven slips in about three weeks and not one of them is about a box, behind about forty read-outs in nine years.** Her position, out loud and unasked: in nine years nobody has ever come in and asked her a question that was not about a box, in about three weeks four people have, she has answered all four and has not got a rule for answering a question, and **a rule that holds when eleven people want something it was not built for is still the same rule.** **A foreman of fifty-eight came in with one line of a slip filled in and three of them left and gave both his reasons: he wanted to see what a name at the bottom of a piece of paper felt like on paper once, and he has got a key to a basement and has not used it and would rather be told no by a person than find out in a basement on his own.** She told him there is nothing in that room about a building's own rooms, that the room is what came out of this room and not what went into buildings, and that she will not add a fifth thing to the sheet and will not help him with the other three. He wrote them: *from when, never; to when, still; who is asking, Bryn Maw, gate, Meridian consolidation point.* **Day arithmetic, fixed: he was last in the prefab office off the Meridian corridor on the Thursday of week fifty-two, which is six days before this Wednesday and not four.** **His cost, out loud, at a counter, in a public room: a slip with his name on it is a thing somebody can point at in four years and say he asked, by somebody who has read about nine hundred plates and wants a name to go with them, and he is not taking it back.** She named the other three because he asked and because she had decided to stop not saying: a man of sixty-ish who asked whether a thing can be safe because a lot of people have been given it; a woman who was told, and it was not written down, **that the difference between the two cards everybody is talking about is that one has a printer's shop name on the back of it and the other has nothing**, and who said that was the first useful thing anybody had said to her in about four months; and a boy of nineteen who asked whether anybody had ever written down what a person said in a room like this and been believed, was told she did not know, and said that is the correct answer.
12. **Ch 170, the Thursday of week 54 — the pencil is turned over, the name is said, a slip is signed in the first person, and the sentence.** A ring file of about a thousand condition sheets in a cupboard on a ground floor, a woman of thirty-six who has done the sheets up for nine years and turns every sheet over because everybody does, and fourteen lines in pencil on the back of one of them. She reads all fourteen out. **The correct action follows and it changes nothing: a line in a building book saying there are fourteen items, one sheet, the week of week fifty, no column, not actioned, and in about four years the pencil will have come off and there will be nothing on that page and that is not anybody's fault.** She is not going to ask for a second sheet and is not going to hand a woman of forty-six a job. **And then the name, said before the question was finished: a man of twenty-two asked me a question in a corridor in the week of the forty-seventh that my sheet could not answer. His name is Marek Senn. He did not ask me to write anything down. I have never told him what I was going to do with it. That is your second time of asking and it is the last thing I am going to say about it.** **THE RELAY IS RESOLVED IN ONE DIRECTION AND IT IS NOT A RELAY: nobody told Marek Senn. The name is in exactly two heads, hers and the woman of thirty-six's, and neither has said it to anybody. He was not in that office, does not know the name was said, and is not going to find out. There is no third account and no man of twenty-six and no relay; an earlier draft of this block carried two more and both are retracted. What the woman of forty-six did put in front of him was on the Thursday of week fifty, with a kettle on and about four days before anybody asked her, and it was what the pencil was going to cost her, and he wrote that down in his own book in a pencil's line, and there was never a way to take that back either.** **In the reading room, on a Thursday, which is not one of her three days, a woman of forty-one is behind the counter because she wanted to find out whether a person who is not asking her for a box would still find a person there, and there was no way of finding that out on a Wednesday.** **Marek Senn, twenty-two, a repairer, wrote across all four printed lines in one sentence in the first person in his own words and signed it with his name on it, and what was on it is his own object and not anybody else's: a card nobody has made, with five things on it, four of them filled in and the fifth being the only one that matters, unfilled for a term and a half, and nobody ever having asked him to. The four lines are about four things and the one thing did not fit them, and the room took it and she was not nice about taking it. She gave the rule again in eleven seconds and said it is a rule and not a mood and it was a rule before she came, and she put both of her numbers on the counter unasked: about forty binder read-outs in nine years and about two hundred people through that door since the spring, of whom about a hundred and sixty wanted a box. She also said that nobody had ever come in there and written one thing across four lines in the first person and signed it, and that she had not known until that morning that it was allowed.** **What the room has for him is nothing: a card does not leave the room, a box does, a box needs a slip, he had a slip, and there is no card in that room for a card that has not been made, because there is nothing to write on the top line of it and no shelf to put it on.** What he has instead is a piece of paper with his name on it in a tray with eleven other pieces of paper, which is twelve in the tray. At about eleven twenty she said out loud that she was going to the post, which is in the same building and takes nine minutes, and went out and left the door open the way she always does — **and the whole of what that room does happens when she is standing behind the counter, and there is no form for it, no post, no minute and no notice, and it is a habit in a room with a counter and a person behind it, and a habit can be stopped by that person walking out.**

## What Movement II did not do, and what it leaves for Movement III

**It did not put a second name in the box in pencil, and it deliberately did not; that is Movement IV's hardest object. It did not open the plans in the basement and nobody went down, and a foreman of fifty-eight with a key has said out loud that he has not used it. It did not resolve the standing problem and it invented no rule about who may be asked, and the count is now six in this volume and the resolution's is the seventh. It did not grow the Exchange's book and no caller signed. It did not name the licensor, the Crown Clause, or an authorship, and the names Iona and Sorn appear nowhere. It did not defeat Threadline and it did not have the Continuity Office agree to anything. It did not name the holder of the box in Brin and it did not use the counter to close anything. It did not make the master leave the pocket and nobody decided anything about it. It did not put a card in a rack and it did not touch the recognition. It did not begin milestone 3, which is Chapters 166 and 167 in the outline and which a Chapter 171 writer must still find unwritten. It did not touch the relationship anywhere except one corridor, and Chapter 170 does not mention it at all. It did not find a woman of thirty-four with a hip, give the nine words to anybody, or use the word *pattern* anywhere.**

**Where this block says something is missing from the world, the correct sentence is *there is not one of those in this city*, and that is a fact and not an invitation.**

**A FLAG ON THE SIZE OF THIS FILE.** The compaction pass of Volume 04 batch 0001 brought the five state files from 1.98 MB to about 350 KB by moving the day-number interval map to `workspace/volume-04/ARITHMETIC-AND-CALENDAR.md` and archiving the three closed volumes. **This file has grown back to about 93 KB, and the growth is Movement II's eight new dated pressures and its seventeen numbered facts, not bookkeeping.** The map has not come back and no per-chapter prose has been restored. **A Volume 04 batch 0003 writer should consider one more compaction of the three Movement repair-pass blocks, whose full record is `reviews/volume-04-batch-0001.md`, and should not add bookkeeping of any kind to this file.**
