# VOLUME 04 — MOVEMENT I (Chapters 151–160), "One Box"

**Volume 04 is open. `outline/volume-04.md` was written before Chapter 151 in the same run and it supersedes the Volume 04 paragraph of `outline/series.md` on two points, both recorded in its *Deviations from the series sketch* and both argued at length in `workspace/volume-03/batch-0005/VOLUME-CLOSE.md` section 10. The load-book run continues from 153 and Movement I is entries 154 to 163.**

## THE TWO RE-BASES, WHICH ARE DECISIONS AND NOT NOTES

1. **THE POWER STATE. `outline/series.md` says *Stage 2, with the first sustained relay*. THAT LINE IS SUPERSEDED.** The relay was staged once, in Chapter 136; the carrier was told the cost before it started, objected before she agreed, and stopped it on the sixth of the seven headings because the sixth came out of her as a road and not as a fact; the observation arrived downstream with a kerb on the outside of a corner and about four stops and a Thursday on it; her round was re-cut on the Monday of week forty and she is not going to do it again for him, for a district, or for a woman in Crown Terrace with a clipboard. **The Volume 03 end state forbids the relay being easier or being repeated, and a sustained relay is a repeated relay, so a sustained relay is not available at any price.** The correct Volume 04 state is **Stage 2 Counterpoint with a permanent sensory sensitivity, an uncorrectable word, a demonstrated-but-unrepeatable relay, and a fourth condition that stops him doing the thing and does not stop him being asked about it.** Growth in Volume 04 is earned elsewhere: archives, a register, a ledger in public custody, a card in a rack, and the discovery that a return path was once required and is not required now. **`outline/series.md` is a planning file and was NOT edited; the supersession is recorded here and in `state/current.md` so that a later writer who reads the sketch does not undo the volume.**
2. **THE RECOGNITION. *A recognised researcher, not a public hero* is kept and re-based into an object instead of a title.** A recognition that arrives with a title is a name on the front of a document, the fourth condition makes him the most findable person in this city, the volume's whole argument is that findability is the hazard, and a man of forty-four has already written *no name* in two boxes in his own hand and will be getting letters about it for about four months. **The recognition in this volume is a named seat in a reading room: a card in a rack, in his own hand, on purpose, renewable, removable by a clerk, no title and no office on it, and it costs him the last thing he has, which is not being findable.** The ground for it is planted in Chapter 154 in six sentences and nothing in Movement I is a recognition and no page anticipates one.

## What Chapters 151–160 added to the record, in the order it is established

1. **Ch 151, the Wednesday of week 47 — the box in pencil takes its first name and it cannot come out.** A counter on the ground floor of a building in Bower Street, about two hundred of the district's withdrawal pages in four stacks with a rubber band on each, four fields, one of them a tick, and one box about the size of a stamp with three words over it in a clerk's hand. **A woman of thirty-eight telephoned it and said her name.** She is Doreen Abbiss and she works nights in a residential home, and she held a share on a bay with four people on the Tuesday of week thirty-eighth, one of them answered, it was not her, and she took it off anyway in four seconds, and a bay went off eleven minutes later and nobody was hurt. **Nobody asked her whether she was willing and nobody is going to.** She was asked one question and did not answer it. She gave her name in about nine seconds to a stranger and then asked for it out. **It cannot come out: the page goes back in a stack, a district picks those up and nobody knows when, and tearing it out makes a hole in a stack, and a hole in a stack is a thing somebody asks about.** She said she had made a phone call and not a mistake, and that nobody asked her whether she wanted to put her name on anything and that she would have said yes to being asked. **A name written in pencil is the least recoverable object in this case and it is the only box in this manuscript that can take a name.**
2. **Ch 151 — what he did was wait, and the word's consequence changed form.** Marek Senn was in the corridor outside the counter for about forty minutes with a docket and nine minutes of a job, and could hear a telephone and could not hear a voice on it or either of the two people in the room, and did not knock and did not ask. He noticed the rubber band on the first stack was tighter that morning and did not think about it for about a week. **The word was at nine a minute, which is the morning's number, and it did not stop him hearing a voice and it did not stop him asking a question about a voice, and he did not ask one.** This is the second half of "he has failed to hear one" and it is now a fact about a woman on a telephone.
3. **Ch 152, the Thursday of week 47 — the plate is a service plate and there are two kinds.** **Ines Kolar, 46**, nineteen years walking four buildings every Thursday with a trolley, a bucket, four cloths, a printout of four pages that comes down on a Friday, and a condition sheet with forty-one lines of which the forty-first is a space two inches high for conditions where there are no conditions that nobody has ever used. **A building plate is enamel, a number and nothing else, one in a hallway in about four years, nobody replaces it. A service plate is printed, with the company's name along the bottom edge the size of her thumb, and the number on it is a service number.** She knew four years ago from a man of fifty-five in a basement and has never said it because nobody has ever asked. **The room in Bower Street has never had its own number on the door; about nine hundred doors in four buildings are the same; the room is 2-07 and the plate says 2-14 and both are correct about themselves and neither is the building's; about four hundred consent leaflets in the rack all say 2-07 and the room on a leaflet is the room on the plan and not the room on the door, and reconciling the two is not a condition.**
4. **Ch 152 — the first question a sheet could not answer, and there is no form that takes a discrepancy.** He asked what happens if the plate is wrong, and who checks, and that the sheet is made of the list, and she made him say it in a different order. **A discrepancy is not a condition and a condition is the only thing that sheet takes, and the moment it goes in there somebody signs the bottom of that page and the signature means the room is as the list says it is, and it is not.** There is no visitor column and he is not on the round; she walks four buildings and he walks with her and the sheet says one name because the sheet says one name, and if anybody asks him why he was in the second building he is to say he was waiting for a door to open, and she said that would be true and would be a worse answer than the truth and is the one that keeps him out of a file.
5. **Ch 153, the Friday of week 47 — the rota, and the standing problem a fourth time, and the word as a work failure.** Eleven jobs, nine in date, the word at nine, ten and eleven a minute between half past nine and two o'clock. A district telephoned at ten past one with a written question and the depot manager of fifty-two read it out; it wanted to know whether a name could be put against a release and whether the name would be the person who gave the word. **He said nine words: a release is finished by the person holding it, not by a person waiting. Somebody wrote it down.** Then the manager said the condition out loud, to nobody in particular, in a Friday briefing, and **said it stops him doing the thing and does not stop anybody asking him about the thing, and that there is no rule in this company or in this city about who may be asked, and that he has checked it three times and the answer is that there is not one.** That is the fourth time and it is not resolved and no rule was invented. **And then the word did a new thing: he could not hold the order of two sentences that had mattered after two o'clock, wrote them down in the wrong order, crossed it out, and wrote a note on the docket saying he cannot hold the order, not the words, the order, ask him again or do not use it — and then decided the note is not right either.**
6. **Ch 153 — a man of thirty-four on nights, and the number from a person who has counted it himself.** He has the lanyard card and the printer's card in one wallet and has stopped telling people that the other sort is a front, because he went back to a doorway in Nine Bridges and the man had gone. **He has counted about nine on a Friday twice since the spring and once since the summer, said twice is a sample of two, said if the card is wrong then four people a week are standing in rooms waiting for a person who is not on the rota, said he has been one of those twice and there was nobody there and he went home both times and did the job anyway, and said doing it anyway is not a system.** He has rung a company's service line four times in four months, about forty minutes of it in total, and has been told to ring the district, and has not got anybody to report to. **The eleven is now known to three people in this city and to a district card and it is still not a rate.**
7. **Ch 154, the Monday of week 48 — the archives open, and the seal is not a lock.** **Wendla Ossick, 41**, registrar of a public reading room in Lattice Ward, Mondays, Wednesdays and Fridays, thirty-one years in the building and nine in the room. **Her rule in eleven words: a box does not come down without a slip, and a slip is a piece of paper and a person.** A slip with a person on it is a request; a slip without one is a piece of paper and she has not got a tray for pieces of paper. **The Marrow Falls material is not in the room and was never in the room. It was four volumes that went down a corridor in the week of week thirty-eighth, fourteen years ago, and the seal is a column in a register in Crown Terrace, and there is no lock in that room and there has never been a lock in that room and there is a sign about eleven feet from the door that says so and nobody has read it.** She said *nothing is sealed* twice, deliberately, the second time watching his face. **A column is not a lock, and a column can be read by anybody who can get into the building, and that building has got a counter on the ground floor and the register is on a table by it.** The room is about four hundred boxes and about four hundred cards in four drawers, one per box, each with a number, a date, a place, a hand in a shorthand, and a shelf.
8. **Ch 154 — the ground for the volume's resolution, planted and not cashed.** **A room like that is built out of about four hundred cards in four drawers and every one of them has got a person who put it there written on it, and the reason anybody can find anything in there is that the finding is a card in a drawer, and the card is not hidden and not locked and is in a building anybody can walk into, and nobody has ever asked her to think about that.** He wrote down six shelf numbers and got one of the six wrong and did not find out until the Wednesday of week fifty, **and the chapter says plainly that the number was not the thing that mattered, and that he did not think about the rest of it until about six weeks later, by which time it was in a book in an inside pocket on a chair and it had cost somebody a name.**
9. **Ch 155, the Tuesday of week 48 — the register, the four columns, and a count.** A bound register, canvas, four columns, about nine hundred lines, on a table by a counter on the ground floor of a building in Crown Terrace, read out to anybody who asks by **a Continuity Office assessor of thirty-four who told him it was a public document and that is her whole view before he asked.** **The columns are date, place, hand, authority, and every one of about nine hundred lines in the authority column is a job title and not one of them has ever been a person.** The hand column is blank in about four lines in ten. **Iven Sore, 58, asked three smaller questions, refused, and gave a count: six lines relate to the binder, two of the about nine hundred were written by a hand rather than a title, one of those two has an author still in this city, and he would not say which and would not say why.** His reason is the best one anybody in this city has given for anything: after the outage about nine people read those pages who were not asked to, four wrote to him, three letters were printed, every one of the three wanted the pages at once, and every one of the three was wrong about a single thing, which is that the pages are not a route, because a route has a person on it who can say no. **And then the one thing he did, which is not an opening: two offices have offered in writing to have the register moved into a locked room upstairs and he has declined both, because a locked room would have been the correct decision about the pages and the end of the corridor, and he would rather have a corridor that does not work than a locked room that does, and he told nobody until Chapter 155.**
10. **Ch 155 — the same mistake three times in three buildings, by four people who have never met.** A name on the front of a levy return, a name on a slip in a reading room, and a job title in a column in a register. **It is not a conspiracy and it is not a company, and it is the same instinct, and he wrote *no name* in a document in the Monday of week forty-two and told a man of forty-four it was not going to be him, and did not know that a building would do the same thing to him in a corridor four weeks later.** The woman of thirty-four said at the threshold that he had asked what was in the fourth column and not what she thought of it, and that those are the same question and not the same question, and that about four people in four years have asked her the first one, and that if he comes back she would like to be asked the first one.
11. **Ch 156, the Wednesday of week 48 — the Exchange, and the book does not go to forty-five.** Nine people, a tin that is not locked, a four-column book at forty-four lines with the fortieth three years old and a hole in line forty-four, and a fifth chair against the wall that has been empty since the spring. **Nobody called, and Bea Nunn, 61, did not open the book, and the reason is a rule and not a mood: a room of people who did not call is not nine callers, and a line in a book is a promise that somebody stood at a telephone and wanted something and got it, and if she writes tonight then in about four years somebody reads line forty-five and finds out that a woman of thirty-eight who did not want to be found said a true thing in a room with nine people in it, and that is not a book of callers, that is a book of people she liked.** She has liked four people in nine years, has written two of them down, and was wrong both times. **Doreen Abbiss was in the room and would not sign, and gave the reason out loud: the last time she signed anything in this city with her name on it, somebody found her.** She then talked for nine minutes and none of it was written down, and said at about eight minutes that she was not asking anybody for anything while she was still talking.
12. **Ch 156 — the Exchange, and the standing room.** **The rule is restated and it is a rule: you can say anything, all of it, in your own words, and nobody will write it down and nobody will stop you; the rule is against the book, because a caller is a person and a person signs their own words and a practice cannot sign anything.** That is the only reason the book and the ledger sheet of four weeks ago are different objects, and the only reason either of them exists. **He was on the half landing and did not go up, and Doreen Abbiss came down past him and said she could hear somebody outside a door on Wednesday morning for about forty minutes and could not hear what they were saying, and that it did not matter at the time and matters now.** She said the thing she had not been able to say to anybody: a form with four fields and a tick is the best thing anybody in this city has written and it does not work, and now there is a box with her name in it and she cannot get it out, and nobody drew that box on purpose and nobody is going to fix it, and **there is no post.** And she gave him nine words for Movement I: *the next time you want a name on a piece of paper, ask the person whose name it is, before you have got a pencil out.*
13. **Ch 157, the Thursday of week 48 — THE CHOICE, and it costs her and not him.** A slip needs a name and the only name in this city he could put on it is not his, because the district leaflet that carries the address carries a job title that is in about four hundred pay packets, and the woman whose name is in the box in pencil is the woman whose district the register is about and whose corridor the corridor is. **He wrote two letters and did not write the third, and she was standing behind him and had read over his shoulder, and she said: *you have just made me the reason*.** She gave the longer half herself: **a piece of paper with no name on it is a piece of paper somebody has to be angry about**, and that is what a room full of people does to it, and four years after a man of forty-four wrote two words in two boxes, four thousand went out to about eleven thousand premises and about forty people who had written in since the spring were told nobody was dealing with it, and she has that leaflet and everybody has that leaflet. He asked her properly, before the pencil, on the Thursday, **and she said yes, and she said the cost before the yes: she works nights, she is awake in the day, the daytime is the only part of the week that is hers, and if she is a person who has been asked something then the next person this city needs is her, and she will come in the day, and the daytime is six weeks of it and then it is not.** She said she wanted it noted that he did not try to talk her out of the cost once. **He tore the slip in four on the counter. The registrar said that was the correct action, that a request with a person on it and a reason on it is better than nothing, that he has thrown away something better than nothing, and that she has a school group in eleven minutes.**
14. **Ch 158, the Friday of week 48 — the seventh session, and the two boxes that are the only two anybody can write to.** Thirty-one folding chairs and two empty, a microphone, four hundred leaflets by the door, a panel of five listed by job of which two are him. **The woman who types things is twenty-six, has been at the fund for three years, types the letters that go out, does not type the specification and does not sign the returns, and has eleven letters in a folder behind her all asking a man of forty-four why a levy return went out with two boxes that say *no name*, and has answered none and nobody asked her to, and said it is not a decision: she has decided there is nothing on the paper to answer them with.** She stood up for the first time in three years and said: **there are five fields, four are empty, one has a date, and the two that say no name are the only two anybody can read from the outside, because they are the only two with words in them, one in a man's handwriting and one in hers, and she did not write either of them, and every one of those eleven letters has gone to a woman of twenty-six who types things.** She asked for two things and neither was a favour: that it be minuted that she had not been asked to do anything and had come, and that she is going back to a folder with eleven letters in it on Monday and is not going to answer them. **And a man of forty-four said the second half of his own sentence out loud for the first time in that room: a document with no name on it is a document people get angry at, and four thousand pieces of paper went out and about forty people who had written in were told nobody was dealing with it, and everybody in this city has that leaflet, and he has read it as carefully as she has. Somebody asked which is it and he said both.**
15. **Ch 158 — Peter Amado, about fifty-one, asked for the microphone off for the second Friday running and somebody switched it off.** He said the true thing and then the other thing: he does not know how long he can keep doing it, nobody has offered him a way out, and he is going to keep coming. **He was told for the fourth time that he did not have to, and said he has thought about the offer more than he has thought about the fee, and that he is going to take the fee anyway, and asked for it to be on a record that he said it while he still knew. Nobody has told him that asking for the microphone to be switched off is the thing that makes it permanent, and Marek Senn has now had two Fridays to tell him and has not.**
16. **Ch 159, the Monday of week 49 — the hundred and forty-one pounds goes back to the person who paid it.** A person in a wet coat came into four feet of shop in the Quiet Quarter and wanted four hundred more of the cards with the fifth line on the back. **They are not printed off a photograph this time; they are set from the same type by a man who has never had a company's card in his hand; and the name of the shop goes on the back of every one of them, in the same place, the same way, the same size, because a card with that shop's name on it is a card a person can trace, and she called that the only weapon she has got.** The four hundred and thirty-two already out have her name in the postcode and none of them has her name on the card, and she said that was a decision she took in the spring in about nine minutes and it was the worst decision anybody has made in that shop in nineteen years. **She then gave the envelope back.** The cost, in her own words: in about four years, if anybody wants to know who printed four hundred and thirty-two cards, there is nothing on that table to take; the money is going out of the shop in a bag with a person; and if that person is the wrong person then the wrong person has got a hundred and forty-one pounds and her with it. **A boy of nineteen on the till wrote the name on four hundred cards and said he is not going to be on any of them with his name and is going to be on all of them with his handwriting, and she said that is the correct answer and it is worse than hers.**
17. **Ch 159 — Petar Vance, Threadline field service engineer, is on the record and is not a villain.** Nineteen years servicing exchange rooms, a service reference he does not give out, and he does not like anybody who asks him whether a card is safe. **About nine hundred plates in four buildings carry his company's name along the bottom edge, and he did not choose the numbers, they come off a room list that is not his, and he has asked for it twice and been told it is the building's, and he cannot change about nine hundred doors because a district nurse wants one room, and that is not a complaint, that is a service, and he has said so in writing and been copied on a letter about it.** The boy of nineteen asked him the second question anybody in this city has asked: if the room list is not yours, whose is it. **And he said the building's, and the building's is a set of plans in a basement in a drawer on a hook, and there has not been a person in that basement since about the spring, and he has the plates and the plans have the rooms, and the two of them have not met in nineteen years, and if you want to know which one is right the answer is that they are both right about themselves and one of them is in a drawer. And then he said that if anybody writes that down he has not said it and he will be able to deny it, because he has not said it.**
18. **Ch 160, the Thursday of week 50 — the close, and the forty-first line has something in it for the first time in nineteen years.** **Fourteen lines in pencil, on the back of the condition sheet, upside down relative to the front, in the space where the conditions go, and it is not a condition.** Nine are in the building in Bower Street, three in the second building, and two in a stairwell in Saltmarket that a company plated in the year the stairwell was new and that are not mistakes, and she put them on the sheet anyway because a condition is a thing and this is a thing. **There is no column for a discrepancy and no form in this city that takes one, so the somewhere is the back of a sheet, and the somewhere used to be nowhere.** The sheet goes into a ring file of about nine hundred sheets in a cupboard on a ground floor in another part of the city and nobody asked for it and nobody is going to. **In about four weeks a woman of thirty-six will do the sheets up and turn it over and ask what the pencil is, and a woman of forty-six will have to say that a man of twenty-two asked her a question in a corridor that her sheet could not answer, and that is the only place his name is going to be in this city as a result of anything she has done, and she worked that out on the Monday and not on the Thursday.** What it is worth, in her words: two weeks ago a man could say the door and the wall do not agree and about four people in four buildings would have told him he was talking nonsense; in about four weeks there is a piece of paper in a ring file that says fourteen times, in a hand, that they do not; **that is worth about a fortnight, and in about a year it will be worth nothing, because the pencil will come off, and then the only thing anybody has got is a woman of forty-six who says it on a Thursday.**
19. **Ch 160 — the price, and the basement he did not go into.** **He did not ask to go down, and the reason is in his own book: nobody in this case has earned a basement, and the case is about records, and a basement is the room where the records are, and if he is the first person through that door then he is the man who went through a door, and about eleven people a week are being taught to wait for that.** Ines Kolar told him before he asked that she had never been down and was not going to that day, and asked him to write it down as a thing she said. **The price on the landing: in four weeks the only thing that has changed in this city is a sheet with pencil on the back of it, and about eleven people a week are still going to be taught that a release waits for a man in a lanyard, and he is still that man, and he did that on purpose in a room with nine people in it. Nobody has said so.** The next dated scene is a Thursday morning in a corridor in Crown Terrace where a woman of thirty-eight who works nights is going to stand with him while he asks a question that is not about him.

## What Movement I did not do, and what it leaves for Movement II

**It did not stage the relay or imply it could be staged. It did not put a name in front of a document. It did not give him a title, a seat, a card or a post. It did not open the plans in the basement. It did not get him the six lines. It did not resolve the standing problem and it did not invent a rule about who may be asked. It did not grow the Exchange's book. It did not pay Roy Amiss's unringed telephone, and that carries. It did not find a woman of thirty-four with a hip, and it did not give the nine words to anybody. It did not name the licensor, the Crown Clause, or an authorship, and the names Iona and Sorn appear nowhere. It did not defeat Threadline and it did not have the Continuity Office agree to anything. It did not make the master leave the pocket, and nobody decided anything about it. It did not spend Series milestone 3 and it did not touch the relationship, and Chapter 160 does not mention Talia Venn.**

**Where this block says something is missing from the world, the correct sentence is *there is not one of those in this city*, and that is a fact and not an invitation.**

## The measured figures for the finished batch

**29,758 words. Raw instances of the word *about*: 315, 10.59 per thousand. True hedges: 182, 6.12 per thousand. Bold spans, strict matcher: 296, 9.95 per thousand, worst chapter 11.95, and no chapter is anywhere near 12.4.** Per chapter, raw / true / bold per thousand: 151 9.14 / 6.49 / 10.61 · 152 7.03 / 2.81 / 11.95 · 153 11.06 / 7.01 / 9.22 · 154 11.65 / 5.83 / 9.10 · 155 10.28 / 5.64 / 9.28 · 156 8.69 / 5.22 / 9.04 · 157 10.84 / 4.90 / 10.14 · 158 12.64 / 7.37 / 10.18 · 159 12.11 / 7.14 / 9.62 · 160 12.33 / 8.32 / 10.17. **The raw and the bold figures sit in the family Volume 03 finished in. The true-hedge figure does not: Volume 03's last batch was 6.80 and this is 6.12, and that is stated here rather than dressed up.** The rule and its word lists are printed in `workspace/volume-04/batch-0001/SUMMARY.md` and in that batch's `PROMPT.md`, and every figure here and there comes from the strict matchers, because a lowercase-literal *about* cannot see a capitalised *About* and the obvious bold regex cannot see a span containing a single-asterisk italic, and **the loose matcher only ever under-reports, and under-reporting is the direction that hides a ceiling.**

**Sweeps, all clean on the ten files as they stand:** every line carries an even number of `*` and an even number of `"`; **no `* * *` marker anywhere**; no month-name, month-date, day-date, year or day number anywhere; **no duplicated paragraph at an eighty-character threshold within the batch**; **no eighty-character overlap with any of the 150 earlier chapters, measured on a normalised whitespace basis, and six overlaps found on the first pass were all reworded** — two in Chapter 151, three in Chapter 156 and one in Chapter 160, and the last of those was the master in the inside pocket on a chair, which is canon and was reworded without changing the fact; and the word *pattern* appears nowhere, and so do the Crown Clause, Iona, Sorn, the Solo Seal, Nell, the Quiet House in the narrator's judgement, and *variant*.

# VOLUME 04 — MOVEMENT I REPAIR PASSES, COMPACTED. All sixteen canon changes stand and all three passes are recorded in full in `reviews/volume-04-batch-0001.md`

**Three review and repair passes have been run on Chapters 151–160. Every finding that was real has been applied, no chapter was restarted, and **no scene, beat, character, age, date, quantity, count, refusal, consent scope, load-book entry number, day-map entry, thread, character state or relationship state was altered by the second or third pass.** The day map never moved and the load-book run is still 154 to 163 continuous, one entry per chapter, eight of the ten about somebody other than Marek Senn, and the two about him being 160 and 163. This block replaces about 22 KB of running narrative; nothing was deleted from the repository and the full text of each pass is in the git history of this file.**

### The sixteen canon changes of the first pass, condensed. A writer working from the pre-repair text contradicts every one of them.

1. **The choice in Chapter 157 costs something.** He does not announce the tearing. He finishes the fourth line of the slip, the registrar reads it and asks him nothing and says it goes in the tray, and it goes in, and it is the only thing in the tray; then he takes it out and tears it in half and then in four. A request with a person on it existed for about a minute and was destroyed, and that is the cost, and it is why Doreen Abbiss is the reason.
2. **The motive is arithmetic and every figure in it is right.** Ines Kolar is a woman of forty-six, she offered the Thursday walk, it has been one Thursday. The woman of thirty-four at the register table is in Chapter 155 on the Tuesday afternoon of week forty-eight and refused to read six lines and read the four column headings instead. **Doreen Abbiss has no district and no corridor in common with the register: she is the one name because she is the only person in this city with a name in a box she cannot get out of, and she did not ask for the box, and he chose his own position, and she was asked one question on a telephone and did not answer it.**
3. **The interval is seven days and nobody meets her before the half landing.** The name went into the box on the Wednesday of week forty-seven; Chapter 156 is the Wednesday of week forty-eight. **She and Marek Senn meet for the first time at twenty past midnight on the half landing on the Wednesday night of week forty-eight**, and Chapter 157 is a day and a half later, and she says so when she names her cost. He stood outside the counter on the Wednesday morning of week forty-seven for about forty minutes and neither of them spoke.
4. **There are two pages and a third thing again.** The page the box is for is in a drawer upstairs, four fields filled in from the Tuesday of week thirty-eight, no box on it. **Doreen Abbiss has a second page of the same kind in a drawer at work, hers says a bay and that one says a room, and neither has a box on it.** The counter's stack of about two hundred is a third thing and nobody has collected those since the spring.
5. **The condition sheet's scope is fixed and identical in Chapters 152 and 160.** Four buildings, about nine hundred rooms on a four-page printout that comes down on a Friday, forty-one doors a week, one sheet a week, forty-one lines, forty of them a room number and a condition and a date, and the forty-first a space two inches high for conditions where there are no conditions, empty for nineteen years, about a thousand condition sheets in a ring file in a cupboard on a ground floor in another part of this city. The fourteen lines: nine in the building in Bower Street, three in the second building, two in a stairwell in Saltmarket that is not one of the four buildings. The fourth building came up with nothing on any of its doors, the first time in nineteen years. **She said in Chapter 152 that there was a thing and would not say what it was until the next Thursday, which is Chapter 157's day, and Chapter 160 is where she says it.**
6. **The man of fifty-five and the drawer on a hook told her about it about four years ago while he was doing something to a pipe.** She has been doing the job nineteen years and has known for four. **"Since before I came" now means one thing only: the room behind the plate numbered 2-14 has been 2-07 since before she came, and Chapter 152 is the chapter that says it.**
7. **The plates are a stock and not a rate.** About nine hundred doors in four buildings carry a company's name along the bottom edge; Petar Vance has printed about nine hundred of them; Ines Kolar has cleaned about four hundred in nineteen years. **The number on the door is a service number off a room list the company does not own, and the company's list and the building's plans have never met.**
8. **The reach figures are separate and stay separate.** Five documents including the levy note go to eleven thousand premises, printed without a hedge because Volume 03 Chapter 141 printed it without one; about four thousand leaflets go out with a levy note and land in nine hundred rooms; about forty people who wrote in since the spring were told nobody was dealing with it. **No sentence in this manuscript puts four thousand pieces of paper in eleven thousand premises.**
9. **The standing problem has been said four times and not resolved, and the count has moved on.** The third was said to nobody in particular, the fourth was the Friday briefing in Chapter 153, the fifth was the room with a microphone in Chapter 158 and Chapter 158 now says five on the page. **`outline/volume-04.md`'s *a fourth time* in Movement II and in the resolution is superseded: Movement II's is the sixth and the resolution's is the seventh.** Peter Amado's count of being told he does not have to say it is a different count and does not move.
10. **The fourth column has no person in it and the hand column has two lines in it.** Every one of about nine hundred lines in the authority column is a job title, none has ever been a person in fourteen years, and she has asked twice. Iven Sore's count is a different column: six lines relate to the binder, two of the about nine hundred have the hand column filled in, one of those two has an author still in this city, and he will not say which or why, and says out loud that he will not argue with a woman who has stood in front of that table for four years.
11. **The registrar's tenure is nine years in the room and thirty-one in the building,** and the other twenty-two were a room upstairs with a bell in it, and she did not like that one either.
12. **The *nine words* motif is no longer contradicted by its own printed text.** The pencil line in Chapter 151 is eleven words and says eleven; the registrar's rule in Chapter 154 is a rule given inside about eleven seconds and the entry's label says seconds; Doreen Abbiss's sentence on the half landing has no count on it; she makes no count on her own sentence in Chapter 157; and Ines Kolar's answer to the woman of thirty-six in Chapter 160 is true and not enough and claims no count.
13. **The Exchange's outlined beat is delivered and the book does not move.** In Chapter 156 Doreen Abbiss asked to be asked to sign, Bea Nunn declined to ask, she said that was correct and that she would not have said yes, gave the reason out loud in a room of nine people, refused the one follow-up question, and Bea Nunn said nobody in that room would ask her a second time, including her. **The fortieth line is still three years old, line forty-four still has its hole, the fifth chair is still empty, and the book is at forty-four lines. No caller signs in Movement II either, and the reason is a rule and not a mood.**
14. **The load-book overlap figure is corrected everywhere it was printed.** It was published as zero in three files and is **108, 93, 259, 279, 266, 230, 200, 170, 249 and 228** — chapter against its own load book, eight-word sliding window, strict matcher. Volume 03's family is 38 to 284; the batch sits inside it and the zero claim is retracted in `state/current.md`, in `workspace/volume-04/batch-0001/SUMMARY.md` and in `workspace/volume-04/batch-0002/PROMPT.md`.
15. **The eighty-character overlap sweep is a canon decision and not a sweep.** Thirty maximal shared runs of eighty characters or more survive against the 150 earlier chapters and this file previously said there were none: **they are canon the prompt requires word for word, same-speaker echoes that are on purpose, or the volume's inherited refrains.** Four passages of prose reuse that were none of those were removed — Chapter 153's opening three paragraphs, which restaged Chapter 142's at 165, 147 and 145 characters and drifted the clock from ten past nine to half past nine in the copying, Chapter 153's own reading of half past nine being the one that stands; Chapter 151's opening sentence against Chapter 149 at 147; Chapter 151's quotation of the clerk against Chapter 149 at 91; and Chapter 156's fifth chair against Chapter 147 at 101.
16. **Two planned beats are undelivered and are owed.** Talia Venn appears in none of Chapters 151 to 160. Milestone 1 and milestone 2 of `outline/volume-04.md` are both off the page, **and milestone 2's four-day gap no longer exists, because the Monday of week forty-nine is Chapter 159 and has passed: the gap is three weeks, and the next writer is to say the gap or leave it alone and not to fudge it.** Recorded, not fixed, because they are Volume 04's relationship beats and Movement II owns them.

### The second pass, in full, because it is short and every item is a fact a later writer would otherwise carry wrongly

**Eight of its twenty-eight sentences were errors against closed chapters. Chapter 151's two pages are now told apart by what is on them. Chapter 160 does not say the plan says 2-07, in the body or the load book, and what the door disagrees with is the building's own record in the rack. Chapter 156 does not put four hundred and thirty-two cards in one coat; they are in about nine places in one district. Chapter 156 does not assert a corridor conversation between Marek Senn and Doreen Abbiss that is on no page and that pre-empted Chapter 157 — she now says she read a leaflet about a practice that belongs to nobody, believes it, and worked out afterwards that the leaflet and the page were about the same thing, and a later writer may not put that corridor back without a scene for it. Chapter 158's Peter Amado is paid for the seventh time for this one and not in four weeks, and his two microphone asks are three events. Chapter 160's close says three weeks, not four and not two, and no later writer may put a different interval in that speech. Chapter 151's entry 154 says a fortnight and not two days. Chapter 154's wrong shelf number is found out about three weeks later. Chapter 157's body prose is third person throughout, and the reach figures, the five fields, the Thursday room and the tray all add up.**

### The third pass, which is this compaction and the Chapter 160 and bold work

**Finding 1, bold.** Bold emphasis was applied arbitrarily, at about thirty spans a chapter, and in **fifty-one places the elaboration after a speech tag was bolded while the lead-in was not, which inverts the house rule** that the lead-in keeps the emphasis and the elaboration loses it; and one clause of narration was bolded at all. The third pass enforces the rule across the ten chapters — **no line now carries bold on both sides of a speech tag** — and removes the narration clause, and leaves the load-book device, where a bold span marks quoted matter, untouched. The batch is now **211 bold spans at 6.69 per thousand, worst chapter 8.08**, against ceilings of 10.75 and 12.4, and the device itself is the house's and is not in question.

**Finding 2, the plate.** The second pass gave Chapter 160's *the plan says 2-07* back because nobody in the batch has been in that basement and the plan belongs to Movement III. The third pass found the fix had gone one step too far and made the volume's central discovery self-referential — a note disagreeing with a door. **Chapter 160 now says the plate said 2-14 and every leaflet in the rack in that room said 2-07, in the body and in the load book, which is the building's own authoritative record and is the same chain Chapter 152 puts it on, where the room number on a leaflet is the room number on the plan.**

**Finding 3, the intervals in Chapter 160.** The body said three weeks and the load book still said *four weeks ago*, and the entry still attributed to a corridor conversation on the Thursday of week forty-seven words that are on no page in Chapter 152 — and the words were a sleep claim that belongs to Doreen Abbiss in Chapter 157, not to Ines Kolar. **The load book now says three weeks and records what Chapter 152 actually says: that she told him on a landing there was a thing and would not say what it was until the Thursday after.** The ring file's contents are called condition sheets in both places so they are not confused with the nine hundred plates.

### Re-confirmed unchanged by all three passes, and re-confirmed in writing because every one of them is a hard stop

The fourth condition is intact, was said publicly a fourth time in Chapter 153 and a fifth time in effect in Chapter 158, and is not resolved. **The master is in an inside pocket on a chair in a repair shop in Lattice Ward and the decision about it is unmade and nobody made it for him, and the drawer under that bench holds five things, four of them other people's sentences.** The Exchange's book is at forty-four lines, was not opened on the Wednesday of week forty-eight, the fortieth line is three years old and line forty-four has a hole in it. The specification is four sections with a funded third line of nine words and there is no ninth line anywhere. The licensor is unnamed and the field is blank. **Iven Sore has declined to agree to anything, has not been defeated, has declined twice in writing to have the register moved into a locked room, and the register is still on a table by a counter in a building anybody can walk into.** Roy Amiss's telephone is still unringed and still owed and is not paid. Threadline is the assembler and the seller and is not defeated, and Petar Vance is not a villain and said one fact he can deny. The seven are still seven and the word *pattern* appears nowhere. **The relay was not staged and no page implies it can be, and Marta Selby was not asked again and was not told she was right in a way that cost her anything.** No System panel and no `* * *` marker, no month-name, month-date, day-date, year or day number anywhere in the ten chapters, the name *Quiet House* not in the narrator's judgement, the Crown Clause not named, Iona and Sorn nowhere, Series milestone 3 not spent, and Chapter 160 does not touch the relationship.

151 the Wednesday of week 47, 152 the Thursday of week 47, 153 the Friday of week 47, 154 the Monday of week 48, 155 the Tuesday of week 48, **156 the Wednesday of week 48 and the only Exchange in this movement**, 157 the Thursday of week 48, 158 the Friday of week 48, 159 the Monday of week 49, 160 the Thursday of week 50.
